### Wrist Ganglionotomy

Osterman first described the technique of arthroscopic treatment for the typical dorsal wrist ganglion arising close to the SL joint in 1995.It is better to describe the procedure as “ganglionotomy” rather than “ganglionectomy” since the ganglion cyst is not excised structurally. The principle is to abolish the “check valve” phenomenon by creating a capsular defect at the stalk portion of the ganglion cyst where the cyst communicates with the wrist joint and therefore allowing spontaneous drainage, shrinkage, and resolution of the ganglion cyst. In the original study, the 6R portal was used as the viewing portal. The stalk could be identified in only 61% of the cases. A full radius resector was introduced from the 3- 4 portal to resect a 1 cm defect from the dorsal capsule. The end point was to see a gush of mucinous fluid from the ganglion draining into the joint, followed by a complete disappearance of the ganglion externally. In Osterman’s series, complete resection was achieved in all of the 18 cases with no recurrence.

It is generally considered to be desirable to identify the stalk of the ganglion at the dorsal capsule- ligamentous junction of the SL joint. Arthroscopically it presents as a capsular bulging lesion with focal synovial hypertrophy. Ahsan and Yao used a color- aided technique of indigo carmine dye to visualize the stalks. However, the experience in identifying the stalks varies, and this might not be a significant factor contributing to success or recurrence. Because the stalk of dorsal wrist ganglion is not a constant finding arthroscopically, an alternative technique has been developed, including the use of an intrafocal portal through the cyst. Wu et al. retrospectively compared the result of transcystic portal and cyst- sparing portal technique and found no difference in recurrence rate, complication, and clinical results at mean

Synovectomy on the dorsal aspect can be visualized and performed through the lateral portals such as 1- 2 and 6R. Midcarpal arthroscopy is mandatory as synovitis is often more extensive than that in the radiocarpal joint. The arthroscope and shaver should reach the distalmost extent of the midcar pal joint to clear the synovitis at the carpometacarpal joint area of the hamate and capitate. Do not use RF probe in clearing dorsal synovitis to avoid thermal injury to the extensor tendons. When using RF probe, insertion of the probe through an arthroscopic sheath allows bubble release and venting of the heated saline. To help clear DRUJ synovitis, an assistant can manually compress the DRUJ in various positions of pronation and supination, forcing the inﬂamed synovium through the defect in the disk into the ulnocarpal joint so that it can be removed by the RF probe or shaver.

Careless portal making may endanger extensor tendons. Excessive use of RF energy can cause thermal injury to the ﬂexor and extensor tendons, leading to delayed rupture of the tendons within the ﬁrst 3 weeks postoperative.

No stitch is required for wound closure. Compressive bandaging for a few days to reduce saline swelling. Early ﬁnger mobilization to avoid tendon adhesion. Monitor for extensor tendon dysfunction for 3 weeks especially if RF probe has been used at surgery. Physiotherapy may be indicated if early stiffness is noted, usually at 2 to 4 weeks postoperative.

follow- up of 3 years. In general, the rate of recurrence varied from 0% to 29.7% in the literature. Systematic reviews and metaanalyses showed comparable recurrence and complication rate following open versus arthroscopic excision of wrist ganglions.

#### Dorsal Wrist Ganglion

**Indications.** Arthroscopic dorsal wrist ganglionotomy is best indicated in a symptomatic patient with a dorsal wrist ganglion arising from the SL joint, as illustrated by the typical location just distal to the Lister tubercle of the distal radius. The ganglion is preferably a sessile one or one with short stalk pivoting at the SL interval (Video 17.10). Tenosynovial cysts from the extensor tendons should be excluded. The nature of the cyst should be confirmed preoperatively either by aspiration to yield gelatinous content or by ultrasound scanning to show its cystic nature, as arthroscopic treatment of a solid soft tissue tumor is contraindicated. The latter can also help establish the capsular relationship of the ganglion with the wrist joint and the likely origin at the SL capsule- ligamentous junction. In general, ganglions larger than 1 cm diameter are preferable, as the surgical end point of treating an occult ganglion less than 1 cm may be illusive. Any wrist mass of uncertain nature should not be subjected to arthroscopic resection. The appearance of the surgical scar is often more favorable than open surgery for larger ganglions, especially in obese patients (Fig. 17.9). Other indications include a dorsal wrist ganglion associated with wrist pain or signs of SL instability since the cause of the pain can be evaluated with wrist arthroscopy and appropriate treatment performed simultaneously. Recurrent ganglion can also be treated with this technique though with increased difficulty. Dorsal ganglions arising from other atypical sites of the wrist are generally treated with open excision, though there has been report on treating wrist ganglia arising from the LT joint with a similar technique and principles(Fig. 17.10).

![[Fig.17.9.png]]
> **Fig. 17.9** A 4-cm dorsal wrist ganglion arising from the scapholunate ligament-capsular junction decompressed by arthroscopic means. Noted the finger extensor outside the dorsal capsular defect as created by ganglionotomy. There was no recurrence after 12 months and the scars at the 1-2 and 6R portals are inconspicuous.

![[Fig.17.10.png]]
> **Fig. 17.10** A 3-cm ganglion over the dorsoulnar aspect of the wrist arising from the lunotriquetral joint treated successfully by arthroscopic means.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC DORSAL WRIST GANGLIONOTOMY

The author prefers the operation to be performed under PSLA as it minimizes the anesthetic risk to the patient. Tourniquet is not essential as bleeding is rarely a problem. A 1.9 mm arthroscope is preferred to improve scar appearance. Two portals are made at 1- 2 and 6R. Lidocaine is also infiltrated around the stalk of the ganglion near 3- 4 portal to minimize pain during the resection process. The author believes that placing the surgical scar on the outer aspect of the wrist in contrast to the more central location of the usual 3- 4 portal can produce a more pleasant esthetic outcome. A clear arthroscopic view is maintained by establishing outflow at 6U portal using an 18- gauge needle. From the 6R portal, the arthroscope is advanced toward the SL joint interval dorsally, where there is always localized synovial hypertrophy. A 2.0 mm shaver is introduced from the 1- 2 portal to remove the synovial folding and to display the dorsal aspect of the SL ligament. The arthroscope is then directed distal and dorsally toward the junction between the SL ligament and the dorsal capsular reflection of the radiocarpal joint where the ganglion stalk may arise (Video 17.11). The stalk of a ganglion can be seen as bulging from the dorsal capsule though it is not always present. Nevertheless, pressing the ganglion externally can always lead to bulging motion at this interval of the capsule. Shaving of the stalk of the ganglion can be initiated with the shaver introduced from the 1- 2 portal. Gelatinous fluid can be seen draining externally or into the wrist joint. Extensor tendons are occasionally seen through the capsular resection.

In the author’s initial experience, arthroscopic ganglionotomy for dorsal wrist ganglion appeared to be a more time- consuming and tedious procedure compared with open excision, though it produced a more cosmetically pleasing scar, particularly for a ganglion bigger than 1.5 cm in size. In order to reduce the tedium and to improve the efficiency of the procedure, we have modified the technique by applying direct digital compression to the ganglion against the end of the shaver after the capsular reflection on the SL ligament was located arthroscopically (manual compression technique) (Fig. 17.11). Most ganglia will rupture after 1 to 3 minutes of shaving. After successful decompression of the ganglion, further resection of the capsular junction is performed under arthroscopic vision. To ensure resection of the potential one- way valve structure at the stalk, a sizable capsular resection of 5 to 10 mm in diameter should be made in the dorsal capsule. At this juncture of shaving, care is taken to avoid damaging important structures like the intracapsular dorsal wrist ligaments and extensor tendons. All wounds are then closed with Steri-Strips only. No suture is required. Bulky dressing and bandaging is applied for 1 to 2 days to reduce swelling and then light dressing is followed to allow full mobilization of the wrist and fingers. The patient is encouraged to perform self- massaging of the original ganglion site for 3 weeks to prevent pseudorecurrence formation due to regurgitation of joint fluid into the empty cyst remnant before the abolished valve is completely healed (Fig. 17.12).

![[Fig.17.11.png]]
> **Fig. 17.11** Manual compression technique: direct digital compression applied to the ganglion against the ending of the shaver after the capsular reflection on the scapholunate ligament was located arthroscopically.

![[Fig.17.12.png]]
> **Fig. 17.12** Pseudorecurrence is a common phenomenon after surgery for sizable ganglion. The patient needs to be taught for frequent and regular self-massage for 3 weeks.

#### Volar Wrist Ganglion

**Indications.** The best indication is sessile type of volar wrist ganglion arising from the radiocarpal joint and centered on the proximal wrist crease. Mobile ganglia with long pedicle are poor candidates for arthroscopic surgery. Preoperative ultrasound scanning helps to confirm the articular origin and nature of the cystic mass. Arthroscopic drainage of a ganglion from the STT joint is feasible with the addition of new portals as described later.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC VOLAR WRIST GANGLIONOTOMY

For arthroscopic treatment of the volar wrist ganglion arising from radiocarpal joint, we first described the technique and arthroscopic pathoanatomy in 2003. Technically it is much easier and more straightforward than performing a dorsal ganglionotomy. The author prefers to perform the operation under PSLA without the use of a tourniquet as this can help monitor any possible iatrogenic damage to the radial artery and its branches during the surgery.

A wrist arthrogram can be performed immediately prior to the arthroscopic intervention or as a separate investigative procedure. The use of an intraoperative arthrogram can facilitate the confirmation and identification of the stalk of a ganglion as arising from the radiocarpal joint. Absence of demonstrable contrast filling the stalk with communication from the radiocarpal joint may preclude the use of the technique. With the patient lying supine and with the arm supported by hand table, 3 to 5 cc of nonionic contrast solution is admixed with 2% lidocaine and injected into the radiocarpal joint at the 3- 4 portal site under fluoroscopic guidance. Typically, the stalk of the ganglion, and occasionally the cyst itself, can be identified at one of the volar radiocarpal ligament intervals (Fig. 17.13). Ganglia arising from the interval between the RSC and the LRL ligament will show up at the more lateral aspect of the distal radius, while those arising from the interval between the LRL and SRL ligaments will show up at a more central position of the distal radius. The relative position of the contrast- filling stalk can aid the identification of the true stalk during the actual arthroscopic procedure and hence facilitate subsequent decompression. As a rule, ganglia arising from the midcarpal joint or STT joint will not be revealed by arthrogram of the radiocarpal joint, unless one of the interosseous ligaments at the proximal carpal row is ruptured and creates a free communication between the radiocarpal and the midcarpal joint.

![[Fig.17.13.png]]
> **Fig. 17.13** Intraoperative wrist arthrogram to demonstrate the communication of the ganglion cyst with the wrist joint through the stalk.

Standard radiocarpal joint arthroscopy is performed. For a righthanded surgeon operating on the right wrist, the scope entry site is typically 1- 2, and working portal is 3- 4, thus the name “1234” technique that we coined. For the left wrist, the sites are reversed. The outflow portal is 6U. Routine diagnostic arthroscopic examination of the radiocarpal joint is performed, followed by localization of the ganglion. Synovial and

Dorsal wrist ganglion centered around SL joint of size 1.5 cm or above Dorsal wrist ganglion associated with wrist pain or evidence of SL ligament lesions Sessile type better than pedunculated type

Differentiate tenosynovial cyst from articular ganglion; an extensor tendon ganglion will move with the tendon. Diagnosis of ganglion must be conﬁrmed preoperatively by aspiration, ultra sound scanning, or MRI to rule out other noncystic tumors. Check mobility of the dorsal ganglion. The less mobile part originates at the SL interval.

Using 1- 2 and 6R portals alone are esthetically more pleasing for isolated ganglion without wrist pain. In cases of wrist pain accompanying ganglion, use standard radiocarpal and midcarpal portals to evaluate. An identiﬁable stalk may not be present. The key is to identify the junction between the dorsal SL ligament and the dorsal capsule of the wrist where shaving should be started. Use of manual compression technique helps to shorten the operative time. Never use RF probe to resect dorsal ganglion.

Keeping the shaver tangentially on the dorsal capsule minimizes the risk to extensor tendon. Keeping the shaver end away from the SL ligament avoids iatrogenic injury to the ligament. After the ganglion ruptures, keep shaving on the dorsal capsule under direct arthroscopic vision till a defect of 5 to 10 mm is achieved, with or without seeing the extensor tendon.

Overaggressive shaving may cause extensor tendon injury. Poor control of the shaver may injury the SL ligament and cause carpal insta bility. Pseudorecurrence of the ganglion may occur early in postoperative period and responds to intensive daily massage for up to 3 weeks.

No stitch is required for wound closure to optimize the scar appearance. Check ﬁnger extensor function. Compressive bandaging for 2 days to reduce saline swelling. Patient should be informed about the possibility of pseudorecurrence phe nomenon of up to 3 weeks postoperative and be instructed to do daily regular massage at the ganglionotomy site.

capsular abnormalities are identified at the interval between the palmar radiocarpal ligaments (Fig. 17.14). Alternatively the site of ganglion connection with the joint can also be identified by direct external manual compression of the ganglion, which will cause internal bulging of the ligamentous interval or even expression of gelatinous content from the ganglion into the joint (Video 17.12). A 2.0 mm arthroscopic shaver is then introduced to debride the region, while gentle external pressure is applied on the ganglion. The working portals may be swapped between 1- 2 and 3- 4 depending on the direction of access to the ganglion stalk. In contrast to dorsal wrist ganglion, a stalk can always be found. The radial artery should be palpated from time to time when the operation is performed without tourniquet. When sufficient enlargement of the capsule defect is achieved, a sudden gush of the gelatinous content into the radiocarpal joint can often be seen, and this is removed by suction until the ganglion completely disappears (Figs. 17.15 and 17.16). In the process when the capsular opening is enlarged further, care is taken not to advance the shaver too anteriorly, endangering the radial artery, which is often lying close to the cyst wall. The cyst wall is not resected. A capsular defect about 5 to 10 mm in size is usually resulted at the interligamentous sulcus after the procedure. The ganglion cyst will keep on bulging out under continuous fluid irrigation. However, with successful decompression, it will characteristically regress completely upon gentle external manual pressure once the irrigation fluid inflow is stopped. Steri-Strips are then used to close the portal sites. The ganglion site is packed with thick gauze bandages and the wrist is protected with crepe wrap to provide continuous external pressure to the decompressed ganglion site. Light dressing is then changed at day 2 with a waterproof dressing material. Patients are advised to have early mobilization and free use of the hand and wrist for daily activities soon after the arthroscopic procedure, although heavy manual exertion is to be avoided for 1 month. They are also instructed to perform frequent daily digital massage at the decompression site for up to 3 weeks to avoid pseudorecurrence. Dressing and Steri-Strips can usually be removed between days 10 to 12 after operation.

![[Fig.17.14.png]]
> **Fig. 17.14** Sign of stalk of volar ganglion from the radiocarpal joint. A, Normal interval between radioscaphocapitate ligament and long radiolunate ligament. B, Abnormal synovitis at the ligament interval in patient with volar wrist ganglion. LRL, Long radiolunate ligament; R, radius; RSC, radioscaphocapitate ligament; S, scaphoid.

![[Fig.17.15.png]]
> **Fig. 17.15** A 4-cm volar wrist ganglion arising from the radiocarpal joint of the left wrist treated with arthroscopic ganglionectomy.

![[Fig.17.16.png]]
> **Fig. 17.16** Gush of gelatinous fluid egresses from the joint after the ganglion is decompressed at the volar capsular stalk area. The surgical scars are minute and inconspicuous.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Volar Wrist Ganglionotomy

Indications
- Volar wrist ganglion centered around the proximal wrist crease indicating a stalk from the radiocarpal joint
- Volar wrist ganglion centered around the distal wrist crease indicating a gan glion from the STT joint
- Sessile type of ganglion more suitable than the pedunculated type

Preoperative Evaluation
- Diagnosis of ganglion must be conﬁrmed preoperatively by aspiration, ultra sound scanning, or MRI to rule out other noncystic tumor or tenosynovial FCR cyst.
- Palpate the radial artery to delineate the relationship with the ganglion.

Pearls
- Performing on- table arthrogram helps to conﬁrm the connection of the gan glion with the wrist joint.
- Abnormal synovitis at the interligamentous sulcus is a hallmark of a volar ganglion from the radiocarpal joint.
- Pressing the ganglion externally should demonstrate bulging of the origin of the ganglion as viewed intraarticularly.

Technical Points
- Just shave the ligament interval with abnormal synovitis and not the ligament itself to abolish the abnormal check- valve of the ganglion.
```

We reviewed the long- term result of 21 volar wrist ganglia arising from the radiocarpal joint, and 75% of ganglia arose from the interval between RSC and LRL ligament and 25% from the interval between LRL and SRL ligament. Two recurrences were noted at an average follow- up of 56 months. They were managed by repeated arthroscopic treatment (Fig. 17.17). No neurovascular complication was encountered. Fernandes et al. described their experience with 31 arthroscopic volar ganglionotomies. They used the 4- 5 portal for visualization and the 1- 2 portal for instrumentation. The authors noted 1 case of superficial radial neuritis, 2 hematomas, and 1 recurrence.

![[Fig.17.17.png]]
> **Fig. 17.17** A patient with a large recurrent volar ganglion treated with arthroscopic ganglionectomy. No recurrence was noted after 2 years of follow-up.

For those ganglia that arise from the STT joint, no technique on arthroscopic surgery has been published until recently. Chai and Ho reported 2 cases of arthroscopic decompression of STT joint ganglion under PSLA with no recurrence at 50 months of follow- up. Good indication includes ganglion associated with STT joint arthritis. Simultaneous synovectomy and joint debridement can be performed to alleviate the joint symptoms.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC STT JOINT GANGLIONOTOMY

The ganglion is typically located at the distal wrist crease level around the scaphoid tubercle. During the operation, the stalk can be evaluated with contrast arthrogram performed at the midcarpal joint. A MCR portal is made, and a STT- R portal is established as previously described. An optional third portal can be made in the palmar aspect of the STT joint, the STT- p portal. By advancing the arthroscope anteriorly negotiating the dorsoradial joint space between scaphoid, trapezoid, and trapezium, the volar scaphotrapezial ligament can be reached. Application of finger trap to the thumb helps to increase the working space. Synovitis is noted at the palmar capsule and the ligament fiber

Do not attempt to shave the cyst wall or otherwise the radial artery is at risk. Palpate the tip of the shaver through the volar surface of the wrist to avoid accidental damage to the ﬂexor tendons and radial artery. If the surgery is performed under PSLA without tourniquet, the radial pulse is checked intermittently to ensure its integrity.

Overaggressive shaving may cause injury to the volar radiocarpal ligaments. Attempted shaving of the cyst wall may cause radial artery damage and bleeding. Inadequate instruction to the patient on massaging the ganglionotomy site at postoperative period may lead to persistence of pseudorecurrence of the ganglion.

No stitch is required for wound closure to optimize the scar appearance. Compressive bandaging for 2 days to reduce saline swelling. Patient should be informed about the possibility of pseudorecurrence phe nomenon of up to 3 weeks postoperative and be instructed to do daily regular massage at the ganglionotomy site.

intervals. External mobilization of the ganglion provokes simultaneous movement of the palmar ligament and the capsule, and this maneuver helps to imply the ganglion origin. Shaving of the ligament interval using a 2 mm shaver while digital pressure is maintained against the ganglion externally is performed until there is complete drainage of gelatinous fluid into the STT joint. Further shaving of the stalk until a capsular defect of 5 to 10 mm is made, with an aim to abolish the pathologic valve phenomenon (Video 17.13). One should avoid passing the shaver too anteriorly to endanger the superficial branch of the radial artery. The postoperative care is similar to those of volar ganglion treatment. The decompression of a ganglion arising from the STT interval is generally more difficult compared with the standard volar ganglion. Small arthroscope of 1.9 mm and small shaver of 2 mm are mandatory.

### Removal of Loose Bodies

Loose bodies in the wrist are uncommon. Koh et al. reported loose bodies in 1.4% of the series of 707 wrist arthroscopy patients. Arthroscopy is considered as the best maneuver in removing an intraarticular chondral or osteochondral loose body that causes symptoms of locking or clicking. The causes can be due to osteoarthritis, articular fracture, cartilage nutrition disorders, and infection. Frequently the loose body is radiolucent but can be detected with the aid of CT scan or MRI. Mosquito graspers, straight and curved, are invaluable in retrieving loose body from within the joint (eFig. 17.31). A hypodermic needle can be used to maneuver or skewer the loose body. Particular attention should be paid to potential recesses of the joint including the prestyloid recess, the recess between the radial styloid and palmar radiocarpal ligament, ulnar facet of the triquetral- hamate joint, and the STT joint region. The author had an experience of removing an unsuspected loose osteochondral fragment from the DRUJ in a female patient presented with locking of the forearm rotation after trivial ulnar styloid fracture. Katolik described an arthroscopic removal of loose body from the pisotriquetral joint following a low- energy trauma in a collegiate rower.

![[eFig.17.31.png]]
> **eFig. 17.31** Removal of a cartilaginous loose body at the radiocarpal joint using a mosquito grasper.

### Capsular Release

Wrist stiffness is a debilitating condition often secondary to trauma or prolonged immobilization and may have intraarticular and extraarticular etiologies. Arthrofibrosis may be localized or diffuse. Arthroscopic arthrolysis is an effective treatment for restoring range of motion and reducing pain. Carpal instability is a potential contraindication for capsular release since release of the volar and/or dorsal extrinsic ligaments may exacerbate the condition. Presence of significant posttraumatic arthritis or osteoarthritis would limit any potential gain. Patients who cannot comply with postoperative dynamic–static progressive splinting and intensive rehabilitation program are not appropriate candidates.

Verhellen and Bain described the use of combined volar and dorsal portals in performing arthroscopic release in two cases of severe posttraumatic capsular fibrosis and stiff wrist. All volar capsular ligaments except the ulnocarpal ligament complex and the volar radioulnar ligament were divided completely with a shaver, RF probe, or arthroscopic knife, followed by gentle manipulation under anesthesia (Video 17.14). The relationship of the major neurovascular structures to the volar capsule had been studied. The average distance from the radiocarpal joint capsule to the median nerve was 6.9 mm, 6.7 mm to the ulnar nerve and 5.2 mm to the radial artery. Release can also be performed for the dorsal radiocarpal ligament. However, risk to the closely related extensor tendons is significant. RF probe is contraindicated because of the risk of thermal injury to the overlying tendons. Arthroscopic knives need to be used with extreme caution even under experienced hands. The main difficulty in arthroscopic arthrolysis lies on the initial entry of the arthroscope and instrument into an already contracted joint space. The trick is to perform progressive gentle dilatation of the joint space using trocar of different sizes before the final introduction of the arthroscope. Also, a small- sized arthroscope of 1.9 mm facilitates the procedure. The joint usually becomes more spacious and visible after initial debridement of the intraarticular fibrosis. Luchetti et al. reported the result on 28 patients with postdistal radius fracture arthrofibrosis at an average follow-up of 28 months. Radiocarpal, midcarpal, and DRUJ portals were used under dry arthroscopy to avoid fluid extravasation into the soft tissues. Wrist flexion/extension increased from an average of 84 degrees to 99 degrees and mean pronosupination increased from 144 degrees to 159 degrees. Hattori et al. reported an improvement of 20 degrees in the flexion–extension arc of 11 patients after arthroscopic capsular release for a variety of posttraumatic arthrofibrotic conditions.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC CAPSULAR RELEASE

The conventional radiocarpal portals are used for arthroscopic arthrolysis, and the volar radioulnar portals may be utilized as necessary. Although less common, if the midcarpal joint is involved, standard midcarpal portals may be used. Arthrolysis starts from the radial side of the radiocarpal joint. The 3- 4 portal is used for viewing and 1- 2 is a working portal. The portals are used interchangeably under triangulation. Once fibrosis is completely removed from the radial side of the wrist, the shaver is transferred to the 6R portal. It is noted that, frequently, a fibrotic band is identified between the SL ligament and the interfacet ridge of the distal radius. This fibrotic band can be partial or complete and is incised using a small dissector through the 6R portal. Depending on the range of motion obtained from arthroscopic debridement of intraarticular adhesions, the volar and/or dorsal capsule may need to be released. Volar capsulectomy is usually easier than dorsal because the structures are immediately in the field of vision when viewing from the standard dorsal arthroscopy portals. A mini- banana blade can be inserted through the 1- 2 portal. The RSC ligament is incised at its base and the procedure continues sequentially to the LRL and SRL ligaments until the ulnar aspect of the wrist is reached. Because of the preexisting stiffness and capsular contracture, the risk of ulnocarpal translation is minimal after the release. The ulnocarpal ligament complex and the volar radioulnar ligament should be preserved to avoid iatrogenic ulnocarpal and DRUJ instability.

To release the dorsal capsule, the arthroscope is placed through the 1- 2 portal and the banana blade through the 6R portal. The dorsal central portion of the capsule is resected first. The arthroscope is then transferred to the 6R portal and the remaining radial side of the dorsal capsule is released with a banana blade inserted through the 1- 2 portal. Extreme care needs to be exercised when using a banana knife in these areas. Good visualization of the structures is mandatory before release. Small open incisions can be used as needed to protect the extensor tendons. Bain et al. advocated the use of a nylon tape to be placed through the 3- 4 and 4- 5 portals and “railroading” it between the dorsal capsule and the extensor tendons. Resection of a portion of the dorsal rim of the distal radius with a bur alternating between the 6R and 1- 2 portals may be helpful when wrist extension is restricted secondary to a malunion of the distal radius. Following capsular release the wrist is taken out of traction and gently manipulated. The procedure is complete when a functional passive range of motion is achieved. Postoperatively the patient is placed in a bulky splint for 2 to 3 days to reduce hematoma formation, followed by aggressive wrist mobilization.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Capsular Release

Indications
- Stiff wrist due to arthroﬁbrosis and capsular contracture

Preoperative Evaluation
- Rule out intraarticular malunion or a mechanical block.
- Check nonarticular causes of stiffness such as tendon adhesion, scarring, carpal malalignment.
- Check tendon function.

Pearls
- A 1.9 mm scope is preferred.
- Use a needle to ﬁnd the potential space in negotiating with the scar tissues inside the joint under arthroscopic view before inserting a shaver.
- It is often difﬁcult to fully appreciate the joint architecture at the beginning of the operation due to diffuse scarring and synovitis. It is imperative to proceed to shaving with a small shaver of 2 mm even with a restricted view, as long as the intraarticular position of the arthroscope can be ascertained. The space and visibility should improve progressively.

Technical Points
- Resect ﬁbrous septum at the interfossa ridge area.
- Shaving the synovitis and ﬁbrous formation on the volar and dorsal capsule helps to improve the pliability of tissue and gain motion.
- Incise the volar radiocarpal ligaments with a banana blade when the lig aments are considered pathologic and too stiff. Preserve the ulnocarpal ligament and the volar radioulnar ligament in all cases.
- Protect the extensor tendons with a sling if attempting to incise the dorsal capsule.
- DRUJ capsule can be released through the perforation in the TFCC using a shaver.
- For midcarpal joint release, inserting the arthroscope at MCU portal is often easier than inserting through the MCR portal.
- Perform gentle manipulation under anesthesia to maximize the motion gain immediately after an adequate arthroscopic capsular release.

Pitfalls
- Overaggressive or inappropriate release of the ulnar-sided ligaments may cause ulnocarpal or DRUJ instability.
- Overaggressive dorsal release may lead to extensor tendon injury.
- Inadequate shaving and ligament release lead to suboptimal result.
- Cartilage may be damaged by violent action of the arthroscope and instru ments in negotiating through a tight space.
- Too vigorous manipulation of the wrist can lead to fracture.

Postoperative Care
- No stitch is required for wound closure.
- Compressive bandaging for 2 days to reduce saline swelling.
- Vigorous active and passive mobilization of the wrist supervised by thera pist with dynamic splinting should commence 2 to 3 days postsurgery.
- Turn- buckle splint is useful in maintaining the motion gained at operation.
```

### Osteotomy

Indications for arthroscopic bony resection include radial styloidectomy for rheumatoid and posttraumatic arthritis and the wafer procedure for ulnar impaction syndrome. Other useful clinical techniques include proximal row carpectomy for posttraumatic arthritis and advanced Kienböck disease, distal scaphoidectomy for symptomatic radioscaphoid joint arthritis in low demand patients, and total or subtotal scaphoidectomy as concomitant procedure for midcarpal arthrodesis in SLAC wrist, scaphoid nonunion advanced collapse (SNAC) wrist, as well as proximal hamate pole resection.

#### Arthroscopic Radial Styloidectomy

Radial styloidectomy is suitable for stage I SLAC and SNAC wrist conditions. Performing the procedure arthroscopically has the advantage of better visualization and can limit the potential injury of the RSC ligament, which is an important stabilizing structure in the wrist. Nakamura et al. recommended styloidectomy of no more than 3- 4 mm as increased radial translation with ulnar and palmar carpal displacement had been demonstrated after 6- mm and 10- mm radial styloidectomy.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC RADIAL STYLOIDECTOMY

The dorsal rim of the radial styloid is a common site of occurrence of early SNAC and SLAC wrist changes and should be assessed in all cases at arthroscopic surveillance. It is best observed with the arthroscope inserted from the 4- 5 or 6R portal. Frequently the associated synovitis in this area may obscure the visualization of the cartilage. This synovial growth needs to be eliminated by using a 2 mm shaver or RF probe inserted from 3- 4 portal. When the synovitis is cleared, the arthroscope is directed toward the dorsal aspect of the radial styloid to confirm the chondral loss and arthritic changes. A 2.9 mm bur can be introduced from the 1- 2 portal to bur on the arthritic articular surface of the styloid region. The origins of the RSC and LRL ligament on the distal radius mark the ulnar extent of the resection. Burring is continued at the radial border until a defect of even thickness of up to 4 mm is obtained. The depth of resection can be judged with aid of the sheath of the arthroscopic bur, or using a probe to measure. Volar aspect of the radial styloid can be spared as it is the important origin of the RSC ligament and impingement symptom seldom arises from this area (Video 17.15). Adequacy of radial styloidectomy can be confirmed by intraoperative fluoroscopy and by on- table manipulation of the wrist to ensure no impingement of the scaphoid with the distal radius (eFig. 17.32). Radiologically the extent of resection always looks less compared with open styloidectomy as the volar rim of the distal radius would be preserved when the procedure is performed arthroscopically.

![[eFig.17.32.png]]
> **eFig. 17.32** Stage I SNAC wrist treated with arthroscopic radial styloidectomy. A, Before surgery. B, After radial styloidectomy. Note the extent of bone resection is smaller than conventional open technique, as the volar rim of the styloid area can be spared to preserve the origin of the radioscaphocapitate ligament.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Radial Styloidectomy

Indications
- Stage 1 SLAC and SNAC wrist
- As isolated procedure or adjunct procedure together with scaphoid non union or SL reconstruction procedures

Preoperative Evaluation
- Radiographs/MRI
- Correlate with clinical signs and local tenderness

Pearls
- As an isolated procedure, it is very suitable to be performed under PSLA with no tourniquet. Usually bleeding is minimal.
- At 3- 4 portal, rotate the lens to 7 o’clock position to observe the radial styloid.
- Always remove the synovitis ﬁrst to uncover the site of chondral loss.
- Resect no more than 5 mm depth of radial styloid.
- Preserve the palmar rim of radial styloid so not to violate the RSC and LRL ligaments.
- Just resect the area with chondral lesions due to arthritic change, normal cartilage should be preserved.
- Radiologic depth of resection is often less compared with conventional open styloidectomy.

Technical Points
- Use the 4- 5 or 6R portal as viewing portal to get a full view of the radial styloid. Alternatively the volar radial portal can be employed.
- Use the 1- 2 portal to introduce shaver and bur.
- Start with one area ﬁrst adjacent to the normal cartilage of the rest of scaphoid fossa until the depth of resection is adequate, as measured by using the width of the arthroscopic sheath of a 2.9 mm bur, which is 3.9 mm as the reference.
- Stepwise extending the area of resection taking reference of the depth of initial site of resection.
- Test for impingement on movement upon completion of the procedure.

Pitfalls
- Overaggressive resection may cause injury to the RSC and LRL ligaments leading to instability.
- Injury to the sensory branch of the radial nerve at 1- 2 portal.
- Inadequate resection leads to lack of improvement.

Postoperative Care
- No stitch is required for wound closure.
- Apply compressive bandaging for 2 days to reduce saline swelling.
- Apply gentle active mobilization of the wrist commenced at 2 to 3 days postsurgery.
- Use a resting splint for 4 weeks.
```

#### Arthroscopic Wafer Procedure

The wafer procedure is an established treatment for ulnar impaction syndrome to unload the ulnocarpal joint. The prerequisite for an arthroscopic procedure is a completely perforated TFCC and is generally feasible only for degenerative TFCC tears of Palmer stage 2C or above. Procedure gone through the DDRUJ portal has been described but is technically demanding. Biomechanical study has shown that excision of 3 mm of subcondylar bone decreased the force transmitted across the ulnar head by 50%. Bernstein et al. retrospectively reviewed the result of combined arthroscopic TFCC debridement and arthroscopic wafer procedure in 11 patients versus arthroscopic TFCC debridement and ulnar shortening osteotomy in 16 patients with ulnocarpal abutment syndrome. Good to excellent result was obtained in 9 of 11 and 11 of 16 patients, respectively. They concluded that the combined arthroscopic TFCC debridement and arthroscopic wafer procedure provided similar pain relief and restoration of function compared with ulnar shortening but with fewer complications. In another level III comparative trial on 42 patients, Oh et al. also noted that arthroscopic wafer procedure and ulnar- shortening osteotomy for idiopathic ulnar impaction syndrome with subtle positive ulnar variance of less than 4 mm achieved similar clinical and radiologic outcomes at 2 years after surgery. However, compared with ulnar- shortening osteotomy, arthroscopic wafer procedure showed lower complication rates and better grip strength, Mayo wrist score (MWS) values, and DASH scores at 3 months after surgery.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC WAFER PROCEDURE

With the arthroscope in 3- 4 portal, burring of the ulnar head can be performed with a 2.9 mm arthroscopic bur introduced from 4- 5 portal, after an initial debridement of the articular disk to the peripheral stable rim and associated synovitis at the ulnocarpal joint as well as the DRUJ. Burring of the ulnar head should be done in an even manner at a horizontal plane, taking care of its 270- degree circumference. This can be achieved by gradually rotating the forearm from pronation to supination position while burring the ulnar head, with the aim to create ulnar minus variance of 2 mm (Video 17.16). Care should be taken not to injure the articular cartilage of the sigmoid notch or prolonged pain with forearm rotation and even iatrogenic DRUJ arthritis may result. Operative fluoroscopy may be necessary as it can be quite difficult to accurately assess the amount of ulnar head excised by arthroscopy alone (eFig. 17.33). Additional portals in the DRUJ can be employed to facilitate the resection process.

![[eFig.17.33.png]]
> **eFig. 17.33** Arthroscopic wafer procedure for ulnocarpal impaction syndrome. A, Before surgery. B, After wafer procedure. Note the radial two-thirds of the ulnar head was resected at 270 degrees of circumference to create 2 mm of ulnar minus variance. The foveal part is spared to preserve the insertion of the radioulnar ligaments.

#### Arthroscopic Proximal Row Carpectomy

Culp et al. first described the technique of arthroscopic proximal row carpectomy in 7 patients in 1997 and achieved satisfactory pain relief and functional gain. The result was echoed by Weiss et al. The procedure avoids disruption of the dorsal capsule allowing for immediate postoperative range of motion. Indications for arthroscopic proximal carpectomy are identical to the open procedure and is reserved for those patients with debilitating wrist pain and intact articular cartilage of the lunate fossa and capitate head.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC PROXIMAL ROW CARPECTOMY

To perform the procedure, the routine radiocarpal and midcarpal portals are established. The arthroscope is placed in the MCU portal and a small joint arthroscopic bur is introduced through the MCR portal. It is important to avoid injury to the capitate head by maintaining the hood of the shaver toward the head of the capitate at all times. Utilizing the small joint bur, the distal ulnar corner of the scaphoid is resected. Once adequate resection of the ulnar corner of the scaphoid has been performed, a large joint shaver and, subsequently, a 4 mm bur can be introduced into the MCR portal. The larger bur facilitates more rapid removal of bone. The scaphoid is then excised with the bur moving from ulnar- to- radial and proximal- to- distal. For complete resection of the most distal pole of the scaphoid, the bur can be placed through the STT portal. A small joint rongeur is helpful to remove small fragments that wind up in the joint capsule.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Wafer Procedure

Indications
- Ulnar impaction syndrome with degenerative tear of TFCC, ulnar positive vari ance without instability
- Preexisting chondral lesions over ulnar head

Preoperative Evaluation
- Posteroanterior (PA) radiograph with forearm in neutral rotation to assess ulnar variance
- MRI to assess bone edema of lunate and ulnar head
- Correlate with clinical signs, pain with ulnar deviation in supination, and local tenderness.
- In the case of associated instability of the DRUJ, ulnar shortening may be preferable as the procedure tightens the ulnocarpal capsule- ligamentous sling.

Pearls
- Procedure can be performed under PSLA with no tourniquet.
- Resect the ulnar head 1 to 2 mm below the level of the lunate fossa, as gauged by the size of the arthroscopic bur.
- Preserve the DRUJ by avoiding the articular surface with the sigmoid notch.
- Resect an arc of 270 degrees of the ulnocarpal surface of the ulnar head but preserving the foveal origin of the radioulnar ligaments.

Technical Points
- Thorough synovectomy of the ulnocarpal joint is essential for pain control.
- Perform drill chondroplasty for proximal lunate lesions.
- Using RF probe to create a wide opening with smooth and stable rim over the articular disk, preserving the dorsal and volar radioulnar ligaments.
- Starting with the forearm in neutral rotation, resect the ulnar head with a 2.9 mm bur from the 4- 5 or 6R portal until a depth of 2 mm below the lunate fossa is achieved.
```

Following excision of the scaphoid, the arthroscope is moved to the STT portal. With the 4 mm bur in the MCR portal, the lunate is excised in a radial- to- ulnar and distal- to- proximal direction. Great care should be taken to avoid injury to the capitate head as well as the lunate facet of the distal radius. Wrist traction and adequate fluid flow are essential to create a safe space above the distal radius and protect the articular cartilage. A rongeur can be used to remove the final fragments of bone that adhere to the capsule. The arthroscope is then moved to the MCR portal and the bur is placed in the MCU portal to remove the triquetrum. The distal scaphoid and pisiform can be left in situ. Fluoroscopic views are made to confirm complete excision of the proximal row of the carpus (eFigs. 17.34 to 17.36; Video 17.17). The operating time is typically longer than that of an open procedure. With the recent availability of larger and more powerful bur designed for small joints, the operative time can be expected to reduce. After the operation, the wrist is immobilized for 2 weeks before starting active mobilization.

![[eFig.17.34.png]]
> **eFig. 17.34** A 22-year-old female with stage IIIB Kienböck disease and failed vascularized bone graft received an arthroscopic proximal row carpectomy procedure.

![[eFig.17.35.png]]
> **eFig. 17.35** Arthroscopic and x-ray views showing the extent of carpal bones resection using bur and rongeur. Noted that the distal scaphoid and pisiform can be retained without risk of impingement. The distal scaphoid is not resected as it does not have contact with radius. The radial column of the hand can also be rendered more stable.

![[eFig.17.36.png]]
> **eFig. 17.36** Early postoperative condition of the wrist with minimal scars and swelling.

With the left hand holding the scope at the 3- 4 portal, the surgeon can use the ring and little ﬁnger of the left hand to grasp onto the volar aspect of the radial styloid. The surgeon then rotates the radius in pronation manner while maintaining the lens in a ﬁxed relationship with the radius. In this way under arthroscopic view, the ulnar head will appear to be supinated, thus exposing more the volar aspect of the ulnar head into view. The surgeon can continue to resect the volar part of the ulnar head to the same reference depth of the initial resection. Conversely when the surgeon rotates the radius in supination in the same manner, the ulnar head will appear to be pronated, thus exposing the more dorsal part of the ulnar head for resection. In this way, a 270- degree arc of the ulnar head can be resected evenly. Check adequacy of resection with ﬂuoroscopy during and after the procedure.

Underresection causes inadequate unloading of the joint and pain relief. Overresection may endanger DRUJ integrity and stability. Resection of the ulnar one-third of the ulnar head may damage the foveal insertion of the radioulnar ligaments.

No stitch is required for wound closure. Apply compressive bandaging for 2 days to reduce saline swelling. Apply gentle active mobilization of the wrist commenced at 2 to 3 days post surgery. Use resting splint for 4 weeks. Expect pain relief may take months to optimize.

However, a longer period of immobilization may be required in some patients in whom the undisturbed extrinsic ligaments may take time to shrink to the appropriate length and tension to produce a stable and mobile wrist.

#### Arthroscopic Distal Scaphoidectomy

Arthroscopic distal scaphoidectomy was first proposed by Ruch et al. in 1998. They described a technique of treating scaphoid nonunion with associated avascular necrosis consisting of arthroscopic resection of the distal pole of the scaphoid combined with radial styloidectomy in 3 patients with complete relief of mechanical pain and improvement of wrist and hand function. Arthroscopic distal scaphoidectomy is indicated for symptom control in selected cases of SNAC wrist, as well as in the treatment of STT joint osteoarthritis. In SNAC wrist, the initial arthritic change is due to the abnormal bone contact and stress concentration between the distal fragment of the nonunion and the radial styloid articulating surface. Distal scaphoid resection can be regarded as a “buying time” procedure in removing the primary source of mechanical symptoms in painful scaphoid nonunion. Its advantage lies on simplicity of the surgery without involving implant fixation or the more involved partial fusion or carpectomy surgeries. It enables early rehabilitation without the need for prolonged immobilization. It does not burn any bridge, even if the patient requires a subsequent scaphoidectomy and four- corner fusion procedure. It can be considered whenever there is doubt concerning the union potential of a chronic scaphoid nonunion, such as avascular necrosis, repeated failed surgery, advanced age, chronic tobacco use, and other significant medical comorbidities. A significant dorsal lunate tilt deformity (DISI) is considered a relative contraindication to distal scaphoid excision.

In a biomechanical study, Matsuki et al. reconstructed a normal wrist model from computed tomography images and performed theoretical analysis utilizing a three- dimensional rigid body spring model. Two types of scaphoid nonunion treated by distal fragment resection were simulated, the volar type and dorsal type, based on definitions by Nakamura et al. in 1991. In the volar type, which was associated with distal scaphoid nonunions, the distal fragment overhung palmar to the proximal fragment. Conversely, in the dorsal type, which is usually associated with proximal scaphoid fracture, the distal fragment shifted dorsally with respect to the proximal fragment. It was shown in the study that in the distal fragment resection simulation for volar type nonunion, force transmission ratio in the radiocarpal joint resembled that of the normal joint. The pressure concentration in the dorsoulnar part of the scaphoid fossa disappeared while there was minimal change at the midcarpal joint. However, in dorsal type nonunion, pressure concentration around the capitate head was aggravated with the simulated distal fragment resection, indicating a potential risk for worsening any preexisting lunocapitate arthritis.

The best indication for distal scaphoidectomy in SNAC wrist is when the cartilage degeneration, osteophyte formation, and deformity are confined mainly to the distal scaphoid articular surface on both radiocarpal and midcarpal joint surfaces, manifested by severe degenerative change at the radial styloid- scaphoid articulation. The lunocapitate cartilage should be intact and there should be no or minimal dorsal lunate tilt. As recommended by Malerich et al., marked arthritis at the lunocapitate joint constitutes a relative contraindication of the procedure, though Soejima et al. reported satisfactory outcome even in patients with stage III SNAC wrist. Ruch and Papadonikolakis considered an intact SL ligament and RSC ligament as prerequisite to minimize progressive midcarpal collapse and DISI.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC DISTAL SCAPHOIDECTOMY FOR SNAC WRIST

We performed the procedure for chronic scaphoid nonunion of average duration of 10.5 years in 4 patients, 2 of which under wide-awake PSLA technique with satisfactory outcome (eFigs. 17.37 and 17.38). The procedure is performed at the midcarpal joint with the aim to resect the entire distal nonunion fragment of the scaphoid. With the arthroscope introduced from the MCU portal, a 2.0 or 2.9 mm full radius shaver is inserted into the MCR portal and directed toward the nonunion site to debride the interposed fibrous tissue and expose the fractured end of the distal fragment. Then an arthroscopic bur of 2.9 mm is inserted into the nonunion site. The distal scaphoid fragment is burred at high speed from the fractured end toward the distal tubercle. Oscillating mode is not as effective compared with unidirectional mode. One should be cautious about the jumping phenomenon when using a bur to excise a particularly sclerotic bone surface. The bur may get caught in the area of hard subchondral bone during high- speed revolution. The resultant force will bounce the bur off the bone and may lead to accidental damage of the articular surface of the surrounding or opposing carpal bones. To have better control of the instrument, it is recommended to hold the arthroscopic bur near the distal end and use the finger to firmly anchor the bur over the skin around the portal site. Bone debris is removed by intermittently applied suction. To avoid accidental damage of the adjacent articular surfaces to be preserved, a shell of cartilage can be left intact until majority of the cancellous bone is removed. This shell of cartilage can help separate the bur from the adjacent carpal bone during the burring process (Fig. 17.18). This can be removed piecemeal at the end of the procedure by using a small pituitary rongeur or arthroscopic punch (Fig. 17.19). In order to speed up the process, an arthroscopic bur of a larger size such as 3.5 mm can be used when there is more space open up after part of the scaphoid is removed. Alternatively, a small osteotome can also be used to break the bone into piecemeal for easier removal. The end point of the process is when the articular surfaces of trapezoid and trapezium can be seen completely. If necessary, operative fluoroscopy can help confirm the adequacy of the resection process and ensure a complete relief of mechanical impingement. Arthroscopic radial styloidectomy can be added on if there is residual impingement noted. For distal third nonunion, the STT portal may also be utilized during the procedure. At the completion of surgery, the arthroscopic portals are sealed with Steri-Strips without suture. The wound is covered with waterproof light dressing, augmented by bulky bandaging of the wrist. The bulky dressing is removed on day 3 postoperative to allow early mobilization of the wrist. There is no need for plaster protection. Optional wrist splint is provided for nocturnal use.

#### Arthroscopic Distal Scaphoidectomy for STT Arthritis

Luchetti and Atzei described the technique of arthroscopic distal scaphoid resection for STT joint arthritis in 17 cases using a dry arthroscopy technique. Exclusion criteria was the presence of midcarpal arthritis and preoperative radiolunate angle of >35 degrees. They utilized a variety of portals including the standard midcarpal portals and STT- R and palmar portals using the Baré et al. and Tham and Dagge methods. The arthroscopic resection proceeded progressively from the radial- dorsal to the palmar- ulnar side. Synovitis was debrided with a 2.9 mm shaver and the arthritic part of the distal scaphoid was resected by 2.9 mm and 3.5 mm bur. They emphasized the importance of keeping the remaining part of the scaphoid head a round and smooth shape. The resection should not exceed the articular margin of more than 3 to 4 mm in order to preserve the scaphotrapezial and scaphocapitate ligament to prevent STT joint instability. After the operation the wrist was immobilized in a splint for 4 weeks. They noted that it normally took about 3 months for patients to return to activities. At an average follow- up of 24 months, all the patients were satisfied. Radiologic follow- up reviewed an increase in radiolunate angle in 10 cases and unchanged in 7 cases. None of these cases became symptomatic. Other studies also reported good outcomes.

#### Arthroscopic Resection of the Proximal Hamate

Arthroscopic resection of the proximal hamate has been proven to be an effective treatment for proximal hamate pole arthrosis, an easily missed diagnosis in ulnar- sided wrist pain. Proximal hamate arthrosis is generally associated with a type II lunate. Burgess first described the two anatomic variations of lunate morphology in the midcarpal joint. In type I lunate, the lunate has a single distal articulation surface with the proximal capitate and no articulation with the hamate. The lunocapitate joint has a smooth transition with the TH joint, and the helicoid surface of the hamate is shallow, blending smoothly with the lunocapitate joint. In a type II lunate, the lunate has a separate medial facet of variable size, which articulates with the hamate. Between the lunocapitate and TH joints, there is a ridged, transition facet on the proximal pole of the hamate distally and on the ulnar side of the lunate proximally. Viegas et al. studied 165 cadaveric wrists and found a type I lunate in 34.5% and a type II in 65.5% of the specimens. They noted cartilage erosion of the proximal pole of the hamate in 44.4% of the type II lunates, while there was none in type I lunate. In a clinical study by Harley et al., the majority of the patients had a type II lunate. Furthermore, all patients had associated LT ligament tears as well as other derangements in the ulnar side such as TFCC degenerative tear. They have proposed the term HALT (hamate arthrosis LT ligament tear) to describe this association. In evaluating the cause of hamate arthrosis in type II lunate, Nakamura et al. and Viegas et al. noted that type I lunate had no contact with the hamate whereas a type II lunate had contact with the hamate primarily when the wrist was in ulnar deviation.

Hamate arthrosis is more common in males and frequently related to wrist injuries and manual work, as well as sport activities with repetitive ulnar deviation loading forces, such as golf. Patients with proximal hamate arthrosis and failed conservative treatment can benefit from arthroscopic proximal hamate pole resection. The technique was first reported by Thurston and Stanley on 4 patients with arthrosis of the proximal pole of the hamate. In 2004, Harley et al. reported retrospectively the result in 23 patients operated between 1991 and 2001.At an average follow- up of 4.7 years, the outcomes were rated as 14 excellent, 4 good, 1 fair, and 2 poor. Patients with preexisting radiocarpal arthritis or who have had previous surgery had less optimal results. They had also performed biomechanical studies and showed no change in the force across the TH joint after excision of the proximal pole of the hamate. The optimal resection depth was 2.4 mm to completely unload the hamate articulation from the distal medial facet of the lunate.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC RESECTION OF THE PROXIMAL HAMATE

At arthroscopy all cartilage surfaces and ligamentous structures are assessed with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 and 4- 5 radiocarpal portals as well as the MCU and MCR portals. Careful attention is paid to the TFCC, the cartilage of the lunate and the distal ulna, if TFCC is perforated. The LT articulation is inspected to assess the LT ligament integrity and joint stability graded according to Geissler. The proximal pole of the hamate is evaluated with the arthroscope in the MCU portal. With the scope in the MCR portal, a full radius resector or a bur is placed in the MCU portal, and 2 to 4 mm of the proximal pole of the hamate is excised with the 2.9- mm bur (Video 17.18). Portals need to be switched for a complete resection. Arthroscopic visualization and fluoroscopy are used to confirm adequate bone resection (Fig. 17.20). Accompanying LT ligament lesion is treated accordingly. Arthroscopic debridement is performed for minor tear while ulnar shortening can be considered when the tear is associated with ulnar impaction syndrome. A light bandage is applied for 10 days followed by early mobilization.

![[Fig.17.20.png]]
> **Fig. 17.20** Pre-and postoperative PA radiographs showing the result of proximal hamate resection for proximal hamate arthrosis (arrow depicts the arthritic proximal hamate pole).

### TFCC Repair

#### Classification, Functional Anatomy, Clinical Features, and Indications for Repair

The TFCC is subjected to considerable axial loading and shear stresses and is frequently injured. Palmar classified TFCC tears into 2 classes. Type I TFCC tear denotes traumatic injury while type II lesions are degenerative in origin. Type I injuries are further classified into 4 types. Except for type 1A tear, which implies a central articular disk perforation with a stable margin, the other 3 types are considered as peripheral tears that may cause instability of the DRUJ or ulnocarpal joint, or both. Estrella and Ho described the dorsal type of tear, which is located at the junction of the dorsal radioulnar ligament and the joint capsule just radial to the ECU tendon subsheath. It constitutes another type of peripheral tear. Most of the peripheral lesions of the TFCC are traumatic in origin, though it may also happen in degenerative status, likely due to chronic repetitive loading of the wrist. Type 1B tear is among the most common peripheral tear of the TFCC leading to chronic ulnar wrist pain and impairment. It broadly includes all injuries of the TFCC at the ulnar attachment, with or without an accompanied ulnar styloid fracture. Nakamura in his detail histology and functional anatomy study further defined the ulnar attachment as a three- dimensional complex with three components: (1) the proximal radioulnar ligaments; (2) the distal hammock structure consisting of the centrally located fibrocartilage disk, meniscus homologue, and ulnocarpal ligaments supporting the ulnar carpus; and (3) the functional ulnar collateral ligament (UCL), which consists of the ECU tendon subsheath and the thickened ulnar capsule.

The distal hammock structure and the UCL are considered as the distal component of the TFCC (dc- TFCC), as opposite to the proximal component (pc- TFCC), represented by the radioulnar ligament. The dorsal and volar radioulnar ligaments span from the dorsal and VU corners of the distal radius, respectively, to a broad area of the fovea at the base of ulnar styloid. A more superficial component runs obliquely and distally to the ulnar styloid. The deep foveal components are considered to be the true radioulnar ligaments that stabilize the DRUJ.Frank DRUJ instability can happen when the proximal foveal component alone is injured, even when the distal component is intact. Based on this understanding, Atzei and Luchetti proposed a comprehensive classification and management guideline for TFCC tears, denoting the possibility of a distal, proximal, or complete tear of the ulnar insertion of the TFCC. Thiru et al. and Bednar et al. showed that the outer 10% to 40% of the articular disk was well perfused and suggested a healing potential of these injured areas upon repair.

Injuries to the TFCC typically occur with extension and pronation of the axial loaded wrist. The most common mechanism of injury is a fall on an outstretched hand. Peripheral tears of the articular disk are common athletic injuries that involve racket sports, golf, or other activities that require rapid twisting and loading to the ulnar side of the wrist. Peripheral ulnar- sided tears of the articular disk are also common work injuries. Patients often describe a mechanism of traction or torquing of the forearm that occurs when a motorized drill bit suddenly binds. Patients with peripheral tears of the TFCC complain of deep diffuse pain across the ulnar side of the wrist. Frequently, they complain of weakness when gripping as well as a clicking sensation with rotation of the forearm. There is pain with twisting motion of the wrist such as turning a doorknob or twisting the lid off a jar. Patients with a peripheral tear have local tenderness over the dorsal TFCC between the dorsoulnar head and triquetrum and/or over the ulnar snuffbox in supination at the foveal area. Pain can be accentuated by passive pronation and supination of the wrist. A dorsally prominent ulnar head in relation to the radius can be seen in foveal detachments. Ballottement of the DRUJ reveals increased translation of the joint associated with pain. TFCC injuries are managed initially using nonsurgical measures especially in acute injury, such as the immobilization of the wrist and forearm, activity modification, and analgesics, for the first 2 or 3 months. Moritomo and colleagues reported that 46% of patients with avulsion of the TFCC from the fovea were pain- free after conservative treatment.

Indications for surgical intervention include specific ulnar- sided wrist pain not relieved by conservative management for 3 months, especially in the presence of symptomatic instability of the DRUJ. Hermansdorfer and Kleinman in 1991 showed good results with open repair of the TFCC to the fovea with 8 of 11 patients returning to normal activities. Cooney et al. reported 26 good- excellent results in 33 wrists using open repairs. The advent of wrist arthroscopy has given surgeons the opportunity to duplicate, if not surpass, the results of open repairs with minimally invasive surgery. Most studies reported results on repair of Palmar 1B tear and less commonly on type 1C and 1D tear. Repair of a type 1D tear is considered controversial. Estrella et al. noted a new type of dorsal tear of TFCC amendable for repair.

Arthroscopy remains the gold standard in diagnosing and assessing the type and extent of the TFCC tear, though a recent study in magnetic resonance arthrography performed with wrist traction applied demonstrated high accuracy of TFCC tear detection of 98%. Most of the time, diagnosis can be obtained at radiocarpal joint arthroscopy. However, there is increasing advocacy of using the DRUJ arthroscopy to directly assess the status of the foveal fibers of the TFCC. It may be the only method to detect and ascertain the diagnosis of the pure proximal type of TFCC avulsion tear. DRUJ arthroscopy is inherently difficult because of the small size and limited space at the DRUJ. However, in the presence of foveal avulsion and DRUJ instability, space will open up because of the laxity of the TFCC and possibly DRUJ capsule, making the procedure more feasible and less risky. Nakamura et al. recommended performing DRUJ arthroscopy in every patient with acute or chronic TFCC tears with DRUJ instability (Fig. 17.21).

![[Fig.17.21.png]]
> **Fig. 17.21** Arthroscopic pictures of DRUJ arthroscopy in two cases showing the injury of foveal tear of TFCC. A, A complete avulsion of the origin of radioulnar ligament from the fovea. B, A chronic foveal tear of TFCC accompanied with scar interposition between TFC and fovea. RU, Radioulnar.

Yamamoto et al. demonstrated in 30% of all patients, DRUJ arthroscopies revealed pathologic findings that could not be identified with a radiocarpal arthroscopy. At DRUJ arthroscopy, it may be noted that there is absence of the radioulnar ligament, which is sometimes replaced by scar tissue, or partial tear of the dorsal or volar portion of the ligament (Video 17.19).

Atzei described the use of two conventional tests on the tension of TFCC to differentiate different lesions of the TFCC during radiocarpal joint arthroscopy. The trampoline test is performed with a probe to assess the tautness of TFCC. The test is positive when the TFCC is soft and compliant and suggests a peripheral TFCC tear (Video 17.20). The hook test is designed to detect foveal avulsion of the pc- TFCC. The sensitivity and specificity of the hook test for foveal detachment diagnosis had been shown to be high at 90% and 90%, respectively.Using a probe inserted through the 4- 5 or 6R portal, traction is being applied to the ulnarmost border of the TFCC. The test is positive when the TFCC can be pulled upward and radially toward the center of the radiocarpal joint. Specific traction on the palmar or dorsal TFCC can help detect which limb of the pc- TFCC is ruptured (Video 17.21).

According to Atzei, three different types of ligamentous damage are possible for 1B TFCC tears. For isolated tear of the distal component, the trampoline test is positive but the hook test is negative. For complete tear of both distal and proximal components, both trampoline and hook tests are positive. A tear of the dc- TFCC is visible during radiocarpal arthroscopy, and a pc- TFCC avulsion is demonstrated by DRUJ arthroscopy. Lastly, for isolated tear of the proximal component, both trampoline and hook tests are also positive but the ulnocarpal joint looks unremarkable. The diagnosis of foveal avulsion can only be confirmed by DRUJ arthroscopy. Arthroscopy can also help evaluate the healing potential of the TFCC tear. Adverse factors include chronic midsubstance ligamentous tears with necrotic and contracted fibrotic edges, and previously failed repair with elongated and frayed ligament.

#### Arthroscopic Repair of Class IB TFCC Capsular Tears

Numerous methods of arthroscopic repair of 1B TFCC capsular tears have been proposed and could be broadly classified into inside- out, outside- in approach and all inside approaches. Instruments employed ranged from simple hypodermic needles, an epidural Tuohy needle, or a meniscal needle to zone- specific repair kit. The prevalence of knot pain and potential injury to the dorsal cutaneous branch of the ulnar nerve in these methods prompted the emergence of all- inside and knotless repair techniques, employing internal fixation devicesor ultrasound- energized suture welding system.

Most published outcome studies represent level IV evidence. A level III study on a retrospective comparison between open and arthroscopic repair in 75 patients over 10 years by Anderson et al. demonstrated no statistical differences in clinical outcome, though there was tendency for increased postoperative superficial ulnar neuritis and reoperation in the open group. There was no study comparing different arthroscopic repair techniques at the time of this writing.

Saito et al. gave a detail account on the summary of various techniques used in the literature from 1991 to 2016. The most common technique being reported in the literature was outside- in, which was first described by Whipple in 1992. In this technique, the arthroscope is placed in the 3- 4 portal and a peripheral tear is debrided with a small shaver through the 6R portal to help delineate the site of the tear and to facilitate revascularization. The 6R portal is then elongated for approximately 1.5 cm and the retinaculum of the ECU tendon sheath is opened. Branches of the dorsoulnar sensory nerve are carefully identified and retracted. The tendon is retracted ulnarly to expose the floor of the sheath of the ECU tendon (ECU subsheath). An 18- gauge needle is then placed as vertically as possible to the ECU subsheath to perforate the articular disk as viewed arthroscopically (eFig. 17.39). A 2- 0 polydioxanone (PDS) suture is advanced through the needle into the radiocarpal space. A suture retriever is then passed through the ECU subsheath distal to the articular disk to retrieve the suture (eFig. 17.40). Usually three sutures are placed, as described in the preceding, to close the tear (eFig. 17.41). The wrist is taken out of traction and placed in slight supination and the sutures are tied. A sliding knot is very useful to tension the repair of the disk through such a small incision. It is vital to close the retinaculum of the ECU following the procedure. Corso et al. reported good- to- excellent results in 41 out of 45 patients with this technique in a multicenter study.

![[eFig.17.39.png]]
> **eFig. 17.39** Arthroscopic view showing an 18-gauge needle being inserted through the floor of the extensor carpi ulnaris tendon sheath and perpendicular to the articular disk. It is imperative that the needle be placed as vertically as possible so that, as the suture is being inserted and tensioned, it will not shred through the articular disk. The photo also shows the outside view of a monofilament suture being inserted through the needle.

![[eFig.17.40.png]]
> **eFig. 17.40** Arthroscopic view showing a suture grasper being inserted distal to the articular disk to grasp the monofilament suture as it is being inserted through the 18-gauge needle. Outside view demonstrating the suture grasper pulling the suture out of the joint to be tied on the floor of the sheath of the extensor carpi ulnaris tendon.

![[eFig.17.41.png]]
> **eFig. 17.41** Arthroscopic view following placement of three monofilament sutures across the peripheral tear after being tied. Note the tensioning of the articular disk.

The inside- out technique was first described by Ekman and Poehling in 1994, using a Tuohy epidural needle to repair peripheral ulnarsided tears to the articular disk. With this technique, a 20- gauge anesthetic Tuohy needle is placed into the radiocarpal space through either the 1- 2 or 3- 4 portal. The arthroscope is placed in the 4- 5 portal. The Tuohy needle is passed across the wrist through the torn edge of the articular disk and advanced out of the skin two times, thus forming a loop to grasp the torn edge of the TFCC to oppose to the ulnar capsule. This process is repeated two or three times to form a horizontal mattress suture (Fig. 17.22).

![[Fig.17.22.png]]
> **Fig. 17.22** Poehling’s epidural needle technique in repairing 1B TFCC tear.

All- inside techniques, in which the entire repair is performed arthroscopically, were developed in the 2000s as a solution to reduce the adverse effect of knotting outside the capsule, which may cause pain and nerve irritation. Yao and Lee reported their technique using the FasT- Fix system (Smith and Nephew Endoscopy, Andover, MA) with excellent results in 92% of their patients based on the quickDASH and Patient- Rated Wrist Evaluation questionnaires.

#### Arthroscopic Reattachment of Class 1B TFCC Foveal Tear

The earlier techniques of TFCC repair aim to reconnect the torn TFC to the peripheral capsule of the ulnar wrist. However, triangular fibrocartilage foveal disruptions could not be reattached anatomically with these techniques, and therefore residual instability of the DRUJ was likely. Failure to reattach the TFCC to the foveal footprint may induce synovitis, which in turn inhibits the healing of the foveal fibers, setting up a vicious cycle and promoting the persistence of painful DRUJ instability. Therefore, precise preoperative determination of foveal versus capsular injury and operative planning become essential to ensuring good outcomes of TFCC surgery. Techniques of foveal reattachment of the torn TFCC have emerged since 2000, and enthusiasm for foveal reinsertion of the distal radioulnar ligaments has flourished. Two common methods of arthroscopic reattachment of the TFCC to the fovea include transosseous sutures and suture anchors. Nakamura et al. noted a better clinical outcome in patients with symptoms for less than 7 months and in patients with neutral or negative ulnar variance. In a cadaveric study, arthroscopic transosseous suture technique demonstrated superior repair efficacy to the open repair technique in terms of biomechanical strength. Both techniques produced significant decrease in total translation of the ulna after reattachment of the TFCC to the fovea. Luchetti et al. performed a nonrandomized prospective study on 49 wrists with posttraumatic DRUJ instability to compare the objective and subjective functional outcomes after foveal reattachment of proximal or complete ulnar- sided TFCC lesions by an open technique in 24 patients and an arthroscopically assisted repair in 25 patients.Arthroscopy demonstrated a positive hook test in all cases. DRUJ stability was obtained in all but 5 patients at a mean follow- up of 6 months. Both groups had significant improvement of NRS pain scores, Mayo wrist score, DASH score, and Patient- Rated Wrist/Hand Evaluation (PRWHE) scores. There were no significant postoperative differences between the two groups in the outcome parameters except for the DASH score, which was significantly better in the arthroscopic group ( *P* < 0.001). The attrition bias of the study was, however, high (42%).

#### Transosseous Suturing Technique (Nakamura)

The technique is best indicated for a symptomatic complete or partial foveal disruption of TFCC of up to 6 months duration from the initial injury. It works best for patients with an ulnar neutral or minus variance. In wrists with positive ulnar variance, shear stress between the ulnar head and the suture site may rupture the sutures. The concept is based on an anatomic consideration, in which a line drawn on an anatomic diagram between the ulnar half of the TFC and a point of the ulnar cortex of the ulnar shaft 15 mm proximal to the ulnar styloid, which passes through the foveal insertion, the isometric point of forearm rotation. Radiocarpal and DRUJ arthroscopy is performed to confirm the foveal disruption. A 1- cm longitudinal incision is made over the midaxial line of the ulna, 15 mm proximal to the tip of ulnar styloid, and palmer to the ECU. The periosteum is incised and elevated for the future drill hole. After debriding the fovea at DRUJ arthroscopy using a shaver, a targeting device is inserted through the 4- 5 or 6R portal and positioned over the TFC (Fig. 17.23). The small spike of the target device is set on the ulnar half of the TFC (Fig. 17.24). Two separate small holes are made with 1.2 mm Kirschner wires through the distal ulna using the guide (outside in) and emerging at the fovea. DRUJ arthroscopy helps to verify the best position of the drill hole placement. A looped nylon 4- 0 suture is passed through a 21- gauge needle that is inserted through one tunnel from the outside. A second looped suture is placed through the second tunnel. Both sutures are retrieved together at the 4- 5 or 6R portal using a fine mosquito forceps. Then, nonabsorbable 3- 0 polyester sutures are threaded through the loop sutures and introduced into the radiocarpal joint. Proximal traction on the looped sutures pulls the polyester suture through the TFCC and out of the ulnar cortex of the ulna advancing the TFCC to the fovea. The suture can be tied over the ulnar cortex to secure the TFCC fixation (Fig. 17.25). The author immobilizes the wrist and forearm in neutral forearm rotation with a long arm cast for 2 weeks, followed by 3 weeks of short- arm casting. Active range- of- motion exercise is initiated after 5 weeks and passive range- of- motion exercise 7 weeks post-operation. The patient can resume sports activities at 6 months.

![[Fig.17.23.png]]
> **Fig. 17.23** Nakamura TFCC targeting device.

![[Fig.17.24.png]]
> **Fig. 17.24** The small spike of the target device is set on the ulnar half of the TFC. Two separate small holes are made with 1.2 mm Kirschner wires over the distal ulna through the guide and the guide pins perforate across the TFC. K wire, Kirschner wire.

![[Fig.17.25.png]]
