#### Arthroscopic- Assisted Suture Anchor Technique (Atzei)

Atzei described a technique of foveal reinsertion of the TFCC using a suture anchor or screw, with the aid of the DF portal (see page 763) to provide access to the fovea. A 2.5- cm longitudinal skin incision is made as a proximal extension of the 6U portal. The DSBUN are identified and protected. The extensor retinaculum is split along its fibers and the DRUJ capsule is incised longitudinally to reach the fovea of the ulnar head underneath the TFCC. With the scope in the distal DRUJ portal, a small shaver or curette is introduced through the DF portal to remove ligamentous remnants, prepare the bone, and drill and insert the suture screw or anchor. A screw or anchor with a pair of sutures (Small Joint Bio- Corkscrew Suture Anchor, ref. AR- 1923BNF; Arthrex Inc, Naples, FL) is inserted in the fovea, and one suture is passed through each limb of the radioulnar ligament to recreate a broader footprint of the pc- TFCC. Sutures are retrieved through the DF portal from beneath the TFCC. The forearm is placed back in neutral rotation so that the screw’s head is under the TFC’s s ulnarmost part. The suture ends are then inserted into the tip of a 25- gauge needle or a Tuohy needle. With the scope in the 3- 4 portal, the 25- gauge needle with the suture of the screw or anchor is inserted in outside- in fashion from the DF portal and through the volar radioulnar ligament into the ulnocarpal joint. A grasper is introduced through the 6U portal to retrieve the suture from the radiocarpal joint. The same procedure is repeated to suture the dorsal limb of the radioulnar ligament. Wrist traction is released while an assistant maintains the ulnar head in a reduced position and the forearm in neutral rotation. Using a small knot- pusher, the sutures are tied tightly at the prestyloid recess under arthroscopic vision. DRUJ stability is assessed as well as forearm rotation. The DRUJ capsule and retinaculum fiber opening are approximated with absorbable sutures and the skin is closed.

Geissler also described an all- arthroscopic knotless technique using a suture anchor system without using the DF portal. Instead, an accessory 6R portal 1.5 cm distal to the 6R portal was created to introduce the sutures, which were used to pass through the articular disk and tensioned with the aid of PushLock® (Arthrex, Naples, FL) to advance into the fovea area of the ulnar head.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC REPAIR FOR CAPSULAR AND FOVEAL 1B TFCC TEARS

Because there is no clear superiority of one method over the others, the author has been using inside- out arthroscopic technique employing epidural needle or outside- in technique with the zone- specific TFCC repair kit since 1997 for all types of symptomatic peripheral/capsular TFCC tears. Advantages included simplicity of administration, a more precise articular control of the repair site, the ability to apply multiple sutures, and the ability to customize one suture pattern to suit different types of tears. In general, vertical mattress sutures are preferred for capsular tears and horizontal mattress sutures for foveal tears.

For all TFC repairs, it is important to debride the rim of the tear down to healthy vascular tissue to promote healing and encourage fibrovascular ingrowth. The principle is to appose the torn peripheral rim of the TFCC toward the capsular tissue, or the disrupted ligaments to the fovea so as to secure healing. Performing arthroscopy without the use of a tourniquet permits accurate assessment of the vascularity of the torn site and ensures a better chance of healing. The epidural needle technique is particularly useful for managing 1B types of capsular TFCC tears.

A 19- gauge epidural needle is introduced from the 1- 2 or 3- 4 portal and the curved tip is guided through to reach the ulnar side of the articular disk approaching the tear site. The needle is then passed through the detached area of the TFCC at least 2 mm from the torn edge. A small counterincision is made volar to the ECU tendon at the ulnar snuffbox. It is prudent to dissect the soft tissue at the incision to look for the DSBUN and use a vessel loop to protect it before the needle is driven out from inside the joint (Fig. 17.26). A 2- 0 PDS suture is then threaded through the needle from the ulnar side to the radial side. The needle is then retracted back into the ulnocarpal joint. The needle, with threaded suture, is then moved distally for 5 to 8 mm to puncture through the ulnocarpal joint capsule adjacent to the first pass of the suture, thus once again being brought out through the ulnar incision. As the beveled end of the needle is not sharp, it protects the suture from being damaged during the second pass procedure. The curved end of the needle also allows the precise direction control by rotating the needle end. The looped end of the suture is held outside the skin while the needle is withdrawn from the radial side. Thus a vertical mattress loop is formed to grasp onto the torn edge of the TFCC to the capsule. Similar steps are repeated two to three times adjacent to the original suture site, each separated by 2 mm. The stitches are finally tightened and knotted deeply just outside the capsule using simple sliding knot with the aid of a fine hemostat forceps, while the surgeon observes through the arthroscope the tightening of TFCC and restoration of the trampoline effect of the TFCC. Extreme care has to be exercised not to injure the DSBUN or the extensor tendons (Video 17.22). Araujo reported satisfactory results in 16 out of 17 patients with a type 1B capsular tear at 4 to 13 months (average, 8 months) postoperatively with this technique.

![[Fig.17.26.png]]
> **Fig. 17.26** The dorsal cutaneous branch of the ulnar nerve needs to be dissected, isolated, and protected before the stitches for repairing the TFCC tear are being tightened and knotted.

For foveal disruptions with DRUJ instability, the epidural needle technique can be modified to anchor the torn part to the foveal region with the perifoveal repair technique. The ulnar part of the TFCC can be lifted off the fovea using a hook probe. A shaver or bur can be inserted from the 6R portal to debride the foveal region of the ulna head down to bleeding bone, without resorting to the DRUJ arthroscopy. The approach and the first suture pass through the TFCC are the same as described above. The second pass of the needle with the threaded suture is through the TFCC again 3 to 5 mm dorsal or palmar to the first pass of the suture, thus forming a horizontal mattress loop grasping onto the TFCC tear site. The direction of needle pass should be as proximal as possible so that when the suture is pulled, the TFCC will be approximated to the fovea. The procedure is repeated two times to secure the repair. Two additional sutures in vertical mattress form help appose the ulnar capsule to the torn TFCC to enhance the repair strength. This method enables multiple sutures for the foveal tear, an advantage over the transosseous technique where usually no more than one to two loops of stitch can be applied (Video 17.23).

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC REPAIR FOR DORSAL TFCC TEAR

Dorsal tear is a common injury pattern involving the connection of the TFCC to the dorsal capsule between the sigmoid notch and ECU subsheath region. Patients with dorsal TFCC tear present as chronic dorsoulnar wrist pain without DRUJ instability. Patients exhibit pain when the injured hand is passively supinated while passive supination of the forearm may not be painful. Surgical repair is indicated when pain persists despite conservative management including splinting for 6 weeks to 3 months. The author favors the use of the TFCC repair kit as designed by Whipple (Fig. 17.27). The kit consists of a curved cannulated suture passer and a loop suture retriever. After the dorsal nature of the tear is identified, a 2- cm transverse incision is made as a transverse extension of the 4- 5 portal. The extensor retinaculum is split and EDM tendon is retracted, exposing the dorsal capsule. The curved cannulated suture passer is then inserted through the capsule on the undersurface of the detached part of the TFCC under arthroscopic guidance. The loop suture retriever is then passed through the capsule above the TFCC and the loop is placed over the end of the passer. A 2- 0 PDS suture is then passed through the hook by spinning the wheel on the passer. The suture retriever is then pulled back out of the joint, bringing the end of the suture with it, and leaving a suture loop grasping the peripheral edge onto the dorsal capsule (Fig. 17.28). After passing two to three loops each separated by at least 2 mm, the sutures can be tied at the position of the forearm when the tear is best reduced (Video 17.24). Corso reported 93% satisfactory rate in 44 patients in a multicenter trial at an average of 37 months follow- up using this technique. From January 1997 to December 2002, 35 TFCC repair procedures were performed at our institution. Overt DRUJ instability was noted in 5 cases, and 18 of the 35 were dorsal peripheral tears. The average duration of symptoms was 8.8 months (range, 1 week to 37 months). Both methods described by Whipple and Phoeling were employed depending on the type of TFCC injury. At an average follow- up period of 39.4 months (range, 4 to 82), 26 patients (74.3%) achieved excellent to good result according to the modified Mayo wrist score. There was a significant reduction of exertion pain and improvement in wrist function score. No correlation of primary outcome was noted with respect to age, gender, hand dominance, work compensation, delay of surgery, and type of TFCC tear.

![[Fig.17.27.png]]
> **Fig. 17.27** TFCC repair kit as designed by Terry Whipple, as used in repairing a dorsal tear of TFCC.

![[Fig.17.28.png]]
> **Fig. 17.28** Diagrams and photos to illustrate that the torn TFCC is reattached to the dorsal capsule with 2- 0 PDS using the TFCC repair kit to form a suture loop across the tear site. (A) A curved cannulated suture passer is inserted through the capsule onto the under-surface of the detached part of the TFCC. (B) A loop suture retriever is passed through the capsule above the TFCC and the loop is placed over the end of the passer. (C) The suture retriever is pulled back out of the joint, bringing the end of the suture with it, and leaving a suture loop grasping the peripheral edge of the TFCC onto the dorsal capsule.

#### Arthroscopic Repair of Class IC TFCC Tears

Class IC tears are rare and involve the distal attachment of the articular disk to the lunate, triquetrum, and LT ligaments. Complete tears of the ulnocarpal ligaments can result in ulnar carpal instability and/or volar translocation of the ulnar carpus in relation to the radius. Patients complain of tenderness over the ulnar snuffbox and pain with grip. Trumble et al. recommended arthroscopic suture repair with good results. Other options include simple debridement or electrothermal shrinkage, which may restore tension back to the ulnocarpal ligaments. Tay and Berger described longitudinal split tears of the UT ligament as a cause of chronic ulnar- sided wrist pain in patients with a stable DRUJ. The UT ligament arises from the palmar radioulnar ligament and attaches to the palmar and ulnar triquetrum, forming an essential element of the TFCC. Berger considered the ulnar fovea sign as a key diagnostic clinical test. It is a provocative maneuver aimed at eliciting tenderness at the ulnar fovea. In patients with clinically stable DRUJ, a positive ulnar fovea sign was reported to be 90% sensitive and 88% specific in detecting a split tear of the UT ligament. The arthroscopic appearance of these lesions is predominantly noted superficially as synovitis. Debridement of this synovitis reveals the longitudinal split tear in the long axis of the UT ligament. These tears are variable in length and generally do not completely penetrate through the substance of the ligament. A longitudinal incision is made volar to the ECU tendon from the ulnar styloid to the hamate body, and the DSBUN is identified and retracted using a vessel loop. The UT ligament split tear is identified arthroscopically. Using an outside to inside technique, the UT ligament split tear is repaired with 2- 0 absorbable monofilament suture placed in a horizontal mattress technique. Postoperatively, patients are immobilized in a sugar tong splint for 2 weeks and then placed into a Muenster cast for an additional 4 weeks. A formalized hand therapy program is initiated 6 weeks after surgery. Alternatively, the inside- out technique can also be used by passing an epidural needle from the 4- 5 portal along the tear site and exiting the wrist through a volar incision developed between flexor tendons and ulnar neurovascular bundle (Fig. 17.29; Video 17.25). Clark and Kakar presented a series of 96 wrists that were treated with repair of UT ligament split tears between 2007 and 2016. Forty- six patients (48%) had a history of trauma. Mean follow- up was 21 months (range, 6 to 112). There were substantial improvements in pain and function, and 84% of patients achieved a good or excellent outcome using Mayo wrist scores.

![[Fig.17.29.png]]
> **Fig. 17.29** Palmer type 1C TFCC tear with separation of the ulnocarpal ligament from the volar radioulnar ligament. Two stitches are placed across the tear site using inside-out technique to close the tear.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Repair of Class 1B TFCC Tears

Indications
- Peripheral capsular or foveal tear of the TFC with or without DRUJ instability that has failed conservative treatment for 6 to 12 weeks

Preoperative Evaluation
- Positive provocative tests for peripheral TFCC tear
- DRUJ instability on ballottement test
- X- ray wrist to rule out ulnar styloid fracture
- MRI wrist

Pearls
- Procedure can be performed under PSLA without tourniquet to allow evalua tion of vascularity of TFCC.
- Normal prestyloid recess and pisotriquetral oriﬁce should not be confused with TFCC tear.
- Abnormal synovitis should be debrided to reveal tear site.
- The torn edge of TFCC should be debrided to a bleeding surface with shaver.
- Foveal tear site can be debrided with shaver introduced from 6R portal down to bone to induce bleeding. Alternatively the fovea can be debrided from DDRUJ portal.

Technical Points
- For inside- out technique, inserting 18- gauge epidural needle from 1- 2 portal can reach the more dorsal part of the tear.
- To reach the more volar part of the tear, 19- gauge epidural needle can be inserted from 3- 4 portal while the arthroscope is positioned at 4- 5 portal to observe.
- The needle should be inserted through the substance of TFCC at least 2 mm from the tear margin. Distance between two needle insertion points should be separated by 2 mm to avoid cut- through. The stitches should be evenly spaced out along the length of the tear.
- At the ulnar- volar incision for the needle to exit, the DSBUN needs to be clearly dissected through a sufﬁcient incision, and well protected with vessel loop before exiting with the needle.
- Absorbable monoﬁlament 2- 0 suture should be used to avoid suture breakage. Knotting should be as deep as possible at the ulnar capsule (for capsular repair) or ulnar periosteal tissue (for perifoveal repair) away from DSBUN. Four knots are optimal.
- Relax the traction during tying of suture and keep forearm in neutral rotation.

Pitfalls
- DSBUN may be injured by the needle or suture knotting if not well deﬁned and protected.
- Rough handling or retraction of the DSBUN causes prolonged numbness of ulnar hand.
- Secure knotting to the capsule or periosteal tissue can prevent loosening of the sutures.
- Superﬁcial suture knots may lead to a sensitive or painful scar.
- An inappropriate rehabilitation program may lead to forearm and wrist stiff ness and prolonged pain due to capsular adhesion.

Postoperative Care
- Reversed sugar tong cast (Muenster cast) with forearm in neutral rotation and wrist in neutral position for the ﬁrst 3 weeks. In contrast to a classic sugar tong splint, the splint fold is located distally at the ﬁrst web space of the hand, instead of the elbow. This enables the patient to perform free elbow motions despite forearm immobilization.
- Allowing midrange active forearm rotation and wrist motion out of splint over the next 3 weeks may prevent stiffness.
- Full range of active motion is allowed at the seventh week postoperative.
- Passive full ROM exercise starts from the ninth week postoperative, or earlier if excessive stiffness is noted.
```

#### Arthroscopic Repair of Class ID TFCC Tears

Class ID lesions of the TFCC involve a traumatic avulsion of the articular disk from its attachment to the sigmoid notch of the distal radius and with or without disruption of the volar and/or dorsal radioulnar ligament origins (eFig. 17.42). It may or may not be associated with a fracture of the distal radius involving the sigmoid notch. Patients may complain of diffuse tenderness along the ulnar side of the wrist. In patients who have a traumatic ligament avulsion associated with a distal radius fracture, these fragments can be reduced and repaired by open (see Chapter 14) or arthroscopic techniques. Disc avulsion from the sigmoid notch without destabilizing the radioulnar ligament usually responds well to conservative treatment or simple arthroscopic debridement. Lesions extending to the radioulnar ligaments causing DRUJ instability is uncommon and repair methods controversial. Overall there were three arthroscopic repair methods reported: (1) outside- in, (2) inside- out, and (3) all- inside.

![[eFig.17.42.png]]
> **eFig. 17.42** Arthroscopic view with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal demonstrating a Palmer type 1D tear of the articular disk. This tear involved the volar radioulnar ligament.

The most common technique used in the literature is an inside- out technique. This technique is performed by using transosseous sutures through the distal radius in ulnar to radial direction. The suture is inserted through a cannula into the central rim of the TFCC, followed onto bone tunnels in the distal radius, and then tied over the radius. The outside- in technique is also performed using transosseous sutures, but in the radial to ulnar direction. Recently, all- inside repair techniques have been reported without using transosseous sutures and no skin incision is made on the radial side to avoid injury to superficial branches of radial nerve.

**Inside- out technique for 1D tear repair (Sagerman).** Sagerman and Short suggested suture repair if the patient did not have an avulsion fracture. The surgery is indicated in patients with chronic ulnar wrist pain beyond 1 to 3 months of conservative treatment, together with clinical or radiographic sign of DRUJ instability. In this technique, a cannula is placed in the 6U portal and the arthroscope is placed in the 3- 4 portal (eFig. 17.43). A 1.6 mm Kirschner wire is put through the cannula in the 6U portal and drilled from the sigmoid notch across the radius to exit the midaxial radial border near the diaphyseal–metaphyseal flare. Care must be taken to avoid injury to the radial sensory nerve (RSN). The authors recommend making a counterincision at the exit point of the Kirschner wire to retract and protect the RSN. Three parallel drill holes are created. Long meniscus repair needles are then inserted at the 6U portal through the articular disk and into the drill holes (eFig. 17.44). A grasper is helpful to be inserted at the 6R portal to invert the disk to help with passage of the meniscus repair needle. Two stitches of a 2- 0 nonabsorbable suture are placed horizontally in mattress fashion. Sutures are retrieved and tied to bone through the radial incision.

![[eFig.17.43.png]]
> **eFig. 17.43** Arthroscopic burring of the sigmoid notch of the distal radius. Similar to a rotator cuff tear, the distal radius is lightly burred to subchondral bone to facilitate revascularization for the articular disk repair. From the 6R portal, it is observed that just a minimal amount of bone is removed with this technique. If too much bone is taken, then the articular disk will not be able to reattach to the bone.

![[eFig.17.44.png]]
> **eFig. 17.44** Arthroscopic view with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal showing placement of a braided horizontal mattress suture through the articular disk for repair to the radius. Long meniscus needles have been placed through the articular disk and through transradial drill holes. When the suture is tied, restoration of the tension of the volar radioulnar ligament is noted.

**All- inside technique for 1D tear repair (Corella).** Corella et al. described a technique for all- arthroscopic knotless repair of radial- sided TFCC tears (eFig. 17.45). This technique requires one visualization portal (3- 4) and two working portals (4- 5 and 6R). The 70- degree TFC suture lasso (Arthrex, Naples, FL) is inserted through the 6R portal (eFig. 17.46). The tip is situated just above the articular disk, and it penetrates from distally to proximally, coming out beneath the tear. The suture lasso is then loaded with a 2.0- fiber wire suture (Arthrex, Naples, FL), with a more rigid end to facilitate insertion in the passer. It is recovered with a minisuture hook (Arthrex, Naples, FL) or with a grasper through the 4- 5 portal (eFig. 17.47). The suture is retrieved through the 4- 5 portal.

![[eFig.17.45.png]]
> **eFig. 17.45** Arthroscopic view demonstrating a Palmer type 1D tear with instability of the distal radioulnar joint.

![[eFig.17.46.png]]
> **eFig. 17.46** Arthroscopic view with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal showing passage of the suture lasso through the 6R portal.

![[eFig.17.47.png]]
> **eFig. 17.47** Arthroscopic view with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal showing grasping of the suture wire with a crochet hook in the 4- 5 portal.

Following this, the suture lasso is withdrawn and is reinserted a few millimeters from the first point in a distal- to- proximal direction. The loop of the suture from the suture lasso is then retrieved through the 4- 5 portal, leaving a vertical mattress- type suture (eFig. 17.48). The suture lasso is withdrawn and a slotted cannula with an operator (TFC instrument kit, Arthrex, Naples, FL) is inserted through the 6R portal. The operator is withdrawn from the cannula and a minisuture hook is used to transfer the two sutures from the 4- 5 portal to the 6R portal (eFig. 17.49). The sutures are passed outside the cannula through the slot so that drilling will not affect the sutures.

![[eFig.17.48.png]]
> **eFig. 17.48** Arthroscopic view following placement of a 2.0-fiber wire (Arthrex, Naples, FL) stitch passed through the articular disk.

![[eFig.17.49.png]]
> **eFig. 17.49** Arthroscopic view with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal showing drilling of the distal radius with the cannula inserted through the 6R portal.

Through the obturator in the cannula, a guidewire can be placed to help confirm the ideal position of the eventual anchor. A cannulated drill is then used over the guidewire to drill the hole for the suture anchor. The 2.0- fiber wire suture is then inserted into a 2.5- mm PushLock anchor (Arthrex, Naples, FL), and the anchor is then inserted through the drill hole (eFig. 17.50). Tension on the sutures can be performed by pulling the suture and holding the PushLock anchor in place. The anchor should be advanced until flush with the bone, locking the anchor and suture into the predrilled hole. The suture is cut and tension on repair of the articular disk is verified with a probe (eFig. 17.51).

![[eFig.17.50.png]]
> **eFig. 17.50** Arthroscopic view showing the mini- PushLock anchor (Arthrex, Naples, FL) being inserted through the 6R portal repairing the articular disk back to the distal radius.

![[eFig.17.51.png]]
> **eFig. 17.51** Arthroscopic view with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal demonstrating repair of the Palmer type 1D tear back to the distal radius with an all-arthroscopic knotless technique.

### Arthroscopic- Assisted Reduction and Internal Fixation

Wrist arthroscopy is a valuable adjunct in assessing joint configuration and assisting reduction of articular fractures around the wrist. Indications include articular distal radius fractures, scaphoid fractures,and in selected cases, perilunate fracture dislocation. In most conditions, fluoroscopy is still mandatory for adequate reduction and fixation. Arthroscopy can also be used for treatment of associated soft tissue injuries at the same setting, which ranges from 40% to 75% in distal radius fractures and 34% to 83% in scaphoid fractures.

#### Distal Radius Fractures

In distal radius fractures, the best indication for arthroscopic intervention is an articular fracture with comminution and displacement. Articular stepping and gapping are the important factors that can affect the functional outcome in distal radius fractures. The goal of articular reduction is within 2 mm of step or gap. However depressed articular fragments are notoriously difficult to be accessed and assessed by percutaneous mean. Articular stepping and gapping are often underestimated when assessed under fluoroscopy. Several authors have demonstrated arthroscopy to be superior to operative fluoroscopy in assessing joint surface reduction, and it simultaneously enables outstanding evaluation of the concomitant soft tissue components of the injury. With arthroscopic visualization, displaced fragments can be identified, elevated, and reduced under direct vision using a probe or a fine bone tamp. Both dorsal and volar portals can be employed. Depressed fragments can be elevated with an osteotome or a bone spike introduced from the metaphysis while the articular reduction is observed and controlled arthroscopically. The reduced fracture is then fixed with subchondral screws or pins. Dry arthroscopy has gained popularity for fracture reduction in recent years for ease of fracture fragment manipulation and less risk of fluid extravasation or compartment syndrome. Both Ruch et al. and Doi et al. documented improved outcomes of articular distal radius fractures managed with arthroscopically assisted reduction and fixation of articular distal radius fractures compared with conventional reduction and fixation with fluoroscopy. A review of 28 studies related to the use of wrist arthroscopy in distal radius fracture supported the findings of better quality of articular reduction with wrist arthroscopy compared with fluoroscopy.

Arthroscopic- assisted fixation can be used for simple articular fractures that have large, well- defined fragments, including the radial styloid, volar and dorsal die punch, volar and dorsal marginal fractures, and as an adjunct in the management of three- and four- part articular fractures with metaphyseal comminution. It is well known that a fracture of the distal radius is a true wrist injury rather than a fracture alone and is associated with additional soft tissue injuries. Both articular and nonarticular fractures of the distal radius have a high prevalence of associated soft tissue injuries of the TFCC as well as SL ligament and LT ligament. These associated soft tissue injuries are easily seen arthroscopically and can be managed at the same setting.

**Operative technique (Geissler).** Arthroscopic- assisted reduction and fixation of displaced unstable articular fractures of the distal radius should generally have surgery within 2 to 10 days from injury. A delay of at least 48 hours helps to minimize bleeding from the fracture during the arthroscopy. For fractures that are 10 days or older, it may sometimes be difficult to manipulate the fragments due to early healing. The operating room is set up so that both fluoroscopy and arthroscopy can be used simultaneously. The wrist is suspended in a traction tower. The standard 3- 4, 6R, and 6U portals are made. It is helpful to lavage the joint of fracture debris and hematoma prior to introduction of the arthroscope. This is done by placing the inflow through the 6U portal and the arthroscopic cannula in the 3- 4 portal. A shaver is brought in through the 6R portal to remove any further fracture debris. A traditional diagnostic wrist arthroscopy is then performed.

Fractures of the radial styloid are ideal for arthroscopic management (eFig. 17.52). Rotation is best judged by looking across the wrist from an ulnar portal. Under fluoroscopy, one or two guidewires are placed using oscillation mode into the tip of the radial styloid but not across the fracture site (eFig. 17.53). Using the guidewires in the radial styloid fragment and a trocar placed in the 3- 4 portal (eFig. 17.54), the radial styloid fragment can be manipulated and anatomically reduced back to the radius (eFig. 17.55). The guidewire is then advanced across the fracture site for provisional stabilization. Once the fracture is judged to be anatomically reduced by arthroscopic and fluoroscopic confirmation, additional wires are placed across the fracture site. One or two headless cannulated screws are then placed into the radial styloid fragment (eFig. 17.56). By utilizing headless screws, the implant is contained within the bone, which decreases soft tissue irritation and enables early mobilization.

![[eFig.17.52.png]]
> **eFig. 17.52** Fluoroscopic view of a displaced fracture of the radial styloid.

![[eFig.17.53.png]]
> **eFig. 17.53** Fluoroscopic view of a guidewire placed into the radial styloid fragment. This can be used as a joystick to help reduce the fracture; it is not placed across the fracture site.

![[eFig.17.54.png]]
> **eFig. 17.54** Arthroscopic view showing the guidewire in the radial styloid fragment. It is best to place the arthroscope in the 6R portal to look across the wrist to judge rotation of the radial styloid fragment.

![[eFig.17.55.png]]
> **eFig. 17.55** Arthroscopic view showing reduction of the radial styloid fragment with a combination of the guidewire joystick and a trocar inserted through the 3- 4 portal to help control rotation and position of the fracture fragment.

![[eFig.17.56.png]]
> **eFig. 17.56** Radiographic view showing anatomic restoration of the joint surface with stabilization of the radial styloid fracture with two headless cannulated screws.

In three- part articular fractures without metaphyseal comminution, the radial styloid fragment may be closely reduced and provisionally pinned under fluoroscopy. This will serve as a landmark to elevate the lunate facet (eFig. 17.57). The wrist is then suspended in a traction tower and the articular surface is evaluated. A headless cannulated screw is placed in the radial styloid fragment as previously described. The lunate facet fragment is identified, and a needle is placed over the fragment percutaneously. This is used as a fluoroscopic landmark. A Steinmann pin is placed approximately 2 cm proximal to the needle to percutaneously elevate the die punch fragment (eFig. 17.58). The previously reduced radial styloid fragment is used as an arthroscopic landmark to ensure the lunate facet fragment is correctly reduced.

![[eFig.17.57.png]]
> **eFig. 17.57** In three-part fractures, the radial styloid is provisionally pinned and reduced. This can then be used as a landmark to help reduce the impacted lunate facet fragment.

![[eFig.17.58.png]]
> **eFig. 17.58** Outside view demonstrating elevation of the impacted lunate facet with a large Steinmann pin as viewed with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal.

Once the fragment is elevated and judged to be anatomic, a guidewire is placed subchondrally transversely beneath the bone surface to stabilize the fragment, which is usually best seen with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal (eFigs. 17.59 and 17.60). It is important to pronate and supinate the wrist to ensure the transverse guide pin does not violate the DRUJ. An additional headless cannulated screw is then placed transversely over the guidewire to support the lunate facet fragment (eFig. 17.61).

![[eFig.17.59.png]]
> **eFig. 17.59** Arthroscopic view of a displaced three-part fracture to the distal radius.

![[eFig.17.60.png]]
> **eFig. 17.60** Arthroscopic view following reduction of the impacted lunate facet fragment with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal.

![[eFig.17.61.png]]
> **eFig. 17.61** Fluoroscopic view showing anatomic restoration of the joint surface with two headless cannulated screws. One screw is placed perpendicular to the radial styloid fragment, and the second is placed transversely beneath the subchondral bone. Following placement of the screw, rotation of the forearm is performed to evaluate for any potential crepitus from a prominent screw.

Usually the guidewire is aimed slightly dorsally because the fragment is generally a dorsal fragment. Following placement of the headless cannulated screw, the wrist is again pronated and supinated to make sure there is no violation of any hardware into the DRUJ. Arthroscopic management of transradial perilunate fractures may be performed. In this example, all fractures were treated percutaneously or arthroscopically, thereby enabling early range of motion.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC- ASSISTED REDUCTION FIXATION OF DISTAL RADIUS FRACTURES

In most of the comminuted distal radius fractures, plating is required and arthroscopy serves as an adjunct for the reduction. Fracture reduction sequence usually starts from the volar part of intermediate column, where the cortex is the thickest and easier to be reduced, followed by the volar part of the radial column. The volar distal radius locking plate is temporarily fixed onto the radius under fluoroscopic guidance using a single cortical screw into the elliptical hole over the plate. The volar distal radius plate now serves as a buttress for the volar fragments, which can be temporarily anchored onto the plate with Kirschner wires, and the wrist is ready for arthroscopic assessment (Fig. 17.30).

![[Fig.17.30.png]]
> **Fig. 17.30** A, Intraoperative photo showing the distal radius fracture was temporarily reduced and volar distal radius locking plate was temporarily fixed onto the radius. B, The distal radius volar fragment was temporarily fixed onto the distal radius volar locking plate with 1.2 mm Kirschner wire as shown in intraoperative fluoroscopic film.

The injured arm is placed on the metal base plate of a wrist traction tower; 10 to 12 lbs of traction force is sufficient for wrist joint distraction. The 3- 4 and 6R portals are used for radiocarpal joint assessment while midcarpal joint surveillance is carried out through the MCU and MCR portals. Wet arthroscopy is performed with gravity driven saline irrigation. A mechanical pump is not recommended because of the risk of fluid extravasation and compartment syndrome. A 1.9 mm arthroscope is routinely used to improve the maneuverability inside the joint. Initially the 3- 4 portal is established to aid in creating 6R portal, which is subsequently used as the main viewing portal. The advantage of using 6R portal as the viewing portal is to prevent the depression of the dorsal rim fragment by the arthroscope. The visual field inside the wrist joint is usually obscured by blood- stained joint fluid and hematoma. The visibility can be improved by using a 10 mL syringe filled up with saline to irrigate the wrist joint. Afterward, hematoma and small loose fragments that prevent articular reduction are removed with a probe and shaver (Fig. 17.31). Temporary Kirschner wires that help stabilize the dorsal articular fragment are withdrawn slightly to free the dorsal articular fragment for further reduction and fine adjustment. Articular fragments are reduced either by joysticks or by a compression maneuver according to the articular fragment positioning and configuration (Fig. 17.32). The fracture gap between volar and dorsal articular fragments can be reduced and compressed with a soft tissue bone reduction clamp (eFig. 17.62). The reduced fragment is then temporarily held in position with Kirschner wires either from dorsal or volar direction. Final fracture fixation is completed by insertion of the volar plate locking screws to lie adjacent to the subchondral surface, followed by the remaining holes in the metadiaphyseal region.

![[Fig.17.31.png]]
> **Fig. 17.31** Radiocarpal joint was assessed from 6R portal showing the hematoma interposed between the fracture fragment that was removed by 2.0 mm arthroscopic shaver.

![[Fig.17.32.png]]
> **Fig. 17.32** Arthroscopic view as seen from 6R portal showing the difference in joint congruity before and after reduction of displaced articular fragment.

![[eFig.17.62.png]]
> **eFig. 17.62** The fracture gap between volar and dorsal articular fragment can be reduced with the soft tissue bone reduction clamp (Synthes GmbH, Oberdorf, Switzerland) by pushing dorsal fragment onto the reduced volar fragment.

This technique is particularly helpful in four- part fractures, where the VU fragments are first reduced with the radial styloid to the radial shaft and provisionally stabilized by the plate. The wrist is then suspended in the traction tower and the dorsoulnar fracture fragments can be percutaneously elevated and reduced to the volar fragment. Once anatomic reduction has been achieved, the distal locking screws are placed (Video 17.26).

After the distal radius fracture has been rigidly fixed, arthroscopic surveillance of the radiocarpal and midcarpal joint is done to look for any accompanied intraarticular soft tissue injuries. Any associated ligament injury is identified and treated at the surgeon’s discretion.

See Updates: Arthroscopy in Distal Radius Fractures by F. del Piñal, available online at ExpertConsult.com.

#### Distal Radius Malunion

del Piñal et al. described an inside- out osteotomy technique under arthroscopic guidance to correct an articular malunion of duration between 5 weeks to 3 months in 4 patients under dry arthroscopy.The joint was explored with a 2.7- mm arthroscope through the standard portals with dry arthroscopy. To allow room to introduce the curettes and the osteotomes (4 mm wide), the instrumentation portals were made slightly larger than usual. Malunited fragments were cut with the osteotomes from inside the joint and advanced out. Fragments were mobilized, and granulating tissue and/or new bone was removed with curettes and synovial resectors. After disimpaction and reduction, fixation with plates or screws via the appropriate open approach was performed under arthroscopic control. Articular stepoffs were corrected to 0 mm and gaps to within 1 mm in each patient. The author concluded that this procedure allowed the surgeon to define each cartilage- containing fragment and to recreate the original articular fracture line without a concern for creation of new fracture lines on the articular surface. This technique could be used for patients with irregularly defined fragments that are not amenable to classic techniques.

#### Scaphoid Fractures

The scaphoid is predominantly articular, so preservation of articular congruity is as important as treatment of any articular fracture.Scaphoid has a tenuous blood supply. It also is pivotal in controlling carpal kinematics. Therefore, preservation of scaphoid anatomy and vascularity is critical for normal hand and wrist function. Conventionally cast immobilization has shown a successful healing rate of 85% to 95% of acute nondisplaced scaphoid fractures. Cast treatment is the treatment of choice for nondisplaced waist fractures. However, the majority (>80%) of patients with scaphoid fracture are young men between the ages of 20 and 30 years with high functional demands. Problems with prolonged cast immobilization include stiffness, decreased grip strength, a significant rehabilitation period, inability to work, and physical and economic morbidities. Percutaneous screw fixation is now well accepted and popular with excellent reported results. Arora et al. compared cast and operative fixation for stable scaphoid fractures and identified a shorter time to union, decreased overall costs, and a 7- week earlier return to sports and/or work in the operative cohort. Several minimal incision techniques have been developed, including a volar percutaneous approach, a percutaneous dorsal approach, miniopen, and an all- arthroscopic approach. The arthroscopic-assisted techniques are described here.

**Geissler technique.** Geissler described a technique that could be used for both acute fractures and selected scaphoid fracture nonunions. The advantage of this technique is that the starting point for insertion of the guidewire and the headless cannulated screw is viewed directly under the arthroscope. There is also a technical advantage over the dorsal percutaneous approach as hyperflexion of the wrist is not required during wire insertion and therefore lessens the chance of inducing a humpback deformity.

The wrist is placed in the traction tower and the arthroscope is inserted in the 3- 4 portal to evaluate any associated soft tissue injuries. The C- arm should be placed in horizontal manner so that fluoroscopic assessment of the wrist can be done simultaneously whenever appropriate. It is helpful to place the forearm baseplate of the traction tower on the dorsal aspect of the forearm to make it easier to flex the wrist. The arthroscope is then transferred into the 6R portal and the wrist is flexed approximately 30 degrees. A 14- gauge needle is inserted through the 3- 4 portal to align the ideal starting point for the guidewire in the scaphoid proximal pole (eFig. 17.63). The needle should pass easily and care should be taken to avoid puncture or injury to an extensor tendon. Viewing from the 6R portal, the needle is pushed by hand into the scaphoid proximal pole at its junction with the membranous portion of the SL ligament at the approximate volar- dorsal midpoint. The flexed wrist is then evaluated under fluoroscopy in the posteroanterior (PA) plane and the starting point of the needle is confirmed and adjusted as necessary (eFig. 17.64). The needle is next aimed toward the thumb and a guidewire is advanced under fluoroscopy down the central axis of the scaphoid to abut the distal pole. The position of the guidewire is then evaluated in the PA plane, oblique, and lateral views under fluoroscopy by rotating the forearm in the traction tower (eFig. 17.65). A second guidewire is then inserted percutaneously to abut the proximal pole of the scaphoid, and the length of the screw is determined by the difference in the length of the 2 wires. The screw should be at least 4 mm shorter than measured to avoid screw prominence or distraction of the fracture site.

![[eFig.17.63.png]]
> **eFig. 17.63** A 14-gauge needle is then brought in through the 3- 4 portal and the proximal pole of the scaphoid is impaled just radial to the junction of the interosseous ligament. It is important as one passes the needle through the 3- 4 portal that it easily goes through the portal so as not to injure an extensor tendon.

![[eFig.17.64.png]]
> **eFig. 17.64** The traction tower is then flexed to horizontal to check position of the needle under fluoroscopy to be exactly at the proximal pole of the scaphoid.

![[eFig.17.65.png]]
> **eFig. 17.65** Fluoroscopic view showing the guide wire inserted through the 14-gauge needle and down the central axis of the scaphoid.

The arthroscope is then placed in the midcarpal space to evaluate the fracture reduction. Fractures of the proximal pole are best seen with the arthroscope in the MCU portal, and fractures of the waist are best visualized with the arthroscope in the MCR portal. The guidewire is then advanced out the volar aspect of the wrist, generally through the thenar eminence. This is an important step because if the guidewire breaks at any point during the procedure, the two ends can be retrieved from dorsal and palmar. If the reduction is not considered satisfactory, the wire is further withdrawn distally until it is in the distal fragment alone, and a joystick is inserted into the proximal pole to help manipulate the fracture to an anatomic reduction. Frequently, manipulation of the wrist position in the traction tower helps facilitate the reduction. Once the fracture is felt to be anatomic, as viewed directly with the arthroscope in the midcarpal space, the guidewire is advanced proximally into the proximal pole of the scaphoid and out the initial 3- 4 portal.

At this point, the guidewire should be exiting the volar and dorsal aspects of the wrist. The scaphoid is then drilled through a sheath to protect the extensor tendons and cutaneous nerves. A cannulated screw is then placed over the guidewire. The position of the screw is evaluated under fluoroscopy in the PA, oblique, and lateral planes (eFig. 17.66). The reduction and compression of the scaphoid are evaluated at the midcarpal space arthroscopically. In the radiocarpal space, it is imperative to evaluate that the headless cannulated screw has been advanced below the articular cartilage. Occasionally, the screw may look to be buried under fluoroscopy, but arthroscopic evaluation shows the screw remains slightly prominent, which could injure the articular cartilage of the distal radius. If this is the case, the screw should be withdrawn back to the scaphoid.

![[eFig.17.66.png]]
> **eFig. 17.66** Fluoroscopic view showing ideal placement of the headless cannulated screw across the scaphoid fracture.

**Slade technique.** Slade described a dorsal arthroscopic-assisted technique. In this technique, the wrist is flexed and pronated until the proximal and distal poles of the scaphoid are aligned into a cylinder. This forms a ring sign (eFig. 17.67). Once the ring sign is achieved, a guidewire is placed down the center of the ring in line with the axis of the x- ray beam. The wire is advanced out the volar aspect of the wrist until it is flush with the dorsal pole of the scaphoid. In this manner, now the wrist can be extended to verify the precise position of the guidewire and the PA, oblique, and lateral planes. The position of the guidewire in the proximal pole of the scaphoid can be verified with the arthroscope in the radiocarpal space. The arthroscope can be placed in the midcarpal space to evaluate reduction of the scaphoid fracture. The rest of fracture evaluation, reduction, and fixation are similar to the Geissler technique.

![[eFig.17.67.png]]
> **eFig. 17.67** Fluoroscopic view of the guidewire in place using the Slade technique. The proximal and distal poles of the scaphoid have been aligned by hyperflexion to form the “ring sign.” The guidewire is passed parallel to the fluoroscopic beam.

**Herbert–Whipple technique.** Another arthroscopic- assisted technique using the Herbert–Whipple jig (Zimmer, Warsaw, IN) has been described. With the arthroscope in the 6R portal, the jig is inserted into the 1- 2 portal and the spike of the jig is placed at the junction of the SL ligament and the proximal pole of the scaphoid, identical to the starting point of the Geissler technique (eFig. 17.68). An incision is then made over the distal pole of the scaphoid and the barrel of the jig is advanced to the scaphoid tubercle to provide provisional compression of the fracture. The reduction of the scaphoid may be arthroscopically assessed by placing the arthroscope in the MCR portal. If the fracture is not anatomically reduced, the barrel of the jig is released and with manipulation or joysticks, the scaphoid fracture is anatomically reduced (eFig. 17.69). Once anatomic reduction of the scaphoid fracture is confirmed arthroscopically, the barrel of the jig is then compressed to provide provisional fixation. A guidewire is then placed down the jig into the long axis of the scaphoid. It is then drilled from distal to proximal and measured, and the appropriate length Herbert–Whipple headless compression screw is placed.

![[eFig.17.68.png]]
> **eFig. 17.68** Arthroscopic view of the Herbert–Whipple jig being inserted through the 1- 2 portal. The spike of the guide is placed at the junction of the scapholunate interosseous ligament and the proximal pole of the scaphoid.

![[eFig.17.69.png]]
> **eFig. 17.69** A, Fluoroscopic view showing ideal alignment of the scaphoid fracture with the Herbert–Whipple jig in place. B, Arthroscopic view of the scaphoid fracture from the radial midcarpal space with the Herbert–Whipple jig applied but not tensioned. The fracture gap remains open. With passive extension and radial deviation, the fracture can be manipulated in the tower to be anatomically reduced, and the jig is compressed to provide provisional stabilization.

Arthroscopy is a good adjunct to this minimal invasive approach in managing the more difficult situations, such as in displaced fracture, fracture comminution, and delay presentation. Wrist arthroscopy can be used to evaluate and assist in fracture reduction. Cases of delay presentation or comminution can be treated with arthroscopically assisted percutaneous internal fixation combined with the use of injectable bone substitute or autogenous bone graft inserted to the comminuted or delay union site through the midcarpal joint.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC- ASSISTED REDUCTION FIXATION OF DISPLACED SCAPHOID FRACTURE

Displaced fractures are first managed by closed reduction and pinning from volar approach under fluoroscopic guidance. If the alignment is not satisfactory, wrist arthroscopy is conducted. Arthroscopic- assisted reduction is performed with midcarpal joint arthroscopy, viewing at MCU portal, and working at MCR portal (Fig. 17.33). The guide wire is withdrawn to just distal to the fracture site but left within the distal fragment. Finger traction force is released. Reduction is performed by manipulating the guide pin in the distal scaphoid. Another larger- diameter Kirschner wire is sometimes necessary if the guide pin is not strong enough to work as a joystick (Fig. 17.34). A 2.7- mm trocar or an angled spatula is inserted through the MCR portal to control and reduce any stepping or rotational malalignment as seen. Then the guide pin is driven across the fracture site, and rotational control gained by inserting a second Kirschner wire. The direction of the second Kirschner wire does not need to be exact. The surgeon can use his or her thumb and index fingertip on the scaphoid tubercle and Lister tubercle, respectively, as a guide to the direction of the second wire. The guide position is adjusted as necessary under fluoroscopy. Measurement, drilling, and tapping can then be performed as above. Screw insertion and fracture compression are visualized arthroscopically.

![[Fig.17.33.png]]
> **Fig. 17.33** Setting of arthroscopic-assisted reduction fixation of scaphoid fracture. Gap in fracture can be confirmed with the arthroscope in the midcarpal joint and reduction facilitated with probing and manipulation under direct vision and correlated with fluoroscopy.

![[Fig.17.34.png]]
> **Fig. 17.34** Use of Kirschner wires as joy sticks to manipulate the fracture fragments in a scaphoid fracture with marked displacement to achieve better reduction under arthroscopic control.

#### Perilunate Fracture Dislocation

In this type of injury, most or nearly all the ligaments around the lunate are disrupted. The wrist is usually swollen with hematoma and hemarthrosis. Anatomic reduction of the scaphoid fracture by closed means is a challenge. Realigning the floating lunate to its normal relationship with adjacent carpal bones, even under fluoroscopic guidance, is more difficult. This injury usually affects young men, whose articular cartilage is often thick, and that makes exact fluoroscopic assessment of the true intercarpal interval another challenge. Arthroscopy helps treat not only the scaphoid fracture component but also the gap, step, and rotation between scaphoid and lunate and between the lunate and triquetrum.

Liu and Chen reported their experience in 24 patients with dorsal perilunate dislocation (4) or fracture- dislocation (20) treated with arthroscopically assisted reduction and percutaneous fixation. The average time from injury to surgery was 8 days (range, 2 to 20). Closed reduction by manipulation was successful in 19 cases but arthroscopic reduction was needed in 5 patients. The mean follow- up was 14.8 months (range, 6 to 32). According to the Mayo Wrist Scores, overall functional outcomes were rated as excellent in 13 patients (54%), good in 6 (25%), fair in 4 (17%), and poor in 1 (4%). The favorable result of arthroscopic- assisted reduction fixation in perilunate fracture dislocation was echoed by other studies.

**Perilunate dislocations (Liu and Chen).** The 3- 4 and 4- 5 portals were utilized. The volarly tilted lunate usually provided sufficient space for the placement of the arthroscope to inspect the radiocarpal joint. As a rule, initial vision was always obscured by blood and debris. Debridement of hematoma, ligament flaps, and bony or chondral debris was required to improve vision. The TFCC, volar, and intercarpal ligaments were examined by direct inspection and manual testing with an arthroscopic probe. TFCC tears were addressed according to the injury types. All volar ligament lesions and chondral flaps were debrided. They noted the cause of failure of reduction was due to entrapment either of the volarly tilted lunate (with or without the proximal scaphoid fragment in transscaphoid injuries), by the capitate, or by interposed torn palmar capsular ligaments. With finger- trap suspension, a Freer elevator or shoulder arthroscopic probe could be used to reduce the dislocation arthroscopically by a “shoehorn maneuver.” The 4- 5 portal was particularly convenient to apply this maneuver because the instruments were directly facing the subluxated lunate. The midcarpal joint was then examined through the MCR and MCU portals. The SL and LT ligaments were assessed by using a probe. Any soft tissue or bony fragments interposed between the SL and LT intervals were debrided or removed to facilitate reduction of the intercarpal joints. Under fluoroscopy, two Kirschner wires were inserted into the lunate from the scaphoid and triquetrum, respectively, without crossing the intercarpal interval. If the lunate was not in neutral position (i.e., VISI or DISI), the wrist was passively extended or flexed to restore the normal radiolunate (RL) angle. The RL joint was then temporarily transfixed with a percutaneous Kirschner wire inserted from the dorsal distal radius. Under direct visualization through the midcarpal portal, reduction of the SL and LT intervals was conducted by manipulating the Kirschner wires of the scaphoid and triquetrum. Correction of steps or rotational deformity between the intercarpal intervals could be obtained by a probe or depressor through the midcarpal portal. The Kirschner wires were then driven across the intercarpal intervals into the lunate. In cases with grossly unstable and easily dislocatable SL joints, which implied incompetency of both primary and secondary stabilizers, the SL ligament reduction would be augmented by a dorsal capsulodesis through a miniopen approach with suture anchors.

**Transscaphoid perilunate fracture- dislocations (Liu and Chen).** A thorough examination and debridement of the radiocarpal and midcarpal joints was as described before. The authors found evidence of SL ligament injuries in almost all patients (19 of the 20 patients) with hemorrhage and attenuation. Usually the scaphoid fractures were treated first. In transscaphoid perilunate fracture dislocations, the scaphoid fractures were often significantly displaced and sometimes comminuted. The most critical and technical demanding step was the arthroscopically assisted reduction and percutaneous fixation of the scaphoid fracture. The wrist was placed on the operating table. Closed reduction was often unsatisfactory and difficult. The authors inserted a guide wire along the central axis of the distal fragment from the scaphoid tubercle but did not cross the fracture line. With grossly unstable or comminuted fractures, additional antirotation wires were placed into the distal fragment as joysticks for arthroscopic reduction. The wrist was then placed in traction and the fractures were visualized through the MCU portal. The fracture was reduced with the joysticks. Sometimes added leverage of the proximal fragment was required by a probe or a Kirschner wire joystick in order to attain precise rotational and translational correction in all planes. When anatomic reduction was achieved, the assistant drove the guide wire and antirotation wire across the fracture line into the proximal fragment. The authors used cannulated headless screws for all scaphoid fracture fixations. Retrograde reaming and screw placement from distal scaphoid were performed after the traction was off and the wrist was put in extension on the operation table, which facilitated the application of compression across the fracture line. For proximal one- third scaphoid fractures, the cannulated screw was placed from a dorsal approach. In these cases, the guide wire was advanced out to the dorsal wrist while the wrist was flexed. Antegrade reaming and screw placement were conducted from a stab incision over the dorsal wrist. In cases with significant fracture comminution or instability, an antirotation wire was left for additional stability. All the wires were cut and buried underneath the skin. Associated soft tissue injuries and carpal malalignments were addressed simultaneously as described for perilunate dislocations. Reduction and compression of the fracture, placement of the screw, and correction of the carpal alignment were confirmed both fluoroscopically and arthroscopically (Figs. 17.35 to 17.37; Video 17.27).

![[Fig.17.35.png]]
> **Fig. 17.35** A case of transscaphoid perilunate fracture dislocation.

![[Fig.17.36.png]]
> **Fig. 17.36** Fluoroscopic and arthroscopic views showing arthroscopic-assisted reduction of stepping and gapping of the scaphoid fracture and fixation with a cannulated screw.

![[Fig.17.37.png]]
> **Fig. 17.37** Final fluoroscopic views of the reduction fixation of the transscaphoid perilunate fracture dislocation and clinical photos of the operated hand with minimal incisions. The swelling due to saline infusion usually subsides in a few days.

After the operation, all wrists were immobilized in short- arm cast. A thumb spica was used for transscaphoid perilunate fracture dislocations. Cases with TFCC injury were immobilized in a long- arm cast with forearm in semisupination, and then in a short- arm splint for 6 weeks. The Kirschner wires and cast were removed at 8 weeks postoperatively, followed by active wrist motion exercise. Manual activities and sports were started after the scaphoid united.

### Carpal Instability

The key to arthroscopic management of carpal instability is the recognition of what is normal and what is pathologic when viewing the interosseous ligaments and the alignment of the carpal bones. Wrist arthroscopy is not complete until the midcarpal space is examined when carpal instability is suspected. The interosseous ligaments are unusual in that they can stretch and attenuate substantially before they avulse. This can allow bulging of the interosseous ligament and rotation of the carpal bones even without complete tearing. The mechanics of carpal instability are complex involving not only the interosseous ligaments but also the intrinsic and extrinsic ligaments. This combination of ligament pathology allows for malrotation of the carpal bones and clinical instability. When an interosseous ligament tears, it may block visualization with the arthroscope in the radiocarpal space. Rotation of the carpal bones is best accomplished with the unobstructed view available with the arthroscope in the midcarpal space.

The SL ligament and LT ligament should have a normal concave appearance as seen from the radiocarpal space. The SL ligament is best seen with the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal and the LT ligament is best seen with the arthroscope in either the 4- 5 or 6R portal because of its oblique distal relationship to the proximal carpal row. In the midcarpal space, the SL interval should be tightly congruent without a step- off or gap. The LT interval should be congruent, but frequently a 1 mm distal step- off of the triquetrum to the lunate is seen when the wrist is under traction. The step- off resolves when the traction force is removed. As long as the joint does not admit a probe, this phenomenon is not considered abnormal. There is generally slight motion between the lunate and the triquetrum.

A spectrum of injury can be seen in both the SL ligament and LT ligament when they are injured. The interosseous ligaments appear to attenuate and then tear from a volar- to- dorsal direction. Geissler et al. devised an arthroscopic classification of carpal instability based on observations of injury to the SL and LT ligaments when associated with fractures of the distal radius (Fig. 17.38). In grade I injuries, there is loss of the normal concave appearance between the carpal bones. The interosseous ligament bulges with a convex appearance as seen with the arthroscope in the radiocarpal space. Evaluation of the midcarpal space shows the carpal bones to be congruent with no step- off. In grade II injuries, the interosseous ligament is again convex as seen with the arthroscope in the radiocarpal space. In the midcarpal space, the carpal bones are not gapped, but they may no longer be congruent. There may be palmar flexion of the scaphoid, and its dorsal articular margin may appear distal to the lunate.

![[Fig.17.38.png]]
> **Fig. 17.38** Arthroscopic views from radiocarpal and midcarpal joints of various degrees of scapholunate instability as classified by Geissler.

With grade III injuries, the interosseous ligament begins to tear and a gap is seen between the carpal bones within both the radiocarpal and midcarpal spaces. A probe may be inserted between the scaphoid and lunate and gapping can be observed from the radiocarpal and midcarpal space. In grade IV injuries, the interosseous ligament is completely detached and a 2.7- mm arthroscope can be passed freely from the radiocarpal to the midcarpal space through the interval of the carpal bones. This is a so- called drive- through sign. This corresponds to a widening SL gap as seen on the radiographs of patients with a complete SL dissociation. European Wrist Arthroscopy Society (EWAS) developed a refined arthroscopic classification system to further define the specific portion of the SL ligament being injured as shown in Table 17.1. This can direct proper region- specific procedure for treating the partial SL ligament tear.

**TABLE 17.1 Arthroscopic EWAS Classification and Relative Anatomic Findings in Cadaver Specimens**

| EWAS Arthroscopic Stage | Arthroscopic Findings of SL Ligament From Midcarpal Joint | Anatomic Findings |
| --- | --- | --- |
| I | The tip of the probe cannot enter scapholunate (SL) space, only synovitis | Not found in cadaver specimens |
| II (lesion of membranous portion of SL ligament) | The passage of the tip can go through the SL space without widening (stable) | Lesion of proximal portion of SL ligament |
| IIIA (partial lesion involving volar portion of SL ligament) | Volar SL widening on dynamic testing from midcarpal joint (volar laxity) | Lesion of volar and proximal portion of SL ligament with or without lesion of RSC/LRL |
| IIIB (partial lesion involving dorsal portion of SL ligament) | Dorsal SL widening on dynamic testing from midcarpal joint (dorsal laxity) | Lesion of volar, proximal and dorsal portion of SL ligament with partial lesion of DIC |
| IIIC (complete SL ligament tear, reducible SL joint) | Complete widening of SL space on dynamic testing, SL joint reducible upon removal of probe | Complete lesion of SL ligament (volar, proximal, and dorsal), complete lesion of one extrinsic ligament (DIC or RSC/LRL) |
| IV (complete SL ligament tear with SL gap) | SL gap with passage of arthroscope through SL joint No radiologic abnormality | Complete lesion of SL ligament (volar, proximal, and dorsal), complete lesion of extrinsic ligaments (DIC and RSC) |
| V | Wide SL gap with passage of arthroscope through SL joint with x-ray abnormalities (SL gap, DISI deformity) | Complete lesion of SL ligament (volar, proximal and dorsal), extrinsic ligaments (DIC and RSC/LRL) and one or more other ligaments (TH, ST, DRC) |

Grade I injury of SL or LT ligament with no joint incongruence typically resolves with immobilization alone. Acute tears of the SL or LT ligament (less than 4 to 6 weeks) that result in incongruence from the midcarpal space may be arthroscopically reduced and temporarily pinned. This procedure is suitable for Geissler grade II and III SL and LT ligament acute tears and in chronic partial SL ligament tears.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC REDUCTION AND KIRSCHNER WIRE FIXATION OF SL INSTABILITY

Diagnostic wrist arthroscopy is performed as previously described. The torn portion of the SL ligament is debrided with 2.0 or 2.9 mm full radius shaver to promote primary healing. The wrist is taken out of traction. A small incision is made over anatomic snuffbox. Blunt dissection is carried down to the level of the capsule and the scaphoid in order to avoid injury to superficial branch of radial nerve (SBRN) and radial artery. Two or three 1.1 mm Kirschner wires are placed just distal to the radial styloid into the scaphoid under fluoroscopy. It is important to place the Kirschner wires either through a cannula or in oscillation mode to avoid injury to the SBRN. The Kirschner wires are placed into the scaphoid aiming toward the lunate under fluoroscopic control. Additional 1.6 mm Kirschner wires can be used as joysticks for controlling the position of scaphoid and lunate. The joystick Kirschner wire for scaphoid is inserted volarly over the scaphoid tubercle as if performing percutaneous fixation for scaphoid fracture, and dorsally for lunate. The lunate can be localized as centered within a square connecting the 3- 4, 4- 5, MCR, and MCU portals. The wrist is suspended in the traction tower but with minimal traction. The arthroscope is placed in the MCR portal. The SL interval is anatomically reduced as viewed from the midcarpal space. Reduction can be facilitated using a bone reduction forceps compressing the three bones in the proximal carpal row. Rotation of the scaphoid can be controlled with a Freer elevator or using the joystick Kirschner wires. When the joint is well reduced, the Kirschner wires are then driven across the SL interval (Figs. 17.39 to 17.41; Video 17.28).

![[Fig.17.39.png]]
> **Fig. 17.39** A case of acute scapholunate (SL) ligament injury of the right wrist with Geissler grade III instability.

![[Fig.17.40.png]]
> **Fig. 17.40** Reduction fixation was assisted with fluoroscopy and arthroscopy. Multiple Kirschner wires were driven across the reduced SL joint.

![[Fig.17.41.png]]
> **Fig. 17.41** Maintenance of the reduction of the SL joint at 10 weeks postoperation. Clinical photos demonstrate good range of the motion of the patient.

Frequently, droplets of fat are seen exiting the SL interval as Kirschner wires are being driven across the carpal bones. Three or four wires are placed and left in position for approximately 8 weeks. The Kirschner wires should be buried underneath the skin to avoid pin tract infection. The wrist is immobilized in a short-arm cast. Finger range of motion is encouraged. The wires are removed at 8 weeks under local anesthesia and gentle active mobilization of the wrist commences. Daytime resting splint is used for 2 more weeks while night splint can be used for additional 4 to 8 weeks. Physical therapy for wrist range of motion and grip strength is initiated after a 3- month interval.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC REDUCTION AND KIRSCHNER WIRE FIXATION OF LT INSTABILITY

The technique is indicated for acute injury of the LT ligament after failed conservative management for up to 4 to 6 weeks. The patient often presents with ulnar wrist pain after fall on hypothenar area with the wrist in dorsiflexion. Clinical examination may elicit tenderness at the LT joint and positive Reagan ballottement test, Kleinman shear test, and/or Lincshield compression test. Posteroanterior radiograph is often normal, though there may be step- off bewteen the lunate and triquetrum and breakage of Gilula’s lines. MRI is less reliable than arthroscopy in defining LT ligament injury. Diagnostic wrist arthroscopy is performed as previously described. The torn portion of the LT ligament is debrided with 2.0 or 2.9 mm full radius shaver to promote primary healing. The wrist is taken out of traction. A small incision is made over ulna side of triquetrum (eFig. 17.70). Blunt dissection is carried out to the level of triquetrum to avoid injury to dorsal cutaneous branch of ulnar nerve. Two 1.1 mm Kirschner wires are inserted from the ulnar side of the triquetrum with a soft tissue protector, aiming toward the center of the lunate. Additional 1.6 mm Kirschner wires can be inserted into the triquetrum and the lunate each from the dorsum as joysticks. Then the wrist is put onto the traction tower. The arthroscope is placed into MCU portal for assessing LT joint reduction. The reduction can be done by a joystick maneuver or using the Freer elevator to control the reduction. Kirschner wires are then advanced across the lunate (eFig. 17.71). The Kirschner wires are placed through a catheter or in oscillation mode to avoid injury to the dorsal sensory branch of the ulnar nerve. Usually, reduction of the LT interval is not as difficult compared with the SL interval. Kirschner wires are cut short and buried underneath the skin followed with below- elbow cast for 8 weeks (eFig. 17.72). Kirschner wires are removed at 8 weeks with active wrist mobilization started immediately. Passive mobilization and strengthening exercise is started at 3 months (Video 17.29).

![[eFig.17.70.png]]
> **eFig. 17.70** A diagram showing a case with acute lunotriquetral (LT) ligament avulsion injury from lunate side with Geissler grade III LT instability.

![[eFig.17.71.png]]
> **eFig. 17.71** Arthroscopic-assisted reduction of the LT joint with percutaneous fixation using two Kirschner wires.

![[eFig.17.72.png]]
> **eFig. 17.72** X-rays showing good reduction and fixation of the LT joint. The Kirschner wires have been cut short and buried underneath the skin to facilitate removal under local anesthesia at 8 weeks postoperative.

#### Arthroscopic Dorsal Capusloligamentous Repair (Mathoulin)

The arthroscopic dorsal capusloligamentous repair described by Mathoulin et al. is useful to treat incomplete dorsal SL ligament (EWAS II and IIIB) tears that are associated with avulsions of the dorsal intercarpal ligament from the scaphoid and lunate, either in isolation or as adjunct to arthroscopic reduction and Kirschner wire fixation. In acute and subacute cases, there are sufficient dorsal SL ligament remnants on the dorsal horn of the lunate and scaphoid. The complex can best be viewed from the 6R portal after releasing the traction on the hand.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Reduction and Kirschner Wire Fixation of SL/LT Instability

Indications
- Acute grade II and III SL and LT ligament injury under 6 weeks

Preoperative Evaluation
- Clinical tenderness
- X- ray with stress view, PA view for LT instability and AP view for SL instability

Pearls
- Reduction and pinning is useful when there is a healthy remnant of the injured interosseous ligament as seen at radiocarpal joint.
- Midcarpal joint arthroscopy with probing is mandatory for diagnosis and grad ing.
- Reduce traction force when assessing joint stability.
- SL joint best seen at MCR portal; LT joint best seen at MCU portal.
- Beware of the type II lunate, which may cause confusion with LT instability.

Technical Points
- Insert Kirschner wire through a cannula or using oscillating mode to reduce the chance of injury to cutaneous nerves.
- Use 1.6 mm Kirschner wire as joystick for reduction.
- Apply a Freer elevator to reduce the step- off.
- Apply a bone reduction forceps to the scaphoid and triquetrum to further sta bilize the joint before pinning.
- May add arthroscopic dorsal capsuloligamentous suture (Mathoulin tech nique, below) to augment the repair.
- Cut short and bury Kirschner wires under skin to avoid pin tract infection.

Pitfalls
- Injury to the sensory branch of the radial nerve and DSBUN
- Premature removal of pins may cause recurrence of instability.

Postoperative Care
- Casting for 8 weeks
- Removal of pins under local anesthesia, beware of radial nerve or DSBUN
```

An 18- gauge hypodermic needle is inserted through 3- 4 portal penetrating the dorsal capsule in a dorsal to palmar direction to capture the radial remnant of the SL ligament. The needle tip enters the midcarpal joint in a proximal to distal direction. The arthroscope is then placed in the midcarpal joint through the MCU portal. A 3- 0 absorbable monofilament suture is threaded through the needle (eFig. 17.73). A second needle with suture thread is passed parallel to the first, through the dorsal capsule and lunate remnant of SL ligament on the ulnar aspect. Both sutures are brought out through the MCR portal simultaneously with a fine mosquito forceps. A substantial knot is tied between the distal end of the two sutures and traction is applied to the proximal end of both sutures at the 3- 4 portal in order to place the knot into the midcarpal joint between scaphoid and lunate, just deep to the dorsal SL ligament (eFig. 17.74). A second knot is tied between the proximal ends of the two sutures and introduced into 3- 4 portal and placed outside the radiocarpal joint on the dorsal aspect of joint capsule opposing the SL ligament. This results in a capsuloligamentous repair or dorsal capsuloplasty between the dorsal SL ligament, the dorsal scaphoid and lunate, and the avulsed intracapsular dorsal intercarpal (DIC) ligament (eFig. 17.75). For more severe injuries requiring SL reduction, the SL repair and capsuloplasty sutures are placed, the SL joint is reduced, and the SL joint is stabilized with two 1.1 mm parallel Kirschner wires from scaphoid to lunate and one 1.1 mm Kirschner wire from scaphoid to capitate. Then the second knot is tied down over the dorsal capsule and DIC ligament to complete the repair. After the operation, the operated wrist is immobilized in a below- elbow cast for 8 weeks. Both the cast and Kirschner wires are removed 8 weeks after surgery and active range- of- motion exercise is begun, followed by passive motion exercise around 10 to 12 weeks.

![[eFig.17.73.png]]
> **eFig. 17.73** A diagram illustrating the technique of arthroscopic dorsal capsuloligamentous repair as described by Mathoulin in case of complete tear of the SL ligament. Two 18-gauge hypodermic needles are inserted through 3- 4 portal and penetrating the dorsal capsule in dorsal to palmar direction into the midcarpal joint to capture the scaphoid and lunate sided remnants of the SL ligament through the dorsal capsule. Both sutures are brought out through the MCR portal simultaneously with a fine mosquito forceps. (Courtesy of Dr. Christophe Mathoulin, MD, FMH.)

![[eFig.17.74.png]]
> **eFig. 17.74** A knot is tied between the distal end of the two sutures and traction is applied to the proximal end of both sutures at the 3- 4 portal in order to place the knot into the midcarpal joint between scaphoid and lunate. A second knot is tied between the proximal ends of the two sutures and placed outside the radiocarpal joint capsule opposing the SL ligament. This results in a capsuloligamentous repair. ![[eFig.17.75.png]]
> **eFig. 17.75** A sagittal cross session of a cadaveric wrist showing the position of the suture knot inside the scapholunate joint (*) and that outside the radiocarpal joint capsule opposite to SL joint (**) creating a block of substantial capsuloligamentous repair tissue. (Courtesy of Dr. Christophe Mathoulin, MD, FMH.)

del Piñal et al. described an all- inside technique for arthroscopic suturing of the volar SL ligament. With this technique, sutures are placed volarly through a Tuohy needle to plicate the volar ligament remnants and the LRL ligament. He illustrated this technique in 4 patients, but outcomes were not reported.

For acute complete tears of the interosseous ligament in grade IV injuries to the wrist, it is generally agreed that a direct primary repair through an open arthrotomy is necessary (see Chapter 13). While Kirschner wires are necessary for stabilization, they can only be left in for a short period of time (6 to 8 weeks). Wires have been associated with pin tract infections, loosening or breakage, and potentially can limit rehabilitation. The concern is that the interosseous ligaments may take a long time to heal, and a period of 6 to 8 weeks may not be enough for them to heal when the pins are removed.

Whipple reviewed the results of arthroscopic treatment of SL instability in patients who were followed for a duration of 1 to 3 years.Eighty- three percent of patients who had SL instability of 3 months and a gap of 3 mm had symptomatic relief compared to only 63% who had symptoms greater than 3 months and more than a 3- mm side- toside difference. The conclusion from the study was that arthroscopic pinning should be reserved for patients with an acute injury and incomplete tears rather than a chronic, complete tear. Osterman and Seidman also reported good- to- excellent result in 16 of the 20 patients with acute LT instability without volar intercalated segment instability.

#### Arthroscopic Debridement and Thermal Shrinkage

Although electrothermal shrinkage of ligaments in other joints of the body, primarily the shoulder, has not been shown to be effective, thermal stabilization of the capsule and ligamentous structures has been used with good success in hand and wrist surgery, and may play a role in the management of chronic partial tears of the interosseous ligaments. This may be because the wrist can be immobilized for a prolonged period of time, allowing the contracted collagen to mature. The wrist may also be subjected to lesser amount of static loading compared with the shoulder joint. Thermal stabilization exploits the heat liable intramolecular bonds that maintain the triple helix structure in type I collagen. Ideal temperatures for thermal stabilization have been described as between 60°C to 67°C. Thermal shrinkage occurs until a plateau is reached beyond which thermal shrinkage will not occur. As the tissue cools, it has been shown that up to 10% of initial shrinkage may be lost and some of the bonds renature.

For the carpus, the technique is best indicated for subacute and chronic grade I and II, and selected cases of grade III injury of the interosseous ligaments. It consists of debridement of the torn portion of the SL and LT ligaments through radiocarpal 3- 4 and 4- 5 portals, followed by capsular shrinkage of the remaining intact but stretched portions using a RF probe inserted into the radiocarpal and midcarpal joints. The monopolar or bipolar probe can be used. The monopolar probe is shown to lead to deeper penetration of heat to contract the interosseous ligament compared with a bipolar probe.
