However, a bipolar probe yields higher surface temperatures that can further increase the temperature of the irrigation fluid in the wrist due to its small volume.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC DEBRIDEMENT AND THERMAL SHRINKAGE

From radiocarpal joint, the SL ligament is visualised using the 4- 5 portal while 3- 4 portal can be used to assess LT ligament. The torn part of SL or LT ligament is debrided to healthy stable margins with a 2.0 mm or 2.9 mm full radius shaver from 3- 4 and 4- 5 portal, respectively. Alternatively, debridement can be performed with the RF probe. The purpose of debridement is to relieve mechanical impingement caused by the detached tissue flap and to alleviate pain resulting from synovitis. Occasionally in LT ligament injury, a substantial amount of scar tissue and synovitis can develop over the dorsoulnar aspect of the radiocarpal joint, which requires removal before evaluation of the LT ligament tear. Stability should be checked by reprobing after debridement via the midcarpal joint portals to ensure the stability is not jeopardized.

After debridement, a unipolar or bipolar RF probe of small caliber is inserted through the corresponding portals. In SL ligament injury, the target is the intact but attenuated or stretched part of the remaining ligament. This can include the volar part, proximal membranous part, and the dorsal portion of the ligament in the radiocarpal joint, as well as the distal edge of volar SL ligament and the RSC ligament in the midcarpal joint (eFig. 17.76). For LT ligament injury, the UT and UL ligaments can be shrunk from a radiocarpal portal as they diverge from their origin on volar radioulnar ligament, as can the dorsal and volar portions of the LT ligament. At the midcarpal joint, the volar part of the LT ligament can be addressed. The probe is applied, and a short burst of energy (not longer than 2 seconds) is applied to multiple spots sequentially over the ligament and capsular structures (eFig. 17.77). Extensive paintbrush motion is not recommended to avoid devascularization. The ligament loses its sheen and fibers condense after thermal shrinkage treatment (Fig. 17.42). Although the probe energy is preset to limit heat buildup, in order to prevent permanent cartilage damage, vigorous saline irrigation and ventilation via a wide bore cannula is recommended to help dissipate the heat during the shrinkage process. The temperature of the irrigation fluid as it leaves the wrist should be monitored. The ligament tension and step- off correction can be assessed by decreasing the traction applied to the wrist in order to obtain a true sense of ligament tension. Because the collagen fibers are initially weakened during repair process, I generally use Kirschner wire fixation or cast immobilization to protect the weakened collagen for 6 to 8 weeks, followed by wrist motion exercises. I then use a removable brace during an additional 4 weeks of therapy to increase motion and strength (Video 17.30).

![[eFig.17.76.png]]
> **eFig. 17.76** In arthroscopic thermal shrinkage of the scapholunate ligament, the proximal interosseous membrane part and the dorsal part can be addressed through the radiocarpal joint portals. The distal border of the volar scapholunate ligament may be approached through the midcarpal joint with a thermal shrinkage probe.

![[eFig.17.77.png]]
> **eFig. 17.77** The volar part of the scapholunate ligament is addressed by a thermal shrinkage probe inserted from the MCU portal. L, Lunate; S, scaphoid. The shiny looking ligament is exposed after the synovium over the surface is removed.

![[Fig.17.42.png]]
> **Fig. 17.42** View with the arthroscope in the 6R portal following electrothermal shrinkage of the scapholunate interosseous ligament. The thermal probe is inserted through the 3- 4 portal. Note the caramel colorization following shrinkage of the scapholunate interosseous ligament and restoration of the normal concave appearance to the ligament.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Debridement and Thermal Shrinkage

Indications
- Subacute and chronic grade I and II, and selected grade III SL and LT liga ment injury beyond 6 weeks
- No DISI/VISI deformity

Preoperative Evaluation
- Clinical tenderness at the corresponding carpal interval
- X- ray with stress views, PA view for LT instability, and AP view for SL insta bility

Pearls
- For SL instability, shrinkage of the dorsal and membranous parts of the SL ligament at the radiocarpal joint; the volar part and the RSC ligament are assessed and treated from the midcarpal portals.
- For LT instability, shrinkage of the dorsal and membranous part of the LT ligament, the UL and the UT ligaments from the radiocarpal joint; the volar part of the LT ligament is treated from the midcarpal portals.
- Reduced traction during the shrinkage process is helpful to assess step- off.
- It is necessary to remove the synovium and scar tissue on the surface of the ligaments before applying thermal shrinkage probe.
- Monitor temperature of the outﬂow saline.

Technical Points
- Apply a short burst of energy (no longer than 2 to 3 seconds) to multiple spots sequentially, and do not repeat an application in the same spot.
- Extensive paintbrush motion is not recommended.
- Vigorous saline irrigation and ventilation via a wide bore cannula helps dissipate the heat.
- Observe for loss of sheen and condensation of the ligament ﬁbers.

Pitfalls
- Extensive and prolonged shrinkage may damage the ligament.
- Excessive heat may cause thermal damage to adjacent tendons.
- Inadequate shrinkage or immobilization risks recurrence of instability.

Postoperative Care
- Casting for 6 to 8 weeks for grade I and II injury
- Kirschner wire ﬁxation preferred for grade III injury
```

### Chondroplasty

Cartilage lesions are commonly seen in degenerative and posttraumatic conditions of the wrist and are an important differential diagnosis of chronic wrist pain. They may be manifested as chondromalacia or a frank osteochondral defect (Video 17.31). Wrist arthroscopy is considered the gold standard in diagnosing chondral lesions in the wrist, though recent advances in magnetic resonance imaging have substantially increased significantly their diagnostic ability.Prognosis depends on the size of the lesion as well as underlying cause for the lesions. Culp et al. have modified the Outerbridge classification for chondral lesions of the wrist. Grade I represents softening of the hyaline cartilage. Grade II consists of fibrillation and fissuring. Grade III has fibrillated lesion of varying depth, and grade IV represents a full- thickness defect down to the subchondral bone. Grade I to III lesions are generally treated with debridement and localized synovectomy. Localized grade IV lesions can be treated with abrasion chondroplasty and subchondral drilling. Whipple showed that a chondral defect smaller than 5 mm responded well to arthroscopic treatment.Lesions with secondary underlying causes such as instability or fracture had poorer outcomes. Chondroplasty is not indicated for diffuse cartilage loss. The aim of abrasion or drill chondroplasty is to excise damaged cartilage and unstable flaps or debris to render the rim of the chondral defect stable and to encourage fibrocartilage formation to cover the exposed subchondral bone. Typically, a motorized shaver or bur is employed. A fine Kirschner wire of 0.9 or 1.1 mm diameter is used to drill multiple times through the subchondral bone over the base of the defect to encourage the emigration of mesenchymal cell for the formation of fibrocartilage (Video 17.32). During the insertion of a Kirschner wire, iatrogenic injury to extracapsular structures can be minimized by threading the Kirschner wire with a metallic trocar of a 14- gauge angiocatheter. Nagle reported promising early results with laser and thermal chondroplasty for grade II and III chondromalacic changes. However, the authors recommend caution for grade IV lesions, as cauterization of cancellous bone may retard fibrous tissue ingrowth.

### Bone Grafting

The articular location of scaphoid nonunions allows an arthroscopic approach for both evaluation and therapeutic intervention without disturbing the intrinsic vascular supply or the periscaphoid ligamentous architecture. In the midcarpal joint, the gentle curvature of the waist and proximal portion of the scaphoid enables a stable and spacious platform for the surgeon to carry out the necessary procedures to graft and fix a scaphoid nonunion. In 1998 Ho et al. first reported on the technique of arthroscopic bone grafting (ABG) in successfully repairing osseous defects in six cases including scaphoid nonunion, acute comminuted scaphoid fracture, bone cyst, and triscaphe fusion.

The technique can be applied to delayed unions and nonunions of the scaphoid, including those with previous implant fixation, and with a few exceptions. Poor blood supply of the scaphoid is not a contraindication to the technique as a union rate of 82% can still be expected, though the chance of persistent nonunion is higher than those nonunions with good bone bleeding observed at surgery. Humpback deformity can be corrected, and the technique provides the surgeon an opportunity to identify other ligamentous or chondral lesions which may potentially alter the course of healing and the choice of treatment. Associated arthritic changes can be identified and addressed by carefully inspecting both radiocarpal and midcarpal joints. In case of the need for revision surgery, open treatment methods can still be applied. Contraindications to ABG include long- standing scaphoid nonunion with significant noncorrectable carpal collapse and advanced SNAC wrist changes. Significant arthrofibrosis that causes difficult access of the arthroscope may be a relative contraindication. Nevertheless, with experience, patience, and use of a smaller arthroscope, most fibrotic joints can be loosened up gradually by careful arthroscopic debridement and dilation to provide adequate working space for complex articular procedures.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC BONE GRAFTING IN SCAPHOID NONUNION

**Arthroscopic surveillance.** Wrist arthroscopy is performed in the standard setting. Continuous saline irrigation is provided using a 3 L bag of normal saline solution driven by gravity. An infusion pump is not used to reduce the chance of fluid extravasation. An arm tourniquet is applied but need not be inflated. My preferred donor site for cancellous bone graft is the iliac crest, and the donor site is generally on the contralateral when two surgical teams are working simultaneously. Adrenaline in 1:200,000 dilution is injected in the portal site skin and capsule to reduce bleeding. Routine inspection of the radiocarpal joint is performed through the 3- 4 portal and the midcarpal joint through the MCR portal using a 2.7 mm or 1.9 mm arthroscope. Any accompanying chondral lesions related to SNAC should be documented since it may affect the prognosis and choice of procedure. Concomitant arthroscopic radial styloidectomy can be performed as necessary (see page 780). Typically, the fracture site cannot be seen from the radiocarpal joint unless it is very proximal. It is frequently embedded within the reflection of radiocarpal joint capsule at the dorsal ridge of the scaphoid. This capsular reflection helps to contain the bone graft, which is implanted through the midcarpal portals. We perform bone grafting through the midcarpal portals in all cases since it provides the most convenient and direct approach to the nonunion site.

**Debridement of the nonunion.** At the midcarpal joint, MCU is generally selected as the entry portal and MCR as the working portal. The MCU portal is usually easier to establish initially, as the ulnar side of the midcarpal joint is more normal. The MCR portal is the best portal to take down the nonunion in the proximal and waist portion. In distal third nonunion, the STT portal can be considered to provide a more direct access. The nonunion site is located by the presence of a cleavage line in the scaphoid articular surface or with frank articular cartilage disruption and fibrous tissue interposition (Fig. 17.43). The firmness of fibrous interposition should be assessed carefully by direct palpation with a small probe inserted from the MCR portal. A stable firm fibrous anchorage may explain the relative lack of symptoms in some patients with nonunion. In delayed union, the fibrous tissue may cover the bridging trabeculae, which usually form initially in the central part of the fracture. If partial union is noted, simple screw fixation without bone grafting or even casting may suffice (eFig. 17.78; Video 17.33). If a frank bony defect is encountered, curettage of the nonunion site can be performed with a fine angled curette, motorized 2.0 or 2.9 mm shaver, or 2.9 mm bur until all the fibrous scar tissue and sclerotic bone are removed. If the patient has undergone prior fracture treatment with a cannulated screw, the loosened screw should be removed at this junction to facilitate a thorough debridement of the nonunion site. The frequently intact cartilage over the proximal side of the nonunion or any pseudocapsule formed should be preserved as much as possible to avoid subsequent graft spillage to the radiocarpal joint. Both ends of the nonunion are burred until cancellous bone with or without punctate bleeding is reached (Fig. 17.44). The tourniquet is not inflated so that the vascularity of the remaining bone fragments can be assessed more accurately. A better view of the proximal fragment can be obtained by changing the entry portal to MCR. Further curettage of the proximal fragment can be performed by switching the instrument to the STT portal if necessary. Tsubokawa et al. recommended the use of a dental mirror for checking the curettage of the nonunion site at the proximal fragment. This site may be difficult to see from dorsal portals. If bleeding is not obvious or uncertain, the saline irrigation can be stopped temporarily to reduce the hydrostatic pressure inside the joint and encourage bleeding from the nascent blood vessels (Video 17.34).

![[Fig.17.43.png]]
> **Fig. 17.43** Nonunion site of the scaphoid over its waist position as viewed through the MCU portal, represented by cleavage of the articular cartilage.

![[eFig.17.78.png]]
> **eFig. 17.78** A series of radiographs showing a case of scaphoid delayed union. In the July 2000 view, there was clear delayed union with significant cystic change at the waist of scaphoid. In the August 2000 view, there was suspicious bone growth within the fracture site. Wrist arthroscopy under PSLA was performed (see Video 17.32) and there was solid bony healing at the delayed union site demonstrated by probing. No further surgical intervention was performed and the fracture healed uneventfully in 2 months as shown by the radiograph taken on October 2000.

![[Fig.17.44.png]]
> **Fig. 17.44** Clear gap at nonunion site and punctate bleeding from cancellous bone in the distal part of the nonunion after initial burring and curettage.

**Correction of humpback deformity.** After adequate debridement and curettage, the two fragments of the nonunion should be mobile enough for reduction of humpback and/or DISI deformity (Fig. 17.45). The hand is taken off the traction tower and reduction maneuver is performed under fluoroscopic control on a hand table. Correction of the deformity can be facilitated by using the Linscheid maneuver. In the presence of intact SL ligament, dorsiflexion deformity of the proximal pole of scaphoid is corrected by passively flexing the wrist to realign the extended lunate with the radius (i.e., to restore the normal RL angle). The RL joint is then temporarily transfixed with a percutaneous 1.6- mm Kirschner wire inserted from dorsal distal radius through a small stab wound (Fig. 17.46). Closed reduction of the wrist is carried out to realign the distal fragment with the proximal one. A surgical towel roll is placed underneath the operated forearm. With the patient’s forearm in full supination, the surgeon can rest his first web space on the patient’s first web space. He then grasps and pulls on the patient’s thumb and thenar area. This maneuver helps to maintain the wrist in an extended, ulnarly deviated and slightly hypersupinated position, which favors restoration of the normal scaphoid length and alignment (Fig. 17.47). In a distal third nonunion where manipulation of the distal fragment is more difficult, a 1.6 mm Kirschner wire can be inserted into the distal fragment of the scaphoid as a joystick for a better manipulation. Manipulation of the fragment can also be done from within the joint using a probe. Percutaneous fixation and arthroscopic bone graft should proceed when the humpback and DISI deformity is corrected by closed technique, and after appropriate arthroscopic- assisted manipulation of the nonunion site (Figs. 17.48 and 17.49). If the correction of deformity cannot be satisfactorily attained, one should consider an open reduction and open bone grafting technique.

![[Fig.17.45.png]]
> **Fig. 17.45** A typical case of scaphoid nonunion with humpback and DISI deformity indicated by the abnormal radiolunate and lunocapitate angles.

![[Fig.17.46.png]]
> **Fig. 17.46** Linscheid maneuver to correct the radiolunate angle and transfixion of the radiolunate joint with a 1.6 mm Kirschner wire. The pin was inserted percutaneously from the dorsal aspect of the distal radius through a small incision.

![[Fig.17.47.png]]
> **Fig. 17.47** The typical manual reduction maneuver to correctly align the scaphoid fragments before the fixation procedure. With the patient’s forearm in full supination, the surgeon can rest his or her first web space on patient’s first web space. He then grasps and pulls on the patient’s thumb and thenar area. This maneuver helps to maintain the wrist in an extended, ulnarly deviated and slightly hypersupinated position, which favors restoration of the normal scaphoid length and alignment.

![[Fig.17.48.png]]
> **Fig. 17.48** Bone graft was inserted from the midcarpal joint to fill up the bone defect at the nonunion site progressively through an arthroscopic approach. Final stabilization of the nonunion was achieved with multiple Kirschner wires.

![[Fig.17.49.png]]
> **Fig. 17.49** Excellent bony union and clinical outcome at 5 months postoperation. The surgical scars are inconspicuous.

Once the fracture fragments are well aligned and the humpback deformity corrected, the fragments are provisionally transfixed with a 1.1 mm Kirschner wire inserted percutaneously from the scaphoid tubercle region under image intensifier control. A 3 mm transverse skin incision is made along the skin crease 5 mm distal to the scaphoid tubercle. A small pointed- tip scissor is used to perforate the STT joint, release a hemarthrosis, and dilate the tract to the starting point on the scaphoid tubercle. The Kirschner wire should aim eccentrically at more a radial and proximal location across the nonunion site so that the pin will not interfere with the subsequent procedure of insertion of bone graft arthroscopically (Fig. 17.50). With the arthroscope viewing from midcarpal joint, the pin will be situated in a deeper and basal position inside the defect (Video 17.35). If the pin is inserted centrally or in a more ulnar and distal manner, the pin will appear more superficial at the midcarpal joint and may block the trocar from pushing the bone graft into the deeper area. When the fixation is complete, the hand can be put back to the traction tower to begin the bone grafting procedure.

![[Fig.17.50.png]]
> **Fig. 17.50** The eccentric position of the first Kirschner wire off the central axis of the scaphoid to align the two fragments.

**Bone grafting at midcarpal joint.** An arthroscopic cannula is introduced through an appropriate portal directly opposing the fracture defect. For fractures located at the proximal two- thirds of the scaphoid, the MCR portal is the most direct (Fig. 17.51). For distal one- third fractures, the STT portal can be considered. A 3.2 mm cannula can be inserted initially, which is then replaced by a 4.5 mm cannula when the joint space is considerably widened. Cancellous bone graft is harvested from the iliac crest using either trephine or an open technique through a small incision (eFig. 17.79). The volume of the bone graft obtained has to be at least three to five times of that of the defect since the graft needs to be compressed tightly in the defect to increase its compaction and strength. The bone graft is cut into small chips using scissors and delivered through the cannula with a slightly undersized trocar with a flat end such as a bone biopsy trocar (Video 17.36). The arm tourniquet can be inflated as needed to enhance visibility.

![[Fig.17.51.png]]
> **Fig. 17.51** Cancellous bone graft was delivered through an arthroscopic cannula at the MCR portal into the nonunion site. The arthroscope was inserted through the MCU portal.

![[eFig.17.79.png]]
> **eFig. 17.79** Cancellous bone graft from the iliac crest is being cut into small chips using scissors.

The most proximal and dorsal part of the defect needs to be meticulously filled up first to reduce subsequent void formation. Similarly, the empty space previously occupied by removal of a prior screw should also be filled in early in the process to avoid undergrafting. In the case of very proximal fracture, the nonunion cleavage site directly communicates with the radiocarpal joint. Filing of a defect in the midcarpal joint with bone graft can result in significant spillage of the graft into the radiocarpal joint. To avoid this undesirable outcome, a pediatric size 6 urinary catheter can be inserted into the radiocarpal joint from the 3- 4 portal, inflated with saline and hence produce a mechanical block right over the nonunion site to allow the graft to be contained within the bone defect (Fig. 17.52). If the RL joint needs to be transfixed with a Kirschner wire following the Linscheid maneuver for correction of the DISI deformity, the balloon should be inserted first to occupy the radioscaphoid space before the RL joint is reduced and transfixed. Nevertheless, significant humpback and DISI deformity are uncommon in the very proximal nonunion situation.

![[Fig.17.52.png]]
> **Fig. 17.52** (A) A Foley catheter (white arrow) was inserted into the radioscaphoid joint through the 3- 4 portal for blocking of bone graft migration. (B) Arthroscopic view of the inflated balloon of the Foley catheter at the scaphoid fossa.

Fluoroscopy is helpful to confirm the completeness and adequacy of the graft filling. A small- angled spatula can be employed at intervals to mold and contour the graft to the articular surface of the scaphoid

(Fig. 17.53). Complete filling of the defect and satisfactory scaphoid alignment is then confirmed fluoroscopically (Fig. 17.54). At the end of the procedure, we routinely inject 1 mL of fibrin glue on the surface of the graft substance after sucking out any excess fluid to contain the graft in place and to prevent adhesion of the graft to the capitate articular surface, which in some cases may lead to loss of motion at the midcarpal joint (Fig. 17.55). The glue also helps to seal off the bone graft from the bathing synovial fluid directly, as the latter may contain inhibitors of osteoblast proliferation, which may retard healing.Routine surveillance of the joint is then carried out to remove any extravasated bone graft material left behind in the midcarpal joint and sometimes in the radiocarpal joint. Release of traction would allow the natural compression effect of the capitate to stabilize the graft and keep it in situ.

![[Fig.17.53.png]]
> **Fig. 17.53** The typical equipment for bone graft delivery: trocar with a flat end, 4.5 mm arthroscopic cannula with introductory tapered-end trocar, angled spatula of different shape/size.

![[Fig.17.54.png]]
> **Fig. 17.54** Cancellous bone graft was being directed through a cannula to fill up the dead spaces in between the fracture fragments.

![[Fig.17.55.png]]
> **Fig. 17.55** Injection of fibrin glue to the bone graft site from the MCR portal.

**Nonunion ﬁxation.** Definitive fixation can be completed with either a compression screw or additional Kirschner wires (Fig. 17.56).

![[Fig.17.56.png]]
> **Fig. 17.56** Compression screw fixation of the nonunion. Note the good filling of bone defect with high density of cancellous bone graft.

Percutaneous screw fixation is indicated when the nonunion has relatively small bony defect and preexisting humpback deformity is not marked (Fig. 17.57). As the bone graft is of cancellous nature, excessive compression should be avoided as this may cause collapse of the nonunion site and loss of the reduction. Multiple Kirschner wires are used for scaphoid nonunion with a relatively sizable bony defect and a large humpback deformity in the waist and distal third nonunion. The method is also indicated for persistent nonunion after previous screw fixation with screw loosening. Use of a percutaneous screw under these situations can be too demanding technically to achieve the ideal position and with sufficient bone purchase. Surgical risk such as fragmentation of the nonunion fragments or excessive compression force over the bone grafting site causing recurrent humpback deformity will be increased. We routinely use three 1.1 mm Kirschner wires. Position of the pins needs not be as exact as in guide pin insertion for compression screw. The first pin should aim at more a radial and proximal location across the nonunion site as described before. After bone graft impaction into the nonunion is completed, the remaining two wires can be inserted percutaneously from the same incision. Absolute parallelism of the pins is not essential. For an unstable nonunion, or in very proximal or distal nonunions, transfixing the SL and SC joints can be considered, respectively. The pins are cut short and buried underneath the skin and wound apposed with Steri-Strips so that no stitching is required (Fig. 17.58). The pin over the RL joint is left exposed outside of the skin for early removal.

![[Fig.17.57.png]]
> **Fig. 17.57** Multiple Kirschner wire fixation of the nonunion.

![[Fig.17.58.png]]
> **Fig. 17.58** The wounds were apposed with Steri-Strips without stitch.

**Postoperative care.** A short- arm plaster slab is provided and maintained for 2 weeks. The pin across the RL joint should be removed after 2 weeks. A below- elbow splint is then fabricated to provide protection to the nonunion site. Active mobilization of the wrist under supervision of hand therapist can be initiated as early as 2 weeks postoperation if the nonunion fixation is felt to be stable. This also applies to cases with Kirschner wire fixation. X- ray and CT scan are taken at intervals to monitor graft incorporation and nonunion healing. Once clinical and radiologic union is confirmed, the Kirschner wires can be removed under local anesthesia, typically at week 10 to 12 postoperatively. Passive mobilization of the wrist can be initiated once union is declared, then strengthening of the hand and upper limb can follow, usually at 12 to 16 weeks postoperative.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Bone Grafting for Scaphoid Nonunion

Indications
- Symptomatic scaphoid delay or nonunion, with or without evidence of hump back deformity, cystic formation, proximal pole avascular necrosis, or history of previous surgical intervention
- Absence of advanced SNAC wrist changes

Preoperative Evaluation
- Clinical correlation between radiologic signs and symptoms
- MRI with contrast to evaluate vascularity of the proximal scaphoid fragment

Pearls
- A right- handed surgeon may feel it easier to operate on a left scaphoid nonunion as the instrumentation portal usually falls on the right hand of the surgeon.
- A patient with pain and radiologic evidence of nonunion but without a step, gap, or humpback may beneﬁt from arthroscopic evaluation under PSLA. If the nonunion is stable on probing with early osseous formation, ABG is not necessary and simple ﬁxation or casting sufﬁces.
- The nonunion site usually cannot be seen from the radiocarpal joint unless in the proximal pole.
- Approaching a nonunion from MCU portal is often easier than from MCR por tal as the radial side joint space is more contracted.
- For cases with failed previous ﬁxation, the implant must be removed prior to nonunion debridement and grafting.
- When debriding the proximal fragment, the key is to remove all sclerotic bone until cancellous bone is reached. The capsular reﬂection on the proximal, dor sal, and volar surface of the nonunion should be preserved.
- Vascularity of the distal fragment is always preserved.
- For humpback deformity correction, employ the Linscheid maneuver (acute wrist ﬂexion and radiolunate pinning) to correct the radiolunate angle and the proxi mal scaphoid alignment. Adequate debridement of the nonunion site, relaxation (but not excision!) of the proximal volar capsule, and manipulation of the distal fragment help correct the ﬂexion, ulnar deviation, and pronation stance of the distal fragment to realign with the proximal fragment before pinning.
- Avoid over- or undergrafting.
- Fibrin glue helps to contain and seal the graft from synovial ﬂuid bathing and protect the cartilage surface of capitate facing the grafting site.
- Multiple Kirschner wires compare well to screw ﬁxation and apply well in proximal pole nonunion and waist nonunion with substantial defect and humpback deformity.
- Early gentle active wrist mobilization facilitates recovery of wrist ROM.
```

**Outcomes.** Wong and Ho reported the results of ABG in 125 patients, including 111 symptomatic scaphoid nonunions and 14 delayed unions, operated between 1997 and 2016, who achieved an overall union rate of 90%. There were 16 distal third, 67 midthird, 42 proximal third, and 8 proximal pole fractures. Twelve patients had failed previous surgery for scaphoid fracture or nonunion. DISI was present in 60 wrists (48%) and radiologic stage I/II SNAC wrist changes in 24 wrists. None were converted to open surgery. Cannulated screws were used in 38 cases and multiple Kirschner wires in 87 cases for scaphoid fixation. The average radiologic union time was 14 weeks (range, 6 to 80). Poor punctate bleeding of the proximal scaphoid was associated with union in 27 out of 33 cases (82%) while good bleeding

When assessing the vascularity of the proximal fragment, temporarily cease the saline irrigation and withhold any mechanical stimulation to the nonunion site to encourage punctate bleeding. Putting the reduction pin eccentrically in a more proximal and volar direc tion rather than in the central axis facilitates grafting from midcarpal joint. Cancellous graft volume should be three to ﬁve times of the defect volume.

Iliac crest donor site is preferred for increased volume. In a proximal pole nonunion, the fracture site may communicate with the radiocarpal joint. Use a pediatric size 6 Foley catheter to block the radioscaphoid joint space before grafting. Bone graft should be tightly packed and compressed. The dorsal and proximal part of the nonunion should be ﬁlled ﬁrst to avoid leaving voids. Larger pieces of graft should be placed at the basal layer. When using Kirschner wires to ﬁx the nonunion, three Kirschner wires of 1.1 mm are ideal. Absolute parallelism is not essential. For a proximal pole nonunion, the Kirschner wires can transﬁx the SL joint to add stability of the construct. The Kirschner wires should be cut short and buried underneath the skin. Leav ing the end too short causes difﬁculty in removal, and leaving it too long will produce pain and discomfort during rehabilitation.

Insufﬁcient mobilization of the nonunion site leads to suboptimal correction of humpback deformity. Putting the initial Kirschner wire in a central axial position blocks the ﬁlling of the defect immediately volar to the Kirschner wire with bone graft, leading to suboptimal grafting. Overgrafting may cause mechanical impingement and reduced ROM.

No stitch is required for wound closure. If no RL pin was used, gentle active mobilization of the wrist is commenced at 2 to 3 days postsurgery out of splint intermittently, both for screw ﬁxation and Kirschner wire ﬁxation cases. For RL pinning cases, the patient is casted for 2 weeks, and motion is begun after removing the RL pin at the beginning of the third week. Resting splint for 8 to 12 weeks until bone union Check bone healing and trabecular formation with CT at 8 weeks postoperative.

predicted union in 62 out of 65 cases (95%). Complications were few. There was no pain in 67 patients (54%) and improved exertional pain in the remaining. There was improvement in activities of daily living (ADL) performance scores and grip strength, with no change in wrist range. All patients had inconspicuous scars. The final SL angle was 61 degrees (range, 49 to 76) and the average AP intrascaphoid angle was 33 degrees (range, 19 to 40) (Video 17.37).

Other authors described similar techniques with successful outcome, with union rate ranging from 97% to 100%, and radiologic union time from average of 9.7 weeks to 4 months. Oh et al. conducted a retrospective comparative outcomes analysis study of arthroscopic (28) versus open (34) bone graft and fixation for unstable scaphoid nonunions. The union rates did not differ by patient subset (arthroscopic, 96.4%; open, 97.1%), and VAS score, grip strength, range of motion, Mayo Wrist Score, and DASH scores were similar at a minimum of 2 years after operation. Open bone grafting, however, restored better carpal alignment than ABG in nonunions with carpal collapse deformities, though there were no differences in clinical outcomes between the two techniques.

Other minimal invasive bone grafting techniques have also been described. In 2006 Slade reported the technique of percutaneous bone graft in patients with scaphoid nonunion and bone resorption at the nonunion interface of >1 mm and cyst formation but intact scaphoid alignment. A core of cancellous bone graft was harvested from the distal radius using a 8- gauge bone biopsy cannula. The graft was delivered to the nonunion site through the reamer portal at the proximal scaphoid using the same cannula before the nonunion was fixed with a screw. Chu and Shih described a similar technique of arthroscopically assisted percutaneous internal fixation augmented by injection of a bone graft substitute in 15 patients (three Schmitt stage I and 12 stage II cases). Union was obtained in 14 patients (93%) at a mean of 15.4 weeks. Geissler et al. also achieved union in 14 of 15 cystic nonunion (grade IV) using a dorsal technique with arthroscopy combined with demineralized bone matrix grafting and screw fixation. These percutaneous grafting techniques were minimal invasive in nature but did not incorporate a large quantity of cancellous graft, and were not suitable for nonunions with a major bone defect or humpback deformity.

### Partial Wrist Fusion

Partial wrist fusion is a motion-preserving salvage procedure for multiple painful wrist conditions. The wrist consists of multiple bony linkages from the forearm to the metacarpus via the carpal bones, and this articular peculiarity offers an opportunity to allow fusion of the painful segments of the wrist while preserving motion in the other unaffected segments. It also helps to halt predictable mechanical collapse of the carpal central column and maintain carpal height in conditions caused by dissociative lesions within or between carpal rows, or loss of bony integrity such as in SNAC wrist or Kienböck disease. A wide variety of partial wrist fusions have been designed to address problems arising from various parts of the wrist. Essentially all carpal bones and intervals can be fused selectively, and the resulting motion loss and the biomechanical effects have been studied extensively in laboratory and in clinical settings. The fusion can be performed between the radius and the proximal carpal row such as radiolunate fusion or radioscapholunate fusion; between the two carpal rows such as the STT fusion, scaphocapitate fusion, lunocapitate fusion, TH fusion, and the four- corner fusion involving the medial carpal bones; and within the carpal rows such as SL fusion, capitohamate fusion, or LT fusion. Open surgery requires capsular and ligament incisions around the wrist to expose the carpal intervals. This may lead to iatrogenic stiffness on top of the mechanical constraint rendered by the selected carpal fusion. Arthroscopic partial wrist fusion has the potential advantages of minimal surgical damage to the supporting ligaments and the capsular structures of the wrist while allowing an unimpeded view of the joint. Arthroscopic assessment ensures a more accurate staging of the arthritis and facilitates clinical decision making on the most appropriate choice of fusion. The remaining carpal motion can be maximized and postoperative pain may be lessened, which favors rehabilitation. There is also cosmetic benefit with the minimal surgical scar.

#### Indications and Contraindications

Partial wrist fusion is indicated for painful posttraumatic and osteoarthritic conditions of the wrist, which affect only part of the articulating system in a patient who is keen to have adequate pain control while preserving useful motion. Common indications include SLAC wrist, SNAC wrist, Kienböck disease, STT arthritis, and posttraumatic radiocarpal arthritis. Chronic painful dissociative or nondissociative carpal instabilities with or without secondary arthritic change are also good indications. In inflammatory arthritis, the disease should be in a quiescent stage to avoid ongoing clinical deterioration due to disease progression. Arthroscopic treatment is particularly attractive for a patient conscious of esthetic outcome.

Partial wrist fusion is contraindicated when there is active sepsis, panarthritis, and rapidly progressive joint destruction at a proliferative stage. Partial wrist fusion is not a guarantee of pain relief. The potential advantage of partial wrist fusion in preservation of a useful arc of motion may be offset by the risks of nonunion or by continued pain despite successful fusion. Chronic smokers have higher incidence of nonunion, so alternatives for pain control such as a wrist denervation can be considered. Arthroscopic partial wrist fusions are technically demanding procedures and require extensive arthroscopic experience.

Patients with preexisting extensor tendon pathology over the wrist region may have higher incidence of tendon complications associated with these complex procedures. Arthrofibrosis and long-standing carpal collapse may pose additional difficulty and risk.

#### Surgical Approach

The operation is typically performed under general anesthesia for convenience and patient comfort in harvesting bone graft as required. It can be done under regional anesthesia if bone substitute is employed, or when no graft augmentation is needed. C- arm fluoroscopy should be available for operative assessment. Arthroscopy is performed in standard fashion. Most of the procedures can be done without the use of a tourniquet. del Piñal advocates dry arthroscopy to avoid the problem of swelling and the extravasation of fluid, but the use of a tourniquet becomes mandatory throughout the procedure, which may take extended time.

Arthroscopy is performed at both the radiocarpal joint through the 3- 4 and 4- 5 portals and the midcarpal joint through the MCR and MCU portals. The aim of the examination is to stage the pathology. Particular attention is paid to assess the cartilage surfaces of the uninvolved joints to determine whether the proposed fusion is appropriate. Radial styloidectomy can be performed as indicated. The articular surfaces of the joint compartments to be fused are then prepared. The extent and depth of cartilage and bone preparation can be precisely controlled using a small arthroscopic bur. Preservation of carpal height is important for single column fusions (e.g., STT, radiolunate, lunocapitate). Bone preparation should cease when all cartilage is removed and a healthy bleeding surface is reached (eFig. 17.80). This phenomenon can be best observed if a tourniquet is not used. A complete resection of the subchondral plate is not always necessary. When preservation of carpal height is not important (e.g., four- corner fusion or radioscapholunate fusion) removal of the subchondral plate and exposure of bleeding cancellous bone may expedite fusion.

![[eFig.17.80.png]]
> **eFig. 17.80** Arthroscopic burring of the articular surfaces pending for fusion. Noted the punctate bleeding which marks the end point of burring process.

DISI deformity is commonly present in many posttraumatic arthritic conditions. It is prudent to correct the deformity in order to maximize motion and to reduce abnormal loading through the lunate fossa. A closed reduction of the lunate can be accomplished by positioning the wrist in moderate flexion and slight ulnar deviation under image intensification so that the radiolunate angle is corrected to 0 degrees and transfixing the lunate with a 1.6 mm Kirschner wire across the radiolunate joint (Linscheid maneuver). The carpal interval(s) to be fused are temporarily fixed with 1.1 mm Kirschner wires percutaneously. The pins are then withdrawn slightly to free the joint to be fused and reinserted when bone preparation and grafting are complete.

Autogenous bone graft or bone substitute is required to fill the void between the articular surfaces to be fused. An arthroscopic cannula is introduced through the appropriate portal directly opposing the prepared surfaces. Autogenous cancellous bone graft is typically harvested from the iliac crest. The bone graft is cut into small chips using scissors and delivered through the cannula with a slightly undersized trocar with a flat end into the joint cavity (see Fig. 17.52). Using a cannula of wider bore such as 4.5 or 5 mm speeds up the process. In order to prevent spillage of the bone graft, a pediatric French size 6 urinary catheter may be introduced into the neighboring joint through an arthroscopic portal using a fine mosquito Hemostat. The balloon of the catheter is inflated with saline solution until the joint space is obliterated, thus blocking any potential graft spillage. The bone graft is impacted with the trocar until a satisfactory volume of graft is achieved.

![[Fig.17.52.png]]
> **Fig. 17.52** (A) A Foley catheter (white arrow) was inserted into the radioscaphoid joint through the 3- 4 portal for blocking of bone graft migration. (B) Arthroscopic view of the inflated balloon of the Foley catheter at the scaphoid fossa.

Definitive fixation is performed by driving the Kirschner wires back cross the interval while external manual compression is maintained. The Kirschner wires may be converted into headless cannulated screws for rigid fixation. In an older patient with osteoporotic bone, multiple Kirschner wire fixation is preferred to avoid hardware migration, such as the protrusion of a screw tip into the joint (eFig. 17.81).

![[eFig.17.81.png]]
> **eFig. 17.81** A self-tapping headless screw may protrude into the joint gradually during healing process of the fusion site in a patient with osteopenic bone.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC PARTIAL WRIST FUSION

**STT fusion.** STT fusion is commonly indicated in stage I or II SLAC wrist, Kienböck disease stage 3A or 3B, and STT joint arthritis.It is frequently performed together with a radial styloidectomy, which can also be accomplished arthroscopically. Arthroscopic STT fusion is performed through the midcarpal and STT portals, using the 1.9 mm arthroscope. The arthroscope is inserted in the MCR portal and directed toward the STT joint. The synovial overgrowth and joint debris are first cleared up with a shaver and/or RF probe. The arthritic joint surface is then debrided of the remaining articular cartilage until bleeding subchondral bone is exposed (eFig. 17.82). Initially the trapezium may be difficult to reach as it is situated deeper and radial to the scaphotrapezoid joint. When the cartilage surface of the distal scaphoid and trapezoid is removed with an arthroscopic bur, more space is created for the bur to reach the surface of the trapezium. The proximal articular surface between the trapezium and trapezoid should also be denuded of cartilage. Percutaneous fixation of the STT joint can be performed with Kirschner wires transfixing scaphotrapezial, scaphotrapezoid, and trapeziotrapezoid joints (eFig. 17.83). The Kirschner wires are then withdrawn slightly to free the scaphoid, while autogenous bone graft or bone substitute is inserted through a small cannula as described above to fill up the void (Fig. 17.59), before the Kirschner wires are driven back into the scaphoid and fixation completed. Alternatively, rigid fixation can be performed by using a cannulated compression screw inserted from the trapezium to the scaphoid percutaneously. Fixation of the scaphotrapezoid and trapeziotrapezoid joints then becomes less critical (Figs. 17.60 and 17.61).

![[eFig.17.82.png]]
> **eFig. 17.82** Radiologic and arthroscopic view showing typical posttraumatic STT joint arthritis with synovial overgrowth and eburnation at joint surface.

![[eFig.17.83.png]]
> **eFig. 17.83** Operative fluoroscopy showing placement of starting awl over the distal tubercle of the trapezium (A) and the guide pin insertion across scaphotrapezial joint (B).

![[Fig.17.59.png]]
> **Fig. 17.59** Delivery of autogenous bone graft through cannula to the fusion site, impaction, and final appearance.

![[Fig.17.60.png]]
> **Fig. 17.60** Wound appearance at conclusion of surgery and progress of radiologic changes showing early union at 5 weeks and consolidation over 3 months postoperative.

![[Fig.17.61.png]]
> **Fig. 17.61** X-ray, CT, and clinical features confirming solid union of the STT joint fusion.

**Midcarpal arthrodesis.** Scaphoid excision and four- corner (lunotriquetral- capitate- hamate) fusion is indicated when there is significant periscaphoid arthritis in the presence of a radiolunate joint with intact cartilage. Rarely, for patients without radioscaphoid arthritis (e.g., midcarpal instability or isolated midcarpal arthritis), a midcarpal fusion may be done while preserving the scaphoid. Scaphoidectomy and lunocapitate (LC) fusion is the author’s preferred solution to manage stage II or III SLAC or SNAC wrist, unless there is concomitant arthritis at the ulnar midcarpal joint. Advantages include a shorter operating time and preservation of the relatively normal ulnar component of the midcarpal joint, while adequately preventing carpal collapse after scaphoid excision. Sparing the ulnar midcarpal joint preserves the proprioceptive function of triquetrum and the normal “helicoidal” TH kinematics (see Chapter 13). If the triquetrum is included in the fusion process, TH motion will be eliminated, which may lead to the development of ulnocarpal impingement in ulnar deviation and abnormal ulnocarpal gapping in radial deviation.Disadvantages of an isolated central column fusion is its historically higher nonunion rate due to the limited bony fusion surface. However, Slade and Bomback demonstrated a high union and satisfaction rate with the arthroscopic-assisted LC fusion, which the authors attributed to the minimal invasive method, rigid percutaneous fixation, and elimination of potential triquetral hamate impingement.

**Scaphoid excision and four- corner fusion.** Arthroscopic scaphoidectomy is described previously (page 783). When doing a complete or subtotal scaphoid resection, progressively larger arthroscopic bur (up to 4.5 mm) are utilized as space is opened up after sections of the scaphoid are excised (eFig. 17.84). Alternatively, a small osteotome can be used to break the bone into smaller pieces for easier removal. The distal few millimeters of the scaphoid can be left in situ to preserve the scaphotrapezial ligament. The distal scaphoid tubercle does not normally articulate with the radial styloid and hence its preservation will not normally cause impingement pain postoperatively.

![[eFig.17.84.png]]
> **eFig. 17.84** Use of a large 4.5-mm arthroscopic bur helps to speed up the burring process.

Once the scaphoid is excised, the arthroscope is inserted through the MCR portal and the MCU portal is utilized for bone preparation. The articular surfaces between the capitate, lunate, triquetrum, and hamate are denuded with a 2.9 mm bur to bleeding bone. The joint surface of the LT joint is also burred. The capitate- hamate joint requires preparation of only the proximal few millimeters, as the joint is normally rigid.

Provisional fixation is then performed using an image intensifier. If there is significant ulnar translocation and/or DISI deformity of the lunate, accompanied by radial subluxation of the capitate, the lunate must be reduced by flexion and radial translation of the wrist so as to restore the normal radiolunate relationship. The aim is to have at least half of the lunate sitting over the distal radius. The lunate is then fixed to the radius with a percutaneous 1.6 mm Kirschner wire inserted from the distal radius (Linscheid maneuver). The capitate is then reduced by ulnar translation of the hand so that it is returned to its anatomic position on the distal lunate articular surface for optimal bone contact (eFig. 17.85). Overcorrection of the capitate in the coronal plane is not advised. A percutaneous Kirschner wire is inserted from the dorsal surface of the capitate at its third metacarpal carpometacarpal (CMC) joint under fluoroscopic guidance in both PA and lateral planes (eFig. 17.86). A mini–stab wound and blunt dissection should be used to avoid iatrogenic injury to the extensor tendons. The Kirschner wire is driven across the LC joint to anchor into the lunate. The angle of inclination has to be sufficiently obtuse to enter the central part of the lunate in order to have a better bony purchase. It is crucial to obtain good surface contact, as the LC joint is the keystone of the four- corner arthrodesis, and fusion is essential to avoid late collapse of the midcarpal joint and loss of carpal height. When satisfactory alignment is achieved, the Kirschner wire is withdrawn slightly from lunate and left in the capitate. Bone graft impaction is performed with the arthroscope in the MCR portal, and the bone graft (or substitute) is inserted through a cannula in the MCU portal. During the procedure, it is helpful to insert a French size 6 urinary catheter via the 3- 4 portal to completely obliterate the empty space left after the scaphoidectomy procedure (eFig. 17.87). The balloon may be left inflated while bone graft or substitute is being delivered to the fusion site.

![[eFig.17.85.png]]
> **eFig. 17.85** The capitate is reduced by ulnar translation of the wrist so that it sits as much as possible on the distal lunate articular surface.

![[eFig.17.86.png]]
> **eFig. 17.86** Guide pin inserted through distal capitate to transfix capitolunate joint.

![[eFig.17.87.png]]
> **eFig. 17.87** A French size 6 Foley catheter is inserted via the 3- 4 portal to completely obliterate the empty space left after the scaphoidectomy procedure.

After completion of the bone grafting, the Kirschner wire in the capitate is driven back into the reduced lunate. Length of the Kirschner wire is measured. This is followed by drilling of the bones with a cannulated drill bit and the insertion of a headless self- tapping cannulated screw to fix the LC joint. The screw tip should extend no more than 2 mm from the proximal subchondral bone of the lunate to avoid screw tip protrusion and iatrogenic damage to the radiolunate articulation. The screw should be completely buried in the capitate so that it will not cause impingement to the extensor tendons. The LT and TH joints are then fixed with percutaneous Kirschner wires, followed by cannulated screws through small stab incisions (eFig. 17.88). Structures at risk include the dorsal branches of ulnar nerve, EDM, and ECU tendons.

![[eFig.17.88.png]]
> **eFig. 17.88** Fusion of the four-corners bones with percutaneous headless screws.

After rigid fixation of the carpal bones, the radiolunate Kirschner wire is left in situ for 2 weeks. The urinary catheter can be removed after the bone grafting procedure or can be left in situ for a day or two to serve as a surgical drain. Wounds are approximated with SteriStrips (eFig. 17.89), and a comfortable bulky dressing is applied and supported by a short- arm plaster slab. The plaster slab is changed to a removable wrist splint after the first week. After removal of the Kirschner wire over the radiolunate joint, active mobilization of the wrist can be initiated out of the splint under the supervision of a hand therapist. Passive wrist mobilization and strengthening exercise is begun when radiologic and clinical union is evidenced, usually around 10 to 12 weeks postoperative (Figs. 17.62 and 17.63; eFig. 17.90).

![[eFig.17.89.png]]
> **eFig. 17.89** Arthroscopic wounds are closed with Steri-Strips without stitch.

![[Fig.17.62.png]]
> **Fig. 17.62** A, A 47-year-old manual worker with SLAC wrist stage III as shown in the preoperative radiographs. B, Radiographs 3 months following arthroscopic scaphoidectomy and four-corner fusion and showed solid fusion.

![[Fig.17.63.png]]
> **Fig. 17.63** Final follow-up at 73 months postoperative; wrist range of motion similar to preoperative (42% versus 44% of opposite wrist), full wrist function score, no pain, grip power increased from 55.4% to 75.7% of opposite side. Scar was invisible.

![[eFig.17.90.png]]
> **eFig. 17.90** Postoperative scar condition and range of motion of wrist at 3 months.

**Scaphoid excision and lunocapitate fusion.** Arthroscopic scaphoidectomy is performed as described before. For fusing the LC joint, the arthroscope is inserted through MCR portal while the MCU portal is reserved for arthroscopic instruments. The articular surface between the lunate and the capitate is carefully denuded to bleeding subchondral bone with 2.9 mm bur, while those of the triquetrum and hamate are protected and preserved. It is important to preserve the subchondral plates to maintain the height of the central column so as to avoid subsequent TH impingement of the medial column. For a type I lunate, the whole distal articulating surface of the lunate should be debrided. For type II lunate, the typical small ulnar facet should not be debrided as it is not involved in the fusion. If the ulnar facet is of considerable size and proportion, one may consider switching to a three- bone fusion between the lunate, capitate, and hamate. If there is significant ulnar translocation and/or DISI deformity of the lunate and radial subluxation of the capitate, the lunate should be reduced and fixed to the radius temporarily with the Linscheid maneuver as described above.

The wrist is taken off from the wrist tower. A guide wire is placed from the capitate to the lunate as above, aiming at the central part of the lunate using a small metal awl to establish the entry point on the dorsoulnar capitate (Fig. 17.64). A second wire should be placed to the radial side of the capitate- metacarpal joint. The guide wires should cross and aim ideally to catch the junction between the dorsal and middle third of the lunate at the LC joint. The slightly different angle of the two guide pins can avoid crowding of the screws upon definitive fixation.

![[Fig.17.64.png]]
> **Fig. 17.64** A small metal awl inserted through a stab wound at the ulnar border of the base of the third metacarpal and the capitate to establish an entry point for the guide pin.

With the two pins in the capitate, the capitate is manually reduced to the lunate by ulnar translation of the hand (Fig. 17.65) so that it sits in its anatomic position on the distal lunate articular surface. The pins are then driven through the lunate until they reach the subchondral surfaces (Fig. 17.66). There is no need for bone grafting as the articular congruency between the two bones is typically excellent. Conversion of the Kirschner wire fixation with cannulated screws is then performed as described above. Lateral and oblique fluoroscopic views are taken to confirm that no screw projects beyond the proximal articular surface of the lunate. The stability and range of motion of the wrist can be checked under fluoroscopy. Passive finger movement should be checked to confirm no impingement by the screws. Postoperative management is similar to four- corner fusion (Figs. 17.67 and 17.68).

![[Fig.17.65.png]]
> **Fig. 17.65** The wrist is manually ulnar translated to reduce the capitate to its anatomic position on the distal articular surface of the lunate.

![[Fig.17.66.png]]
> **Fig. 17.66** Two pins are inserted percutaneously under image intensifier to transfix the capitolunate joint. A cannulated drill is inserted over the percutaneous pin to prepare the tracks for the cannulated screw.

![[Fig.17.67.png]]
> **Fig. 17.67** A 55-year-old man with SLAC wrist stage III underwent arthroscopic scaphoidectomy and capitolunate fusion. X-ray at 5 years 6 months postoperative showed solid fusion and good carpal alignment.

![[Fig.17.68.png]]
> **Fig. 17.68** Clinically the patient had an excellent arc of flexion-extension and radioulnar deviation, with 95% grip strength compared with his opposite side. Operative scars were inconspicuous.

**Radioscapholunate fusion.** Radioscapholunate fusion is typically indicated for severe painful posttraumatic arthritis involving the whole radiocarpal joint while the midcarpal joint is relatively preserved.It has been shown that an accompanying distal scaphoidectomy procedure can help improve midcarpal motion especially on ulnar radial deviation. This can also be accomplished arthroscopically.

With the arthroscope in the 3- 4 portal, a 2.9 mm bur is inserted into the 4- 5 portal and both the lunate fossa and the proximal surface of the lunate are debrided of articular cartilage. The portals are then switched so that the bur is introduced from the 3- 4 portal to have a better clearance of the articular cartilage of the proximal scaphoid and the scaphoid fossa including the radial styloid area. After completion of the burring process, percutaneous Kirschner wires are inserted from the distal radius to transfix the radiolunate and radioscaphoid joints (eFig. 17.91). With the wrist in neutral flexion- extension and radioulnar deviation, two 1.1 mm Kirschner wires are inserted using a protective sheath from the distal radius to fix the lunate. Another incision is made over the radial styloid at the bare area between the first and second extensor compartment. After careful blunt dissection of the superficial branches of the radial nerve, two Kirschner wires are inserted in sequence to transfix the distal radius to the scaphoid. The range of motion of the wrist can then be checked on table. If there is insufficient radial- ulnar deviation noted, an arthroscopic distal scaphoidectomy can be performed at the midcarpal joint (see section Arthroscopic Distal Scaphoidectomy). After verification of the wire position, they can be backed out from the carpal bones and left in place in the distal radius for the ABG.

![[eFig.17.91.png]]
> **eFig. 17.91** A, Arthroscope is in the 3- 4 portal during radioscapholunate fusion. B, Kirschner wires are positioned in the scaphoid and lunate.

With arthroscope introduced at the 4- 5 portal, an arthroscopic cannula is inserted through the 3- 4 portal to reach the radial side of the scaphoid fossa. Autogenous bone graft or bone substitute can be inserted to fill the radial side of the radioscaphoid joint (eFig. 17.92). To prevent grafting spilling into the ulnocarpal compartment, a size 6 urinary catheter is inserted through the 6R portal and is inflated with saline to obliterate the space there. When the grafting procedure is complete, the Kirschner wires are driven back into the carpal bones just short of the articulating surface of the midcarpal joint. These can be converted into cannulated compression screws (Fig. 17.69). In osteopenic bone where screw purchase can be suboptimal, the four Kirschner wires can serve as the definitive mean of fixation. They are cut short and buried beneath the skin.

![[eFig.17.92.png]]
> **eFig. 17.92** Fine granules of artificial bone substitute are inserted into the radiocarpal joint space through a cannula before the definitive bony fixation.

![[Fig.17.69.png]]
> **Fig. 17.69** Solid union of radioscapholunate fusion site at 92 months postoperative. Surgical scar was minimal.

**Radiolunate fusion.** Radiolunate fusion is most commonly utilized in rheumatoid arthritis where there is painful ulnar translocation of the carpus at the radiocarpal joint. In posttraumatic situations, it is indicated when the articular cartilage destruction is confined to the radiolunate joint, such as might occur following an incompletely reduced die- punch fracture of the lunate facet (Fig. 17.70).

![[Fig.17.70.png]]
> **Fig. 17.70** A 53-year-old nonrheumatoid woman developed severe isolated radiolunate arthrosis without history of trauma.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Scaphoidectomy + Four- Corner Fusion/Lunocapitate Fusion

Indications
- Stage II and III SLAC and SNAC wrist
- Type II lunate with a signiﬁcant hamate facet may not be suitable for LC fusion. Three- corner fusion including the hamate bone should be considered instead.

Preoperative Evaluation
- X- ray and MRI, correlated with clinical symptoms and signs
- Can consider a diagnostic wrist arthroscopy under PSLA for staging before treatment

Pearls
- Operative time is longer than open surgery especially for a four- corner fusion. To avoid compartment syndrome, do not use pressure pump for saline irriga tion. To prevent tourniquet palsy, inﬂate tourniquet judiciously and only when necessary.
- Complete scaphoidectomy is not necessary and time consuming. Distal one ﬁfth of scaphoid can be left intact. Concomitant radial styloidectomy is usu ally not required.
- Remove the scaphoid ﬁrst before fusing the four corner bones to facilitate reduction of DISI alignment.
- Use progressively increasing size of burs from 2.9 mm to up to 4.5 mm to facilitate excision.
- Bur the bone but keep a shell of cartilage intact and remove bone piecemeal by using a small pituitary rongeur.
- Preserve extrinsic ligaments.
- Complete excision of cartilage is necessary down to bleeding subchondral bone plate.
- Correct DISI deformity by Linscheid maneuver.
- Use cannulated screws for good quality bones and multiple Kirschner wires for osteopenic bones.

Technical Points
- 1:200,000 adrenaline in portal sites can reduce bleeding when performing surgery without tourniquet.
- Use reversed cutting mode in burring scaphoid.
- Avoid jumping phenomenon when using the bur by employing ﬁnger pivoting technique to control the bur tip motion.
- When removing cartilage shell fragments, grasp the fragment with pituitary rongeur or arthroscopy punch tightly, twist, and rotate axially instead of force fully avulsing to detach the fragment from the adjacent capsule.
- Use intraoperative ﬂuoroscopy to monitor the progress of scaphoidectomy.
- When keeping the distal one- ﬁfth of scaphoid, check the passive movement of the wrist to avoid impingement.
```

The operation is similar to radioscapholunate fusion, except that the radioscaphoid joint is spared. In addition, distal scaphoidectomy is not necessary (Fig. 17.71). Thus during the burring procedure, the articular surface of the proximal scaphoid and scaphoid fossa should be well protected. Also during the graft insertion procedure, a second urinary catheter can be inserted at the 1- 2 portal to obliterate the space at the radioscaphoid articulation and isolate the graft placement to the RL joint (Fig. 17.72). The arthroscope is placed in the 3- 4 portal while bone substitute is delivered to the radiolunate joint through a cannula at the 4- 5 portal. The fixation can be accomplished by two Kirschner

![[Fig.17.71.png]]
> **Fig. 17.71** Operative fluoroscopy showed good position of the guide pins across the radiolunate joint. As shown at arthroscopy of the radiolunate joint and without tourniquet on, burring of proximal lunate and lunate fossa revealed good subchondral punctate bleeding.

![[Fig.17.72.png]]
> **Fig. 17.72** Arthroscopic view showing position of a Foley catheter at the scaphoid fossa and the granular form of bone substitute at the radiolunate joint space.

For the four- corner fusion, denude completely the cartilage of LC, HT, and LT joints, and the proximal aspect of CH joint using 2.9 mm bur; for LC fusion, denude just the cartilage of LC joint. Reduce ulnar translocation and DISI deformity of the lunate and pin to radius with 1.6 mm Kirschner wire into the ulnar half of the lunate to avoid interference with the LC pin/screw. Reduce the radial translation of the capitate manually by translating the hand ulnarly after pinning the RL joint, and then pin the LC joint in the normal LC alignment with an entry point of the pin at base of the third metacarpal. For the four- corner fusion, LC pin is aimed at the center of the lunate. Angle of the LC pin has to be acute enough to catch the central part of the LC joint when passing through. For the LC fusion, position the 2 LC pins on each side of the third metacarpal base. The angles of insertion should be slightly different: one aims at midpoint of LC joint and the other slightly dorsal on the lateral view. For the four- corner fusion, graft is needed to ﬁll the void at the junction of the four corners. Use cancellous bone graft from the iliac crest or bone substitutes inserted through the MCU portal. Use a pediatric size 6 urinary catheter and inﬂate the balloon to ﬁll the scaphoidectomy site through the 3- 4 portal to avoid graft spillage. For LC fusion, bone graft is not generally necessary. Manually compress the LC joint when applying cannulated screw, and sub tract 4 mm from the measured length of the screw to help avoid iatrogenic screw protrusion at RL joint. Check balloon integrity of the urinary catheter after being removed from the joint.

Inadequate resection of the scaphoid may lead to impingement. Inadequate correction of a DISI deformity may lead to suboptimal healing and loss of ﬂexion. An incorrectly sized LC screw length may protrude into the RL joint and lead to progressive arthritis. Inadequate countersinking of the screws may lead to impingement of the extensor tendons and cutaneous nerves. Prolonged surgery may lead to tourniquet palsy or ﬂuid extravasation and compartment syndrome.

Compressive bandage and elevation for 2 days With stable cannulated screw ﬁxation, start early active wrist mobilization at week 2 to 3 when swelling subsides and RL pin is removed. For Kirschner wire ﬁxation cases, motion exercise commences when healing is evidenced in x- ray or CT scan, typically around 8 to 10 weeks. Early mobilization of the digits is begun immediately postoperative to avoid tendon adhesion wires or two compression cannulated screws inserted percutaneously from the distal radius as described above (Fig. 17.73). In a patient with significant ulnar positive variance, an accompanying arthroscopic wafer procedure or ulnar shortening osteotomy may be performed to unload the ulnocarpal joint as well as to avoid potential ulnocarpal impaction after the radiolunate fusion. Note: It is critical in this procedure to maintain lunate height by filling the prepared RL surface with bone graft or graft substitute; otherwise, an articular step- off will develop at the midcarpal joint that may lead to arthritis. It may also produce overloading of the radioscaphoid joint.

![[Fig.17.73.png]]
> **Fig. 17.73** Final definitive fixation of the radiolunate joint with two percutaneous cannulated screws and well-contained bone substitute at the fusion site. Noted that both the radioscaphoid and the ulnocarpal joint were free of bone substitute due to blockage by a Foley catheter. Solid bone union was shown at 11 years 2 months postoperative with no scar seen on the wrist.

**Scaphocapitate fusion.** Scaphocapitate (SC) fusion is indicated in advanced Kienböck disease as a salvage solution. It is best indicated when the lunate has collapsed and the articular surfaces are fragmented, such as in a Lichtman stage IIIA or IIIB situation. It is commonly combined with resection of the necrotic lunate.

Arthroscopic lunate excision is performed from the midcarpal joint. With the arthroscope introduced from MCR portal, arthroscopic burs of increasing size from 2.9 mm to up to 4.5 mm are inserted into the MCU portal and directed toward the distal surface of the lunate. The lunate is burred at high speed from articular surface down to the core cancellous bone. To avoid accidental damage of the adjacent articular surfaces, a shell of cartilage can be left intact until a majority of the cancellous bone is removed. This shell of cartilage can be removed piecemeal at the end of the procedure by using a small pituitary rongeur or an arthroscopic punch.

Fusion of the SC joint is carried out. The arthroscope is inserted through MCU portal while the MCR portal is reserved for arthroscopic instruments. The articular surface between the scaphoid and capitate is denuded of cartilage with a 2.9 mm bur. A small stab wound is made at the anatomic snuffbox area until the bony cortex of scaphoid is reached. Two guide wires with adequate space in between are inserted and driven across the scaphoid toward the capitate. To avoid injury to the radial artery and superficial branches of the radial nerve, the guide pins can be inserted through a metal trocar of a 14- gauge angiocatheter serving as a guide. In SC fusion, the articular congruency achieved between scaphoid and capitate is typically good. The use of bone graft is optional if the lunate has been excised; otherwise, intercarpal distancing should be preserved. Conversion of the Kirschner wires to cannulated screw fixation is then performed as previously described (Fig. 17.74).

![[Fig.17.74.png]]
> **Fig. 17.74** A 22-year-old patient with Lichtman stage IIIB Kienböck disease on his right wrist underwent arthroscopic lunate excision and scaphocapitate fusion with two headless cannulated screws augmented with injectable demineralized bone matrix at the fusion interface. Solid fusion and excellent esthetic result was seen at 11 months postoperative.

**Lunotriquetral fusion.** LT fusion is indicated for symptomatic chronic LT instability with or without ulnocarpal impaction syndrome. In the latter condition, ulnar shortening osteotomy is often performed to decompress the ulnocarpal joint. In the absence of secondary chondral damage, an alternative option of LT ligament reconstruction can be considered, since failure to unite is common in surgical fusion of the LT joint. In a metaanalysis of the outcome of intercarpal arthrodesis conducted by Siegel and Ruby the overall reported nonunion rate of open LT fusion in seven clinical series was 26%, while 46% of patients had persistent postoperative symptoms. Sennwald et al. also identified nonunion in 13 of 23 patients (57%) following attempted open LT fusion.

Arthroscopic fusion of the LT joint can be performed through the midcarpal portals. Routine radiocarpal joint arthroscopy is performed to evaluate the status of the TFCC, associated chondral lesions of the ulnar head, proximal lunate and triquetrum, and the UT ligament. If a significant chondral lesion is present at the carpal bone surface, ulnar shortening or arthroscopic wafer procedure can be used as an alternative to or in conjunction with an LT fusion.

The arthroscope is then inserted in the MCR portal and a small probe is inserted at the MCU portal to reach the LT joint to confirm instability. This is then replaced by a 2.9 mm bur and debridement of the articular cartilage is performed from distal to proximal direction. Attention should be paid to the dorsal aspect of the joint by rotating the 30 degrees forward, slanting the lens to a downward position. Difficulty may be encountered in burring this portion as the angle of the bur is frequently restricted by the dorsal soft tissue of the wrist. A smallangled curette may be found useful in removing cartilage in this area.

When burring is complete, the joint gap is filled with bone graft or bone substitute through a canula inserted at the MCU portal. The former is preferred as nonunion of the fusion site is notoriously common. This is then followed by percutaneous fixation with a compression cannulated screw inserted from the ulnar aspect of the hand. A small incision is made opposing the ulnar surface of the triquetrum and bluntly dissected to free the overlying DSBUN. Under image guidance, a guide wire is inserted from the triquetrum across the LT joint to reach the lunate bone. Length of the guide wire is measured. After further advancing the wire to avoid inadvertent extraction during drill or screw placement, a cannulated drill bit is applied and a compression headless cannulated screw is inserted to compress the fusion site (Fig. 17.75). Preferably two screws should be inserted to obtain maximal stability of the fusion site. Alternatively, a Kirschner wire can be used to augment the strength of a single screw if the bone is too small to accommodate two screws. All wounds are closed with Steri-Strips, and a short- arm plaster is applied to protect the fusion site until radiologic union or stable fibrous union is achieved.

![[Fig.17.75.png]]
> **Fig. 17.75** Arthroscopic view of the screw in the lunotriquetral joint, which was eventually covered with arthroscopic bone graft. X-ray showed good fusion position.

#### Outcome and Complications

In 2008, Ho first reported the technique and outcomes of arthroscopic partial wrist fusion in 12 patients with an average follow- up of 70 months. Procedures included STT fusion (3), scaphoid excision and four- corner fusion (4), radioscapholunate fusion (3), radiolunate fusion (1), and LT fusion (1). All cases were grafted with autogenous bone or bone graft substitute, and fixation was performed using Kirschner wires or cannulated screws. Nine of the 12 cases united, and complications included pin tract infection, skin burn, and delayed union. All patients had subjective improvement, excellent esthetic outcomes, and return of functional motion.

Subsequently, del Piñal et al. reported the use of dry arthroscopy to perform four cases of arthroscopic scaphoidectomy and four- corner fusion. The authors recommended a miniopen “scapholunate” portal, just distal to the 3- 4 portal, to resect the scaphoid with a rongeur in a dry environment to save time. The excised scaphoid was utilized for autogenous bone graft in two and the distal radius in two. Following lunate reduction to neutral and temporary pinning, the bone surfaces are prepped and grafted through the 6R and MCU portals. Three cannulated screws are inserted between the LC, LT, and triquetrocapitate articulations, and the procedures were completed within 2 hours. Patients were splinted until healing, and all four cases successfully united. Baur reported the results of arthroscopic partial wrist fusion of various types under a mixed wet and dry arthroscopy in 39 patients from 2011 to 2016. The author also compared 13 open scaphoid excision and four- corner fusion procedures with 12 arthroscopic procedures. For the arthroscopic procedure, she recommended a miniopen approach through a zigzag incision at the scaphoid tubercle to excise the scaphoid.

STT ligaments were released and a “carpal stick” (KLS Martin, Cardiff, UK) was helpful to remove the scaphoid. The excised scaphoid was used for autogenous bone graft. The overall revision rate was 20%, and the partial nonunion rate was 10%. Two patients were lost to follow- up while all other cases healed with satisfactory results. Both cohorts of 4CF had the same results for pain, function, and satisfaction.

### TFCC Reconstruction with Tendon Graft

The most important stabilizers of the DRUJ during pronosupination are the palmar and dorsal radioulnar ligaments. Damage to either or both of these ligaments generates pain and instability during forearm rotation. A biomechanical analysis by Adams et al. concluded that traumatic disruptions of the distal radioulnar ligaments from the fovea occurred predominantly from tension, but they proposed that a combination of forces was required to destabilize the joint. The authors proposed an anatomic reconstruction of the distal radioulnar ligaments using a palmaris longus tendon graft as a solution for this difficult condition and reported good results in 12 patients with posttraumatic DRUJ instability at 26 months’ follow- up evaluation.

This method aims to restore normal DRUJ kinematics by using a single tendon graft with uniform tension, which is passed through the edges of the sigmoid notch and through the ulna at the foveal insertion site (see Chapter 14). Based on this method, Ho et al. published results of their arthroscopic modification of this technique. In this minimally invasive technique, open capsulotomy and capsular dissection are not necessary, minimizing soft tissue scarring, fibrosis, and stiffness and allowing earlier rehabilitation. TFCC reconstruction with tendon graft is indicated in irreparable TFCC injuries with symptomatic DRUJ instability, neglected chronic injuries, or after suboptimal healing following nonoperative or surgical repair. Skeletal malalignment that may be responsible for the DRUJ instability should be addressed concomitantly, including ulnar positive variance, malunion of the sigmoid notch causing DRUJ incongruency, and dorsal tilt or coronal shift of the radius. Osteoarthritis of the DRUJ and an axial instability of the forearm due to interosseous membrane injury are contraindications to TFCC reconstruction.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC TFCC RECONSTRUCTION WITH TENDON GRAFT

The surgical approach and the anatomic structures at risk are illustrated (eFig. 17.93). The procedure is performed under general or regional anesthesia with the aid of an image intensifier to locate the isometric point for tunnel drilling. An arm tourniquet is placed. Radiocarpal joint arthroscopy through the standard 3- 4 viewing and 4- 5 working portals is conducted to evaluate and debride the TFCC lesion and associated synovitis. If the central part of the articular disk is intact, a central perforation of 4- 5 mm is created with an arthroscopic knife or preferably RF ablation to expose the ulnar head (eFig. 17.94). The wrist is then taken off the traction tower for subsequent tendon graft and bone tunnel preparation.

![[eFig.17.93.png]]
> **eFig. 17.93** Surgical approach and structures at risk. A, The dorsal incision is made between the common finger extensors and the extensor digiti minimi tendon. B, The volar incision is between the digital flexor tendons and the ulnar neurovascular bundle. The exit point of the distal radius bone tunnel is usually located just distal to the distal border of the pronator quadratus muscle.
