C, The ulnar incision is made at the subcutaneous border of the distal ulna 1.5 to 2 cm proximal to the tip of ulnar styloid. The ulnar tunnel should exit at the fovea of the ulnar head. The dorsal cutaneous branch of the ulnar nerve is at risk.

![[eFig.17.94.png]]
> **eFig. 17.94** If the central part of the TFCC is intact, a central perforation of 4- 5 mm is created with an arthroscopic knife or preferably radiofrequency ablation to expose the ulnar head.

The limb is exsanguinated and the tourniquet inflated. A 2 cm volar longitudinal incision is made midway between palmaris longus (PL) tendon and radial border of the FCU tendon extending proximally from the proximal wrist crease (Fig. 17.76). The PL tendon is harvested in full length with a tendon stripper. The flexor tendons and median nerve are retracted radially, and the FCU, ulnar nerve, and artery are retracted ulnarly to expose the distal radius. The distal border of the pronator quadratus marks the approximate level of the radial tunnel.

![[Fig.17.76.png]]
> **Fig. 17.76** The typical planning of the three incisions over dorsal, ulnar, and volar aspect of the wrist.

The second incision is a 2 cm dorsal longitudinal incision extending proximally from the 4- 5 portal. The extensor retinaculum is split longitudinally over the fifth dorsal compartment. The EDM is exposed and retracted with a cotton sling. The septum of the fourth extensor compartment is stripped subperiosteally to expose the edge of the sigmoid notch. A 1.1 mm guide pin is inserted on the dorsal surface of the distal radius radial to the sigmoid notch, 5 mm proximal to the articular surface of lunate fossa and 5 mm radial to the sigmoid fossa with a volar tilt of 10 to 15 degrees (parallel to the surface of the lunate fossa). Tendons and neurovascular structures on the volar side are gently retracted while the guide pin is advanced with direct visualization of the tip (Fig. 17.77). After guide pin position is confirmed by fluoroscopy, the tunnel is enlarged from 2.0 to 2.5 mm using cannulated drill bits and soft tissue protection at both entry and exit sites.

![[Fig.17.77.png]]
> **Fig. 17.77** Sigmoid notch bone tunnel creation. Drill hole should be made 5 mm proximal to the articular surface of lunate fossa and 5 mm radial to the sigmoid fossa with a volar tilt of 10 to 15 degrees parallel to the surface of the lunate fossa.

The third incision is a 3 cm longitudinal incision made over the subcutaneous border of ulna beginning 1 cm proximal to the tip of ulnar styloid. A 1.1 mm guide pin is inserted at the middiaphyseal point from 1.5 to 2 cm proximal to the tip of ulnar styloid at an acute angle of about 60 degrees targeting the fovea of the ulnar head and parallel to the ulna shaft in the sagittal plane under fluoroscopic guidance (Fig. 17.78). This can be facilitated by the surgeon placing their thumb on the ulnar styloid as a reference. Once the entry point is decided, the wrist can be suspended in a vertical position with the forearm in neutral rotation position, and the foveal exit site of the guide pin is monitored under arthroscopy to ensure its exit at the isometric point of the insertion of the distal radioulnar ligaments, with further adjustment if necessary (Fig. 17.79). An eccentric bone tunnel may result in loss of rotational motion due to unequal tension of the two limbs of the graft (eFig. 17.95). The ulnar bone tunnel is created by stepwise drilling to 2.9 mm or 3.2 mm under arthroscopic control; a larger size than the radial tunnel is required since two limbs of the tendon graft will pass through the tunnel. The tip of the guide pin is grasped within the joint with a hemostat passing through the 4- 5 portal during drilling to avoid iatrogenic damage of the articular cartilage of lunate or triquetrum during drilling (Video 17.38).

![[Fig.17.78.png]]
> **Fig. 17.78** Freehand technique of ulnar tunnel creation: A 1.1 mm guide pin is inserted at midulnar shaft from 1.5 to 2 cm proximal to the tip of ulnar styloid at an acute angle of about 60 degrees targeting at the fovea of the ulnar head, parallel to the ulna shaft in the sagittal plane under fluoroscopic guidance. This can be facilitated by placing the thumb on the ulnar styloid as a reference to gauge position.

![[Fig.17.79.png]]
> **Fig. 17.79** Ulnar bone tunnel aiming at the fovea using a freehand technique. The tip of the guide pin is grasped within the joint with a hemostat passing through the 4- 5 portal to avoid iatrogenic damage of the articular cartilage of the lunate or triquetrum during drilling.

![[eFig.17.95.png]]
> **eFig. 17.95** MRI and x-ray showing the typical trajectory of the ulnar and distal radius bone tunnels.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic TFCC Reconstruction With Tendon Graft

Indications
- Symptomatic DRUJ instability after neglected chronic TFCC injury, massive nonrepairable tear, or failed previous surgical repair
- Absence of any signiﬁcant skeletal malalignment, DRUJ arthritis, or Essex Lopresti lesion

Preoperative Evaluation
- Clinical symptoms and signs of DRUJ instability
- Radiographs to check ulnar variance and forearm alignment
- Diagnostic arthroscopy or high- resolution MRI to evaluate the potential for TFCC repair

Pearls
- Good lighting and loupe magniﬁcation are essential. An assistant is necessary to protect the volar- sided neurovascular structures and tendons.
- Use a longitudinal incision for the 4- 5 portal and extend it for the open dissection.
- If intact, create a central perforation of the articular disk with an RF probe.
- Protect the EDM with a cotton sling.
- Keep the radial tunnel size under 2.5 mm and ulnar tunnel under 3.5 mm to reduce the risk of fracture and promote ingrowth.
- Keep the radial tunnel 5 mm away from the lunate sigmoid fossae to avoid fracture.
- In creating the ulnar tunnel, keep the forearm in neutral rotation while insert ing Kirschner wire at the middiaphyseal point and parallel to the ulnar shaft, targeting the fovea.
- Using a 2 mm minigrasper to deliver the tendon graft through the ulnar bone tunnel.
- The length of the PL graft may not be long enough to wrap around the ulnar neck. May use a transverse osseous tunnel or interference screw to secure the tendon graft instead.

Technical Points
- Synovitis in the ulnocarpal joint should be cleared and the perforation in the articular disk should be wide enough to allow direct observation of the fovea.
```

A 2 mm arthroscopic grasper is inserted through the bone tunnel in the radius from dorsal to volar, grasping the tendon graft and passing it through the tunnel from volar to dorsal (eFig. 17.96). A volar capsular window at the interval between the UL and SRL ligaments is created bluntly using the trocar of a 2.7 mm scope introduced from the 4- 5 portal (Fig. 17.80). Through this window, the volar limb of the tendon graft is pulled into the joint by a 2 mm arthroscopic grasper introduced from the 4- 5 portal. This tendon graft is then grasped and pulled through the ulnar tunnel to the exterior by another 2 mm grasper introduced through the ulnar tunnel exiting the fovea.

![[eFig.17.96.png]]
> **eFig. 17.96** Mosquito grasper used to deliver the tendon graft across the wrist through the sigmoid notch tunnel.

![[Fig.17.80.png]]
> **Fig. 17.80** At the 4- 5 portal, the surgeon passes a 2-mm mosquito grasper volarly through a window between the short radiolunate and ulnocarpal ligaments to exit the joint. The volar limb of the tendon graft is pulled into the joint while the finger flexor tendons and the ulnar neurovascular bundle are carefully retracted to prevent encirclement by the tendon graft as it is delivered back into the joint.

The dorsal limb of the tendon graft is braided with suture at its end. It is then grasped and pushed into the joint through the 4- 5 portal with a smooth forceps or grasper deep to the extensor tendons. It is then pulled through the ulnar bone tunnel using a grasper inserted from the exterior (Fig. 17.81; Video 17.39).

![[Fig.17.81.png]]
> **Fig. 17.81** Tendon graft inside the wrist being grasped by a 2-mm mosquito grasper inserted into the joint through the ulnar tunnel, to be delivered outside the ulna.

The radial tunnel should exit volarly just distal to the pronator quadratus muscle. Rotate the forearm to allow the Kirschner wire tip in the fovea to emerge through the articular disk perforation. Protect the carpal bones when using the cannulated drill to enlarge the ulnar tunnel. When passing the graft back into the joint, make sure it does not catch or loop a tendon or nerve. Deliver the two ends of the tendon graft either separately through the ulnar tunnel with the minigrasper or simultaneously with tie- and- pull method after braiding the ends together. Use a probe to guide the passage of the tendon grafts through the ulnar tun nel to avoid binding of the tendon. Tension and knot the graft with the forearm in neutral rotation. Check the stability of the DRUJ and pronosupination at the end of procedure.

Fracture of the sigmoid notch or lunate facet if the radial tunnel is too close to the joint line. Fracture of the ulnar styloid if the ulnar tunnel is too wide or too distal. Breakage of a Kirschner wire in creating the tunnels if mishandling the motor hand- piece Nonfoveal placement of the ulnar tunnel leads to loss of rotation motion. Tendon graft encirclement of a tendon or nerve A narrow ulnar tunnel may cause binding of the tendon graft and failure of tensioning.

Reverse sugar tong cast with forearm in neutral rotation is preferred to long arm cast to allow early elbow motion. Change to reverse sugar tong splint after 3 weeks and allow early midrange forearm rotation. Allow full range active forearm rotation after full 6 weeks, start passive motion after full 8 weeks.

Alternatively the volar limb of the tendon graft can be retrieved through the joint dorsally at the 4- 5 portal before the two ends are pulled through the ulnar tunnel simultaneously. The end of the graft is braided to the dorsal limb of the graft, and the suture is introduced back into the joint to be retrieved by a minigrasper inserted through the ulnar tunnel (Video 17.40).

The tendon graft should have a smooth course and uniform tension throughout. The stabilization effect is checked by manually tightening both limbs of the graft and balloting the DRUJ. The forearm rotation motion should not be impeded. Another transverse bone tunnel is made about 1 cm proximal to the oblique tunnel at the distal ulna using a 2.5 mm drill bit. One limb of the graft is passed through the tunnel and then tied securely to the other limb in maximal tension with the forearm in neutral rotation (Fig. 17.82). A 2- 0 nonabsorbable braided suture is used to secure the tendon knot as well as to anchor the graft to the surrounding fascial structures. The wounds are then closed in layers.

![[Fig.17.82.png]]
> **Fig. 17.82** Both limbs of the tendon graft delivered outside the ulna. One limb is passed through a separate transverse osseous tunnel proximal to the ulna head tunnel, to be tied with the other limb under maximal tension.

A reversed sugar tong cast (Muenster cast) is applied to immobilize the forearm in neutral rotation for 3 weeks. A reversed sugartong brace is then utilized for 3 weeks to allow supervised midrange active and passive forearm rotation exercise and free wrist flexion and extension exercise out of splint. Afterward, a below- elbow wrist splint is worn at rest for 2 more weeks and then only at night for another 3 weeks. By the seventh week after operation, mobilization exercise with no range limitation is started. Passive mobilization commences at the ninth week, followed by strengthening exercises.

#### Outcomes and Complications

Mak and Ho reported the outcome on 28 patients at a mean follow- up of 62 months (range, 3 to 138). Sixteen patients had previous surgery on their affected wrists. There was improvement in grip strength, flexion- extension, Mayo wrist score, and VAS pain score in all cases. The mean pronosupination range improved to 91% of the normal side. Radiographic assessment revealed no progressive enlargement of the tunnels or arthrosis of the DRUJ (Fig. 17.83). Complications included transient neurapraxia of the ulnar nerve in 2 patients and hypersensitive ulnar scar in 3 cases. There were 4 cases of graft rupture that presented at 10 to 18 months after the index procedures, two of them due to a new injury. Revision tendon grafting was performed in 2 cases.

![[Fig.17.83.png]]
> **Fig. 17.83** Long-term follow-up showing the radiologic appearance of the bone tunnels 18 years after the surgery. These two patients remained pain-free with stable DRUJ clinically.

Atzei et al. described a similar technique in 2006. The major technical modifications in comparison with our technique are the following: (1) Dry arthroscopy was employed, (2) the end of the palmar limb of the graft was pulled inside the ulnocarpal joint using a grasper inserted from the 6R portal through the interval between the UL and UT ligaments, with the goal of tensioning the ulnocarpal ligaments and hence stabilizing the ulnocarpal joint, and (3) both ends of the graft were fixed inside the ulnar tunnel with a 4- mm interference screw. Luchetti and Atzei reported the long-term results in 11 patients operated between 2005 and 2012. At a mean follow- up of 68 months all but 1 had a stable DRUJ. Pain and grip strength, Mayo wrist score, DASH, and PRWHE scores improved. Complications included a tendon graft rupture and an ulna styloid fracture.

### Combined Dorsal and Volar Scapholunate Ligament Reconstruction With Tendon Graft

Scapholunate (SL) dissociation is the most common chronic carpal instability. Numerous surgical techniques have been described to restore or improve the stability of the SL joint and to retard and prevent the progression to arthritis. These procedures included various forms of capsulodesis, static or dynamic tenodesis, ligament reconstruction employing tendon graft, bone- retinaculum–bone reconstruction and the reduction and association of the scaphoid and lunate (RASL) procedure. Tenodesis and ligament reconstructive techniques that tether the distal and proximal carpal rows impair normal wrist kinematics. Recurrent or persistent gapping, tendon loosening, limited motion and grip strength, fracturing of drill holes, and osteonecrosis have been reported. Furthermore, most methods only provide dorsal or uniplanar reconstructions of a three- dimensional complex (see Chapter 13).

Many studies have emphasized the importance of the volar intrinsic and extrinsic ligaments. It is logical to attempt to restore both the dorsal and volar components of the SL ligament. In one biomechanical study, Yi et al. used a PL tendon to pass through drill holes in the anteroposterior plane of the scaphoid and the lunate. The SL diastasis was effectively reduced to normal, and the scaphoid and lunate contact pressure on the radius and the scaphoid- to- lunate contact ratio were significantly improved after the reconstruction. Zdero et al. used bovine tendons passing through double bone tunnels of the scaphoid and lunate in 19 cadaveric wrists. There were no differences in the mechanical property compared with normal wrists. These cadaveric studies support the clinical application of a combined dorsal and volar SL ligament reconstruction. Dobyns et al. had previously used a tendon graft passed through anteroposterior bone tunnels in the proximal pole of the scaphoid and lunate to reconstruct the SL linkage. Stability was obtained by tightly looping the tendon graft across the scaphoid and lunate. However, creating drill holes across poorly vascularized areas of bone in an open surgical fashion may have compromised their blood supply and fractures of the carpal bones resulted in abandonment of this procedure.

In 2014, Wong and Ho published an arthroscopic-assisted technique to reconstruct both the dorsal and volar SL ligament simultaneously using a free tendon graft in a box- like structure without violating the major blood supply to the scaphoid and soft tissue envelope(Fig. 17.84). The use of arthroscopy obviates the need to open the wrist capsule and helps to preserve vascularity without compromising joint visualization. Wrist arthroscopy allows complete evaluation of the SL joint, status of the volar and dorsal intrinsic and extrinsic ligaments, and reducibility of the SL diastasis and associated SLAC wrist changes. The reconstruction also augments the dorsal and volar stabilizers of the SL joint. The indication is subacute and chronic SL dissociation of greater than 6 weeks. Both dynamic and static instabilities are appropriate, as long as the SL diastasis and any postural deformities of the carpus are reducible, as confirmed arthroscopically and radiologically (eFig. 17.97; Video 17.41).

![[Fig.17.84.png]]
> **Fig. 17.84** Schematic diagram showing the concept of simultaneous reconstruction of the dorsal and palmar scapholunate ligaments anatomically with the use of palmaris longus tendon as tendon graft in a box-like structure. (Redrawn from Ho PC, Wong CWY, Tse WL. Arthroscopic-assisted combined dorsal and volar scapholunate ligament reconstruction with tendon graft for chronic SL instability, J Wrist Surg 4[4]:252–263, 2015.)

![[eFig.17.97.png]]
> **eFig. 17.97** Reduction of the scapholunate (SL) diastasis can be demonstrated radiologically by passive radial deviation of the wrist. Closing of the gap at the SL joint upon wrist maximal radial deviation signifies good reducibility.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC SL LIGAMENT RECONSTRUCTION

The surgery is performed under general anesthesia or regional block in standard setting. Radiocarpal joint arthroscopy is performed initially without a tourniquet through the 3- 4 and 4- 5 portals with the 6U for outflow, followed by midcarpal joint arthroscopy through the MCR and MCU portals. Small transverse incisions are made along the skin creases for better scar appearance and to facilitate wound extension. The joint is inspected systematically and the findings documented. Synovectomy and radial styloidectomy are performed at the same time if necessary using a 2 mm shaver and 2.9 mm bur. Capsular fibrosis is resected with a shaver to improve wrist motion and to facilitate reduction of the SL diastasis and malalignment.

The limb is taken off the traction tower and exsanguinated and the tourniquet is inflated for the reconstructive procedure. A 2 cm transverse incision is centered on the 3- 4 portal incision. The extensor retinaculum is split along its fibers. Radially the interval between the extensor carpi radialis brevis (ECRB) and the ECRL tendons is identified, which usually corresponds well to the ideal position of the scaphoid tunnel (Fig. 17.85). The lunate is exposed by retracting the EDC tendons ulnarward or working between the EDC tendons (Fig. 17.86). Volarly, a transverse incision is made along the proximal wrist crease from the radial border of the PL to the ulnar border of the FCR tendon (Fig. 17.87). The palmar cutaneous branch of the median nerve should be identified and protected with a sling. The PL is harvested using a tendon stripper. The anterior forearm fascia is incised. The interval between the FCR tendon and the finger flexor tendons and median nerve is entered to reach the volar wrist joint capsule and its palmar extrinsic ligaments (eFig. 17.98). Both the volar and dorsal wrist joint capsules are preserved without violation. The wrist is then ready for bone tunnel preparation.

![[Fig.17.85.png]]
> **Fig. 17.85** Scaphoid tunnel landmark. A 2 cm transverse incision is centered on the 3- 4 portal. The interval between the extensor carpi radialis brevis (ECRB) and the extensor carpi radialis longus (ECRL) tendons is identified, which usually corresponds well to the ideal position of the scaphoid tunnel.

![[Fig.17.86.png]]
> **Fig. 17.86** Lunate tunnel landmark: Lunate is exposed by retracting the EDC tendons ulnarward or more commonly working between the EDC tendons.

![[Fig.17.87.png]]
> **Fig. 17.87** Volarly, a transverse incision is made along the proximal wrist crease from the radial border of the palmaris longus to the ulnar border of the flexor carpi radialis tendon. The palmar cutaneous branch of the median nerve needs to be identified and protected with sling.

![[eFig.17.98.png]]
> **eFig. 17.98** The interval between the FCR tendon and the finger flexor tendons and median nerve is entered to reach the volar wrist joint capsule and its palmar extrinsic ligaments. Both the volar and dorsal wrist joint capsules are preserved without violation.

The hand is examined fluoroscopically. If DISI deformity is present, the extended posture of the lunate is corrected by flexing the wrist to restore the normal radiolunate angle, and the radiolunate joint is transfixed with a 1.6 mm Kirschner wire inserted percutaneously through the distal radius. The radiolunate (RL) pin is inserted in the ulnar half of the lunate to avoid interference with the lunate bone tunnel. The wrist is then extended passively to correct the flexion deformity of the scaphoid and to restore a normal SL angle. If the DISI deformity is not correctable by closed means, the arthroscopic SL reconstruction should be abandoned, and an open technique utilized (see Chapter 13). Through the dorsal incision, the EDC tendon interval is opened, and a 1.1 mm guide pin is inserted through a protective sheath under fluoroscopic guidance into the lunate, aiming at the center of the bone. The guide pin should be perpendicular to the long axis of the lunate (i.e., parallel to the line joining the tip of the volar and dorsal lips of the lunate), but sufficiently proximal to the subchondral plate to avoid penetration of the midcarpal joint with the drill. The guide pin is advanced volarly, perforating the volar cortex and exiting through the volar wound, while the flexor tendons, median nerve, and palmar cutaneous branch are carefully retracted and protected by an assistant (Fig. 17.88). Another guide pin is then inserted into the scaphoid through the interval between ECRB and ECRL tendons, with a starting point more distal to the guide pin entry site of the lunate, and directed proximally and volarly, to provide a counterrotational force on the scaphoid and correct the flexion and pronation deformity (Fig. 17.89). The guide pin aims at the proximal scaphoid at least 2 to 3 mm from all the surrounding articular margins to avoid iatrogenic fracture. With the FCR tendon retracted radially, the scaphoid pin exits through the volar wound. The scaphoid and lunate guide pins should form a convergent angle in both sagittal and axial planes. Sequentially expanding the lunate and scaphoid tunnel with cannulated drill bits from 2.0 to 2.5 mm is performed depending on the thickness of the PL graft. The smallest diameter drill hole for the smooth passage of the tendon graft is used to avoid iatrogenic fracture or avascular necrosis. Too small a tunnel may cause binding of the tendon graft inside the tunnel, which can lead to graft attrition or even rupture when being pulled through the tunnels. The free tendon graft is delivered through the bone tunnels with a 2 mm arthroscopic grasper, from the volar side of the scaphoid to dorsal, and from the volar aspect of the lunate to dorsal (eFig. 17.99). The tendon graft is passed outside the capsule to cross the SL interval so that the reconstruction also helps to tighten the capsule and the extrinsic ligaments, which confer added stability to the SL joint (Fig. 17.90). It is important to minimize the potential risk of inducing ischemic necrosis in the carpal bones by vigorously preserving their soft tissue and its blood supply, use of small bone tunnels, and the inclusion of the capsule at the reconstruction site.

![[Fig.17.88.png]]
> **Fig. 17.88** Preparation of the lunate tunnel with Kirschner wire inserted through the dorsal capsule typically from dorsoproximal to volar-distal direction at around center of lunate bone.

![[Fig.17.89.png]]
> **Fig. 17.89** Preparation of the scaphoid tunnel with Kirschner wire inserted through the dorsal capsule typically from dorsodistal to volar-proximal direction.

![[eFig.17.99.png]]
> **eFig. 17.99** Palmaris longus tendon graft is delivered through the wrist capsule and the bone tunnels using a 2-mm mosquito grasper.

![[Fig.17.90.png]]
> **Fig. 17.90** The volar part of the tendon graft after passing through the scaphoid and lunate bone tunnels rests on the surface of the volar extrinsic ligaments of the wrist as well as the volar capsule, thus serving to augment the secondary stabilizers of the wrist at the scapholunate joint. It is important to make sure that the tendon graft does not loop across any flexor tendons or the neurovascular bundle when passing to the dorsal side.

The wrist is put back on the traction tower with minimal traction. The midcarpal joint is then inspected through either midcarpal portal. The RL pin is then withdrawn from the lunate so that the lunate is mobile. With manual traction of the two ends of the tendon graft, any SL gapping or step- off is corrected (Fig. 17.91). Any interposed tissue in the SL interval that prevents a complete reduction is excised arthroscopically. The reduction can also be facilitated by using a large bone reduction clamp between the scaphoid and triquetrum. Once adequate reduction is confirmed, the hand is put back on the hand table. The tendon graft is maximally tensioned and tied like a shoelace in square knot fashion securely over the dorsal capsule and secured with 2- 0 nonabsorbable braided sutures using noncutting needle (Fig. 17.92). SL joint diastasis, DISI correction, and reduction of dorsal scaphoid translation is confirmed arthroscopically and fluoroscopically. If concerned with the security of fixation, the tendon graft sutures may be augmented. If there is any doubt on the resulting stability, two 1.1 mm Kirschner wires are inserted through a small incision in the anatomic snuffbox region to transfix the scaphocapitate joint and protect the reconstructed ligament during healing (eFig. 17.100). Pinning across the SL joint directly carries the risk of damaging the tendon graft during the passage and is not recommended. The Kirschner wires are cut short and buried underneath the skin. Additional suture anchors can be placed adjacent to the dorsal bone tunnels for the scaphoid and lunate with a clear margin of 2 to 3 mm from the tunnels for additional graft fixation. The RL pin is then advanced to maintain the lunate reduction if necessary, such as in a situation where there is marked DISI deformity before the surgical correction. The tendon knot is then sutured to the adjacent dorsal joint capsule and the extensor retinaculum is repaired. The tourniquet is released for hemostasis. The wound is closed with absorbable subcuticular sutures (Video 17.42). A bulky dressing and a scaphoid plaster slab are applied with wrist in a neutral position and the thumb in neutral palmar abduction. The RL pin is removed at the beginning of the third week, and the wrist is immobilized in a short- arm thumb spica cast for a total of 6 weeks. The cast is then changed to a thumb spica splint for an additional 2 weeks, at which time gentle wrist mobilization is allowed out of the splint. The SC pins are removed at the beginning of the ninth week under local anesthesia. The splint is worn at night for another 4 to 6 weeks. Gradual wrist range- of- motion exercise under physiotherapy supervision is started after the pin removal. Gradual strengthening exercises are begun at the 13th week.

![[Fig.17.91.png]]
> **Fig. 17.91** Under arthroscopic observation, tensioning of the tendon graft closes the gap and reduction of the DISI deformity at the SL joint. The traction force should be minimized when doing this maneuver.

![[Fig.17.92.png]]
> **Fig. 17.92** Tendon graft knotted and sutured under maximal tension dorsal to the wrist capsular ligaments.

![[eFig.17.100.png]]
> **eFig. 17.100** The radiolunate joint and the scaphocapitate joint are transfixed with 1.1 mm Kirschner wires to protect the ligament repair.

#### Outcomes and Complications

From October 2002 to June 2012, the treatment method was applied in 17 patients with a mean age of 42 (range, 26 to 60) and chronic SL instability of average duration of 9.5 months (range, 1.5 to 18). There were three Geissler grade III and 14 grade IV instability cases. The average preop SL interval was 4.9 mm (range, 3 to 9). DISI deformity was present in 13 patients and the average SL angle was 85 degrees (range, 52 to 102). Six patients had stage I SLAC wrist.

At average follow up of 48.3 months (range, 11 to 132), 13 patients were able to return to their preinjury job level and four had some restriction at work. There was no pain in 11 patients while the rest had mild exertional pain or pain at the extremes of motion. The average total pain score was 1.7/20 in performing 10 ADL tasks, compared with the preoperative score of 8.3/20. VAS of daily maximum pain with exertion averaged 1.8/10 (preoperative 6.2/10). Compared with preoperative, the average improvement of range of motion in extension, flexion, radial deviation, and ulnar deviation was 13%, 16%, 13%, and 27%, respectively. Postoperative average total wrist performance score (Prince of Wales Hospital ADL Performance Assessment) was 37.8/40, which improved 35% from the preoperative score. Mean grip strength improved to 120% of the preoperative status and was 84% of the contralateral side. There was no major surgical complication. Radiographs showed an average SL interval of 2.9 mm (range, 1.6 to 5.5) and the average SL angle was 63 degrees. Recurrence of DISI deformity was seen in 4 patients without symptom. In one patient, ischemic change was noted in the proximal scaphoid and remained inconsequential. The patients with stage I SLAC wrist showed no progression of arthritis. All patients were satisfied with the procedure and treatment outcome (Fig. 17.93).

![[Fig.17.93.png]]
> **Fig. 17.93** A patient with chronic SL dissociation treated with arthroscopic-assisted tendon graft reconstruction. Result at 9.5 years postoperative demonstrated sustained reduction of the dissociation with no arthritic change as seen in x-rays and MRI.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Scapholunate Ligament Reconstruction With Tendon Graft

Indications
- Dynamic or static, subacute or chronic SL dissociation greater than 6 weeks from injury
- Reducible SL diastasis and DISI deformity, conﬁrmed radiologically and arthroscopically
- Intact articular cartilage or at most stage I SLAC wrist

Preoperative Evaluation
- Clinical symptoms and signs of SL instability
- Radiographs or ﬂuoroscopy in radial deviation to conﬁrm reduction of the SL gap, and ﬂexion to conﬁrm reduction of DISI
- Diagnostic arthroscopy under PSLA or high- resolution MRI to conﬁrm diagno sis and cartilage integrity

Pearls
- Good lighting and loupe magniﬁcation are essential.
- An assistant is necessary to manage and protect the volar neurovascular bun dle and tendons.
- Conﬁrm reducibility of SL dissociation before proceeding to reconstruction.
- Arthroscopic capsular release improves reducibility of the SL dissociation, improves tissue pliability, and removes intervening scar tissue at SL joint.
- Keep bone tunnels away from the articular margins of the scaphoid and the lunate in both PA and lateral views to avoid iatrogenic fracture.
- Keep the bone tunnels small ( 2.5 mm) and the capsule intact to prevent ischemic insult to the carpal bones.
- Do not use a cutting needle to suture the tendon graft knot as it may rupture the graft; always suture the graft to the adjacent capsular- ligament structures to augment secondary stabilizers.
- Do not pin across the SL joint to avoid iatrogenic injury to the tendon graft; pin the SC joint instead for more stable construct.

Technical Points
- Dorsal transverse incision centered around the 3- 4 portal, split extensor reti naculum but preserve capsule.
- Palmar incision follows the proximal wrist crease between the PL and FCR tendons, protect the palmar branch of the median nerve.
```

#### Corella Technique

Corella et al. described another technique of arthroscopic reconstruction of the dorsal portion of the SL ligament and the secondary stabilizers in 2011, and with further modification in 2013 to add on a volar component to the reconstruction. They emphasized the preservation of the DIC ligament as one the most important SL secondary stabilizers. The three- dimensional anatomic reconstruction not only restored the integrity of the SL ligament on the dorsal and volar aspect but also reversed the pronation tendency of the scaphoid and reduced dorsal translation of the scaphoid. Their indications were (1) arthroscopically complete SL ligament tear (i.e., EWAS classification grade IIIC, IV, or V SL lesions); (2) very easily reducible instability (i.e., the scaphoid can be easily reduced and positioned by an arthroscopic probe); and (3) the presence of clinical symptoms (i.e., pain, a decrease in the grip strength, and/or impairment of wrist function).

Locate the scaphoid between the ECRL and ECRB; locate the lunate through the EDC interval. Keep the scaphoid and lunate pins convergent both in the sagittal and axial planes to correct scapholunate gap and pronation deformity. In applying Linscheid maneuver to correct lunate position, keep RL pin in ulnar half of lunate to avoid interference with the lunate tunnel. When passing scaphoid pin to volar side, slight retraction of the FCR tendon radialward sufﬁces to protect radial artery. When passing the lunate pin to the volar side, good retraction of the ﬂexor tendons and the median nerve ulnarward with the wrist in a ﬂexed relaxed position is required. When passing the minigrasper through the bone tunnels to deliver the tendon graft, extreme caution is needed to ensure the grasper is inside the tunnel. Accidental slippage outside the bone may not be noticeable. Assess and conﬁrm the joint reduction in gap and step arthroscopically under reduced traction by maximally pulling the tendon graft in opposite direction. Withdraw the RL pin from lunate before attempting reduction. Recheck joint reduction arthroscopically after securing the ﬁrst tendon knot, readjust tension accordingly if reduction is suboptimal. Two tendon knots in square knot are secure enough; three knots will be too bulky. Tendon knot will remodel and ﬂatten in 6 months. If joint reduction is stable and secure, SC pinning is not necessary.

Inadequate removal of scar tissue prevents good joint reduction. Risk of fracture if tunnels are too close to the articular margin. Bone ischemia may occur if bone tunnels are too wide. Grasper may miss the bone tunnel. An injury to the palmar branch of the median nerve or the median nerve Entrapment of a ﬂexor or extensor tendon by tendon loop Loss of reduction if mobilized too early

Below- elbow cast for 6 to 8 weeks before active mobilization Passive mobilization by 10 weeks postoperative

The authors performed dry arthroscopy using multiple incisions, including the standard radiocarpal portals (3- 4 and 6R) and dorsal midcarpal portals (radial and ulnar). Two incisions were used for graft passing, including one 1.5 cm longitudinal dorsal central incision overlying the fourth extensor tendon compartment and another volar central portal. Two more incisions were used to retrieve a tendon graft, one overlying the FCR tendon and scaphoid tubercle, another one located 10 cm proximal to the distal incision. Two bone tunnels were created, one from the origin of the SL ligament on the proximal scaphoid to the scaphoid tubercle, another one centered on the lunate in the PA view and parallel to the distal articular surface of the lunate. Tunnels were created using 2.5 mm then 3 mm cannulated drill bits. They used a split FCR tendon graft based distally and measuring 3 mm wide × 8 to 10 cm long. The graft was delivered from the distal scaphoid tubercle to the proximal scaphoid at the 3- 4 portal. It was then redirected from the 3- 4 portal to the dorsal central wound beneath the extensor tendons. The graft was then passed through the lunate tunnel to the volar side and exited through the volar central wound. It was finally directly fixed to the volar scaphoid at the SL ligament footprint using a suture anchor. Additional augmentation of fixation in the scaphoid tubercle and dorsal lunate was performed with interference screws. Graft resistance and strength could be increased further with the use of a 1.3 mm suture tape. Kirschner wire fixation was not used. Mobilization was started at 2 to 4 weeks postoperative, and employed mainly dart- throwing exercise to avoid overloading the graft. Proprioception exercises were started at 6 weeks after the surgery.

The authors reported results on 11 patients with static instability and 16 patients with dynamic instabiliity. There were significant increase in the grip strength and decrease in VAS pain score and DASH scores 6 months after surgery. On radiologic follow- up, a significant decrease in the average overall SL gap and SL angle after surgery for static patients were found. However, the author commented that despite a significant improvement in the SL angle and the gap, the gap remained open in many patients with static instability, though there was no correlation between radiologic and clinical outcome.

Aviles et al. described an arthroscopic version of the RASL procedure for stabilization of the SL joint. It was indicated for patients with SL dissociation without arthritis and, in selected cases, with stage I SLAC wrist. MCR and 3- 4 radiocarpal portals were used to excoriate and prepare the SL joint surfaces. By use of 1.6 mm Kirschner wire joysticks in the lunate and distal pole of the scaphoid, the scaphoid was reduced by extension and supination, while the lunate was flexed. A 0.9 mm guidewire was advanced through the scaphoid waist, across the SL joint to the proximal medial corner of the lunate. Supplemental Kirschner wire fixation, from the scaphoid to the capitatum and lunate to the radius, stabilized the reduction for placement of a cannulated screw through a 1- 2 portal, while reduction and positioning were confirmed arthroscopically. A good result was reported by Caloia et al. in 9 wrists of 8 patients at a mean follow- up of 34.6 months. In 3 cases, screws were removed owing to loosening or symptoms.

#### Osteochondral Grafting

A focal chondral lesion is a common cause of chronic wrist pain from synovitis. While smaller chondral lesions respond well to drill chondroplasty, the best treatment for the more sizable lesion remains unknown. Ho et al. published a technique of arthroscopic transplantation of osteochondral autograft from the knee joint to the distal radius for posttraumatic osteochondral lesions of 6 to 10 mm with a satisfactory clinical result at average follow- up of 70.5 months (Video 17.43). In all cases, the osteochondral grafts were shown by CAT scan or MRI to have complete incorporation to the host bone by 3- 4 months postoperation (Fig. 17.94). Second look arthroscopy at 6 to 9 months postoperation in 3 patients confirmed preservation of normal- appearing cartilage at the grafted area(Fig. 17.95).

![[Fig.17.94.png]]
> **Fig. 17.94** CT/MRI of the wrist and clinical pictures of a patient receiving arthroscopic transplantation of osteochondral autograft 10 years 4 months after the surgery. Note the excellent range of motion and incorporation of the osteochondral graft to the host bone and joint.

![[Fig.17.95.png]]
> **Fig. 17.95** Arthroscopic view of the osteochondral defect at the dorsal lunate fossa, being replaced with an osteochondral plug from the knee at operation. Second look arthroscopy at 29 months postoperative demonstrated excellent healing with minimal junctional defect. The patient was pain-free.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC- ASSISTED OSTEOCHONDRAL GRAFTING

The operation is performed under general anesthesia. A tourniquet is applied to the arm but is not inflated during the arthroscopic assessment and debridement. Synovitis over the dorsal lunate fossa is debrided with a 2.0 mm shaver to uncover the underlying osteochondral lesion (Fig. 17.96). The cartilage debris is cleaned until the subchondral bone is reached. The true extent of the lesion is determined using the probe for measurement. The proximal side of the articular surface of the lunate should be evaluated. A well- developed “kissing” chondral lesion may preclude the use of an osteochondral graft as treatment. The Osteoarticular Transfer System is employed for the harvest and transfer process (eFig.17.101). The arthroscope is placed through the 4- 5 portal while a 6 mm recipient harvester is inserted into the joint through the 3- 4 portal after the wound was gently dilated with a small hemostat. Extreme care is exercised to avoid damage to the healthy cartilage. The harvester is directed toward the osteochondral lesion at the dorsal aspect of the lunate fossa. At this junction, the wrist is passively flexed to about 50 degrees while it is still maintained by digital traction (Fig. 17.97). This maneuver moves the dorsal horn of the lunate palmarward and opens up the space between the lunate and the lunate fossa to accommodate the harvester. With the harvester positioned as vertical and perpendicular as possible to the articular surface of the lunate fossa just over the osteochondral lesion, the harvester is manually hammered gently to drive the trephine into the bone to a depth of about 10 to 12 mm, which can be monitored through observing the gauge of the trephine (Figs. 17.98 and 17.99). At least 2 mm of an intact articular margin should be maintained dorsally. By removing the harvester, a cylindrical plug of bone of 10 to 12 mm long containing the osteochondral defect is removed from the joint.

![[Fig.17.96.png]]
> **Fig. 17.96** The osteochondral lesion is often covered by localized synovitis. After synovial shaving, the osteochondral lesion is fully exposed and good bleeding from the subchondral bone can be seen.

![[eFig.17.101.png]]
> **eFig. 17.101** The Osteoarticular Transfer System (OATS) with the harvester trephines. (Courtesy of Arthrex, Naples, FL.)

![[Fig.17.97.png]]
> **Fig. 17.97** The traction tower allows joint manipulation with the wrist in a flexed position under traction to maintain the working space.

![[Fig.17.98.png]]
> **Fig. 17.98** The wrist is flexed to allowed positioning of the trephine at the 3- 4 portal. The arthroscope is placed at the 4- 5 portal to monitor the process. With the harvester positioned vertically and perpendicular to the articular surface of the lunate fossa just over the osteochondral lesion, the harvester is manually impacted to drive the trephine into the bone.

![[Fig.17.99.png]]
> **Fig. 17.99** Arthroscopic view of the trephine over the osteochondral defect at the dorsal lunate fossa.

The osteochondral graft is harvested from the nondominant knee according to the technique described by Hangody et al. A tourniquet is applied to the thigh. A small incision is made over the lateral aspect of the knee at the lateral femoral condyle. A 6 mm donor harvester is inserted to the sulcus terminalis of the lateral femoral condyle to a depth slightly deeper than that in the wrist (eFig. 17.102). The osteochondral plug is removed and delivered to the wrist. The osteochondral plug can be trimmed on table to the precise length required (eFig. 17.103). The defect in the knee can be partially refilled by packing in the cancellous bone obtained from the recipient site (eFig. 17.104).

![[eFig.17.102.png]]
> **eFig. 17.102** A 6 mm OATS harvester is inserted to the lateral femoral condyle to harvest the osteochondral graft.

![[eFig.17.103.png]]
> **eFig. 17.103** The osteochondral plug can be trimmed on the table to the precise length required.

![[eFig.17.104.png]]
> **eFig. 17.104** The defect in the donor knee.

A short transparent plastic delivery tube is attached to the tip of the donor harvester containing the osteochondral plug. It is then gently inserted into the defect through the 3- 4 portal with the aid of a rotating dial (Fig. 17.100). Graft insertion can be monitored through the plastic sheath (Fig. 17.101). The harvester is removed when the graft is flush with the surrounding articular surface. A plastic tamp is used to tap the plug so as to obtain a press- fit. When there is a mismatch noted at the junction of the articular cartilage, the redundant cartilage can be excised using an arthroscopic banana knife. As the articular cartilage of the knee is generally thicker than that of the wrist, this shaving maneuver will not result in a significant loss of articular cartilage. The graft is very stable after press fitting so no internal fixation is required. The stability of the graft and the smoothness of articulation can be tested by passively moving the wrist.

![[Fig.17.100.png]]
> **Fig. 17.100** The 6-mm Osteoarticular Transfer System (OATS) harvester with the osteochondral graft on is inserted into the wrist joint through the 3- 4 portal. The graft is delivered into the defect with a dialed pusher.

![[Fig.17.101.png]]
> **Fig. 17.101** Arthroscopic view of the osteochondral graft delivered into the defect through a transparent plastic tube.

The harvester is removed and the wound is closed by Steri-Strips, followed by a bulky compressive dressing (eFig. 17.105). A wrist splint is prescribed for 2 to 4 weeks and the patient is instructed to perform gentle active wrist motion from day 3 onward. Passive motion exercises can be initiated by 4 to 6 weeks. Active use of the hand in self- care and daily activities is allowed but load bearing of the wrist is discouraged for the first 6 weeks to allow incorporation of the graft.

![[eFig.17.105.png]]
> **eFig. 17.105** Small wounds at completion of surgery.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Osteochondral Grafting

Indications
- Isolated painful posttraumatic osteochondral lesion of size >5 mm in lunate fossa
- Failed drill chondroplasty treatment

Preoperative Evaluation
- Clinical tenderness at dorsal RL joint and on loading in wrist extension
- Diagnostic arthroscopy under PSLA to conﬁrm isolated osteochondral lesion without signiﬁcant kissing lesion over lunate side

Pearls
- Maximally ﬂex the wrist while in traction to allow perpendicular orientation of the bone trephine.
- Insert trephine from 3- 4 portal for a dorsal lesion; for a more central lesion, use a 4- 5 portal.
- Need gradual dilation of the portal to allow insertion of a 6 mm trephine safely without injuring the extensor tendons.
- Trim excessive knee cartilage after implantation of the osteochondral plug.

Technical Points
- Remove the osteochondral lesion down to 10 to 12 mm depth under direct vision.
- Maintain 2 mm intact dorsal articular margin.
- When delivering the graft into the wrist joint, do not use excessive force while hammering.
- The subchondral bone plug from the wrist can be inserted back to the donor site.

Pitfalls
- Fracturing dorsal cortex of lunate fossa
- Injury to extensor tendons by the trephine
- Oblique trajectory of the osteochondral plug

Postoperative Care
- Active mobilization immediately postoperative without casting
- Passive mobilization when bone healing is conﬁrmed by CT scan, usually around 6 to 8 weeks postoperative
```

### Complications of Wrist Arthroscopy

Complications of wrist arthroscopy are relatively uncommon. Beredjiklian et al. reported 11 complications in 211 patients who underwent wrist arthroscopy. There were two major complications in his studies: one case of severe arthrofibrosis and one case of a ganglion that developed at the 3- 4 portal. Nine patients developed minor complications including transient ulnar neurapraxia, transient wrist and finger stiffness, skin burn, portal site infection, and ECU tendonitis (eFig. 17.106). All complications had resolved at the last follow- up visit. Pell and Uhl noted three tendon ruptures in their series of 47 patients treated with thermal ablation during wrist arthroscopy. One patient had a full- thickness skin burn after the use of thermal shrinkage. Leclercq and Mathoulin et al. reported a large- scale multicenter retrospective study on complications of wrist arthroscopy in 36 centers comprising 10,107 wrist arthroscopies. There were 605 complications (5.98% of the cases), of which 5.07% were listed as serious and 0.91% as minor. The most frequent ones were failure to achieve the procedure’s goals (1.16%) and nerve lesions (1.17%) (eFig. 17.107). Cartilage lesions and complex regional pain syndrome each occurred in 0.5%. Other complications (wrist stiffness, loose bodies, hematomas, tendon lacerations) were less frequent (eFig. 17.108). They found a lower complication rate in surgeons with over 25 arthroscopies a year and/or greater than 5 years of experience. They concluded that there was a significant learning curve with the technique of wrist arthroscopy, both in terms of volume and experience.

![[eFig.17.106.png]]
> **eFig. 17.106** Complication one: The Kirschner wire can be broken and left inside the joint during ulnar tunnel creation for TFCC reconstruction with tendon graft.

![[eFig.17.107.png]]
> **eFig. 17.107** Complication two: The dorsal cutaneous branch of ulnar nerve was hit by the sutures at multiple sites during arthroscopic repair of Palmar-type 1B TFCC tear.

![[eFig.17.108.png]]
> **eFig. 17.108** Complication three: an FPL tendon rupture after arthroscopic thermal shrinkage of the volar plate of thumb MCP joint. The rupture was due to thermal injury with delay onset and presented about 3 weeks after the index procedure.

To prevent complications of wrist arthroscopy, careful portal planning is essential. A few millimeters difference with regard to placement of the portals can make a big difference in proximity to vital structures and ease of joint access. A needle should always be used, prior to cutting the skin, to determine precise portal placement. The needle should pass easily into the joint without impaling tendons, carpal bones, or the distal radius. It is important to always use blunt trocars to prevent iatrogenic damage of the articular cartilage during introduction of the arthroscopic cannula. Separate inflow and outflow portals limit fluid extravasation into the soft tissues. Use of physiologic solutions (e.g., lactated Ringer’s) allows reabsorption of extravasated fluid and minimizes the risk of compartment syndrome. Kirschner wires should always be inserted with the use of a soft tissue protector or the oscillation mode to limit any potential damage to the numerous sensory branches surrounding the wrist.

### Thumb Carpometacarpal Joint Arthroscopy

Description of wrist arthroscopy is not complete without mentioning the role of arthroscopy in the CMC joint, especially in the thumb, where painful osteoarthritis (OA) is prone to develop. While there has been considerable attention paid to the treatment of advanced disease, much less attention has focused on early intervention to prevent or delay the need for a salvage procedure. Recent advances in small joint arthroscopy allow for the treatment of symptomatic basal joint disease in a minimally invasive fashion without joint destabilization. Menon first published the use of arthroscopic techniques in treating thumb CMC joint OA in 1996. He did 25 cases of arthroscopic partial trapeziectomy and interposition arthroplasty with autogenous tendon graft, Gore- Tex, or fascia lata allograft. A promising place in treating thumb CMC joint OA by arthroscopy was mentioned by Culp and Osterman in 1997. Culp and Rekant did 24 cases of arthroscopic debridement, shrinkage, and partial or complete trapeziectomy with 88% good to excellent outcomes. They concluded that arthroscopic debridement and synovectomy offered an exciting alternative in Eaton I and II arthritis. Badia defined an arthroscopic classification of thumb CMC joint arthritis. In stage I, there is diffuse synovitis but the articular cartilage is intact and volar capsule laxity is demonstrated. In stage II, there is central fovea articular cartilage loss of the trapezium, deep metacarpal base loss, and resulting synovitis. In stage III, there is widespread articular cartilage loss and deep osteophyte on trapezium. Currently described arthroscopic techniques have shown efficacy and success in treatment of thumb CMC joint OA, including arthroscopic debridement, synovectomy, capsuloligamentous shrinkage, hemi- or total trapeziectomy, suture button suspensionplasty, and arthroscopic fusion. Good to excellent outcomes have been obtained from all of the above arthroscopic procedures, except that complication rates with synthetic materials in interposition series and interposition without partial trapeziectomy were higher. Cobb and Edwards both considered interposition unnecessary following arthroscopic resection arthroplasty.

#### Indications

As a general rule, any patient who has isolated and refractory degenerative disease of the thumb CMC joint is a candidate for an arthroscopic treatment. This form of treatment does not preclude an open trapeziectomy and/or ligament reconstruction at a later date as a salvage procedure. Arthroscopy also has a role in intraarticular fracture management.

#### Contraindications

Distortion of the anatomy due to prior injury or surgery, unstable or friable skin that would preclude the use of traction, and uncontrolled local or systemic infection are contraindications to surgery. Patients with pathologic ligamentous laxity such as Ehlers- Danlos syndrome or lupus arthritis are not ideal candidates for this procedure although a successful arthroscopic tendon arthroplasty has been reported. If present, metacarpophalangeal (MCP) joint hyperextension must be carefully considered and addressed separately, as it can confound both open and arthroscopic surgery on the thumb CMC joint.

#### Arthroscopic Anatomy

The arthroscopic anatomy of the thumb CMC joint has been described in detail by Slutsky. The anterior oblique ligament (AOL) is a twopart ligament that consists of a superficial portion and a deep portion that are visible arthroscopically (Video 17.44). The superficial anterior oblique ligament (sAOL) is immediately deep to the thenar musculature, which overlies the volar aspect of the CMC joint and is superficial to the deep anterior oblique ligament (dAOL). The dAOL, formerly known as the volar beak ligament, is an articular ligament that lies deep to the sAOL. The sulcus between the sAOL and dAOL can often be palpated with an arthroscopic probe. Fenestrations or tears of the AOL will permit arthroscopic views of the FCR. The UCL is an extracapsular ligament that is slightly ulnar to and superficial to the sAOL. Arthroscopically, it is identified by its oblique fibers running ulnar to the AOL. Tears of the UCL will reveal the thenar muscle fibers running behind it. On the dorsal side, there are two important ligaments. The posterior oblique ligament (POL) is a capsular ligament arising from a fan- shaped base on the dorsoulnar side of the trapezium immediately ulnar to the dorsoradial ligament (DRL) and can be seen tangentially from the modified radial portal. It runs obliquely to insert into the dorsoulnar aspect and palmoulnar tubercle of the first metacarpal base. The DRL is the shortest, thickest, and widest ligament that spans the joint. It is a fan- shaped capsular ligament that originates from the dorsoradial tubercle of the trapezium and broadly inserts into the dorsal base of the first metacarpal. It can also be seen from the modified radial portal, lying radial to the POL. If the dorsal ligament complex is cut or torn, the thumb CMC joint can be dislocated, even in the presence of an intact AOL. Finally, the dorsal intermetacarpal ligament (DIML) originates from the dorsoradial aspect of the index metacarpal radial to the ECRL insertion and inserts onto the palmar- ulnar tubercle of the base of the thumb metacarpal along with the POL and UCL.

#### Thumb CMC Joint Arthroscopic Portals

The standard portals for thumb CMC joint arthroscopy are defined by Berger as the 1R and 1U portals (Fig. 17.102). Similar portals were described by Menon in 1996. The 1R portal is just radial to the APL tendon. It passes through the nonligamentous capsule just lateral to the AOL. It is best for viewing the DRL, POL, and UCL. The 1U portal is located just ulnar to the EPB tendon. It passes between or through the DRL and POL. It provides good views of AOL and UCL. There is no true internervous plane because branches of the SRN surround the field and are at risk for injury. The radial artery courses immediately posterior and ulnar to the arthroscopic field.

![[Fig.17.102.png]]
> **Fig. 17.102** The typical portals used at thumb CMC joint arthroscopy as shown in a cadaver as well as in a patient: The 1R portal is just radial to the APL tendon. The 1U portal is located just ulnar to the EPB tendon. The thenar portal is created through the radial border of the thenar muscle and is approximately 90 degrees from the 1U portal. It is usually employed as the outflow portal.

Accessory portals have been described by Orellana and Chow.They described a modified radial portal (RP) for improving the radial view of the thumb CMC joint. The radial portal is located just distal to the oblique ridge of the trapezium following a line along the radial border of the FCR tendon rather than the APL. It allows a better assessment of the DRL and POL, and a complete view of the lateral side of the joint. The scope is placed in the 1U portal and the light source is pointed to the radial portal, which lies just radial to the AOL. A 22- gauge needle is placed percutaneously just distal to the ridge of the trapezium and visualized arthroscopically before finalizing the portal.

Walsh et al. described a thenar portal, which is made by illuminating the thenar eminence with the arthroscope in the 1U portal and inserting an 18- gauge needle through the bulk of the thenar muscles at the level of the thumb CMC joint. This portal is approximately 90 degrees from the 1U portal. The ulnar recess can be visualized from the thenar portal and complements the ulnar tubercle, the tapered prominence of the ulnar aspect of the trapezium. It is straddled by the UCL. The portal was described to be safe from the motor branch of the median nerve (MBMN), with average of 23.0 ± 1.6 mm from the thenar portal. However, a recent study showed that the terminal branches of the MBMN were all located in the proximal third of the thenar muscles, where the thenar portal was introduced. The distances between the superficial and the deep branches of MBMN and the thenar portal were 1.2 mm (range, 0 to 6.2) and 2.2 mm (range, 0 to 7), respectively. Five (35.7%) of the 14 fresh- frozen specimens indicated the superficial branch laid directly over the portal. The authors concluded that great caution needed to be exercised when using the thenar portal.

Slutsky developed a distal dorsal (D2) accessory portal that allowed one to look down onto the trapezium directly as opposed to across it, which he felt was safer for resection of medial osteophytes. This portal is situated in the dorsal aspect of the first web space ulnar to the EPL tendon and 1 cm distal to the V- shaped soft spot at the junction of the index and thumb metacarpal bases. The portal lies just distal to the DIML. A 22- gauge needle is inserted 1 cm distal to the index- thumb metacarpal junction and angulated in a proximal, radial, and palmar direction while hugging the thumb metacarpal. Confirmation of joint access is performed from either the 1R or 1U portal. A small skin incision is made, and tenotomy scissors are used to spread the soft tissue and pierce the joint capsule followed by the arthroscope or instrumentation. Because of the nearby dorsal branch of the radial artery, there is no true safe zone for the D2 portal, so a careful wound- spread technique is of paramount importance.

#### Arthroscopic Examination of the Thumb CMC Joint

The procedure is very suitable to be performed under PSLA without tourniquet or sedation. The patient is placed in the supine position with the operated arm placed on a hand table. A standard wrist arthroscopy tower and equipment with a 1.9- mm arthroscope are used. Adequate padding should be interposed between the patient’s arm and the metal baseplate and the stand to avoid impingement discomfort. Only the thumb needs to be suspended with a single nylon finger trap and vertical traction force of about 8 lbs is applied. Taping may be applied to the junction of the finger trap and the thumb to avoid slip out. The forearm is supinated by wrapping the other four fingers onto the tower stand so that the dorsum of the thumb base is directly facing the surgeon. Continuous irrigation is achieved with a 3- L bag of normal saline hanging 1.5 m above the patient and driven by gravity (Fig. 17.103).

![[Fig.17.103.png]]
> **Fig. 17.103** The setting of arthroscopic surgery for the thumb CMC joint. The thumb alone is connected to the traction tower with a single finger trap. The other fingers are wrapped and taped to the pole of the tower to keep the dorsal side of the thumb (nail side) facing the surgeon.

Fluoroscopic identification of the thumb CMC joint is usually not necessary. However, it can be quite easy to go into the wrong joint (i.e., the STT joint) inadvertently. The following technique helps to localize the portal with certainty. By palpating the radial border of the second metacarpal and ulnar border of the thumb metacarpal from its shaft to the base, the confluence of the second and thumb metacarpal base can be easily located. Draw a horizontal line radialward from this confluence point; the line will represent the level of the thumb CMC joint. Palpate EPB and APL tendons, which are always found together. The intersection of the horizontal line with the ulnar border of the EPB forms the 1U portal. The intersection of the horizontal line with the radial border of the APL represents the 1R portal (Fig. 17.104).

![[Fig.17.104.png]]
> **Fig. 17.104** Marking of the portals: Draw a horizontal line radialward from confluence point of the first and second metacarpals; the line will represent the level of the first CMC joint. Palpate EPB and APL tendons, which are always found together. The intersection of the horizontal line with the ulnar border of the EPB forms the 1U portal. The intersection of the horizontal line with the radial border of the APL represents the 1R portal.

Xylocaine (2%) with 1:200,000 adrenaline is injected through a 25- gauge needle to the portal sites, to the subcutaneous layer, and to the capsule only. The joint is distended with around 2 to 3 mL of normal saline using a 19- gauge needle. The needle tip generally points slightly upward indicating the correct joint orientation. In contrast, if the STT joint is entered, the needle usually points slightly downward. Transverse skin incisions are made along the skin crease to provide improved esthetics. A fine mosquito forceps is used to dissect the subcutaneous layer and protect cutaneous nerves. The joint capsule is punctured. The directions of the portals should be pointing toward the thenar region. The correct placement of the arthroscope into the thumb CMC joint can be confirmed with the concave saddle appearance of the trapezium (Fig. 17.105). If by accident the arthroscope is inserted into the STT joint, the scaphoid will be immediately recognized by its convex, dome- shaped articular surface. The 1R and 1U portals are interchangeable for viewing and working. Outflow can be achieved with an 18- gauge needle at the thenar portal, which is 90 degrees perpendicular to and at the same level of the 1U portal, through the thenar muscle bulk. The second or third portals can be established under direct arthroscopic visualization. Synovectomy is performed with a 2- mm shaver or a small RF probe to remove any inflamed synovium and frayed capsule (Video 17.45). Loose bodies floating or adherent to the capsule are removed. Thermal shrinkage can be performed on lax ligaments. Prominent osteophytes are burred in order to create a smooth opposing articular surface. Xylocaine with adrenaline can be injected through the joint to the volar joint capsule if the patient feels pain when operating on the volar structures. The operation is concluded by releasing the thumb traction and checking for full active movement of the thumb by the patient. The wound is apposed by Steri-Strips and suture is not necessary. A bulky dressing is applied for soft tissue swelling for a few days before changing to waterproof light dressing. For joint debridement and synovectomy, no cast or immobilization is necessary. Patients can start free range of motion immediately after the operation as tolerated. For those who had thermal shrinkage on the thumb CMC joint ligaments, cast or splint immobilization is recommended for 4 to 6 weeks.

![[Fig.17.105.png]]
> **Fig. 17.105** The correct placement of the arthroscope into the thumb CMC joint can be confirmed with the concave saddle appearance of the trapezium.

#### Arthroscopic Debridement and Capsular Thermal Shrinkage

As in its use for the interosseous ligaments of the proximal carpal row (see page 805), thermal shrinkage at the thumb CMC joint is usually performed in association with arthroscopic debridement and synovectomy. It is useful for patients in earlier phase of thumb basal joint arthritis where the integrity of the lax ligaments is still maintained, and articular cartilage destruction is not advanced. A partial denervation effect may also provide immediate pain control.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC CAPSULAR THERMAL SHRINKAGE

For thermal capsular shrinkage, the arthroscope is initially placed in the 1U portal. A motorized shaver is used to debride any synovitis and to expose the capsular ligaments. A thermal shrinkage probe is then introduced into the 1R portal and is passed repeatedly over the capsuloligamentous structures in a controlled, systemic manner. The energy level is usually preset by the manufacturer of the thermal apparatus. As the CMC joint is a very small space, the fluid can heat up rapidly. If the probe is passed too slowly, this may result in excessive thermal damage. If is passed too rapidly, it may result in insufficient shrinkage; 2 to 3 seconds on each spot of application suffice. It is also important not to paint the capsular structures but rather to make precise stripes to reduce devascularization. Also, the energy control and timing are even more critical when working on the dorsal capsule, as there are overlying extensor tendons that are prone to thermal injury. A sling should be passed through the 1U and 1R portals and beneath the APL and EPB to protect the tendons.

The AOL is initially shrunk and progress is made toward the UCL. At this point, the portals are switched and the remaining UCL, POL, and the DRL can be treated in the same way. When completed, a Kirschner wire is placed across the thumb CMC joint or between the thumb and index metacarpals following the procedure to stabilize the joint. The pin is removed after 4 weeks and range- of- motion exercises begin. Strengthening exercises are started 6 weeks postoperatively.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Debridement With or Without Capsular Shrinkage of the Thumb CMC Joint

Indications
- Diagnostic arthroscopy of the thumb CMC joint
- Painful OA thumb CMC joint of all Eaton stages failed conservative treatment

Preoperative Evaluation
- Imaging and clinical examination of STT joint and thumb MCP joint

Pearls
- Very suitable arthroscopic procedure for PSLA technique without tourniquet
- 1.9 mm arthroscope recommended
- While suspended in traction, supinate the forearm to bring the dorsum of the thumb CMC joint to face the surgeon.
- Needle tip when inserted into the CMC joint generally points slightly upward; in contrast, a needle in the STT joint tends to point slightly downward.
- Use ﬁnger pivoting technique to control the arthroscopic lens movement.
- Thenar portal is good for outﬂow using 18- gauge needle, especially when using RF probe.

Technical Points
- Pass a sling beneath the tendons of the ﬁrst dorsal compartment through the 1U and 1R portals to protect tendons before shrinking dorsal ligaments.
- Use 2.0 mm shaver to remove synovitis, followed by RF probe for ﬁnal touch up and to produce a denervation effect for pain control.
- Use 1U and 1R portal interchangeably to reach all areas of the CMC joint.
- Systematically perform thermal shrinkage: probe in 1R portal can reach AOL and UCL; probe in 1U portal can reach UCL, POL, and DRL.
- Remove bony spur with 2.9 mm arthroscopic bur.

Pitfalls
- Incorrectly inserting the arthroscope into the STT joint
- Excessive thermal energy application may cause tendon rupture.
- Injury to SBRN near portals

Postoperative Care
- For simple synovectomy and joint debridement, early active mobilization is allowed after 2 to 3 days.
- Thumb spica cast for 4 to 6 weeks after thermal shrinkage. Kirschner wire ﬁxation is optional.
```

#### Outcomes and complications

Wong and Ho reported the outcome of simple arthroscopic debridement and synovectomy with or without thermal shrinkage in 76 thumb CMC joint OA cases, the majority of which were in advanced stages and had failed conservative management; 91% of patients experienced pain relief, which occurred on average 3 months postoperation. Longer- term excellent to good pain relief occurred in 44.7% of the patients at an average follow- up of 59 months. Poor results were seen in 18 patients (23.7%) who required other revision procedures. Pain relief was not statistically correlated with symptom duration, Eaton or Badia staging, or radiologic progression.

With more severe degenerative disease of the thumb CMC joint, or for cases resistant to simple joint debridement and synovectomy, arthroscopic partial or complete trapeziectomy with or without stabilization procedure is indicated.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: ARTHROSCOPIC PARTIAL TRAPEZIECTOMY

The arthroscope is inserted in the 1U portal. A full- radius resector is placed through the 1R or thenar working portal to debride the synovitis and degenerative articular cartilage. The advantage of a thenar portal is that it is located farther from the superficial branch of the radial nerve and is perpendicular to the 1U portal to allow for easier triangulation. A 2.9- mm arthroscopic bur is then placed through the thenar portal to complete the partial trapeziectomy. Approximately 3 to 5 mm of the distal aspect of the trapezium should be removed. The depth of the bone being removed can be measured by the bur. Approximately 1.5 times the diameter of the 2.9- mm bur, or the diameter of the 3.9- mm bur sheath, is the appropriate amount of bone to be removed. An even thickness of distal trapezium removal should be achieved. The medial osteophyte should be resected to allow a better reduction of the thumb CMC joint. The Slutsky D2 portal can be utilized for a more direct approach to the osteophyte. The shaver and arthroscope are switched between the portals for optimal visualization and debridement. It is vital to confirm the degree of trapezial resection under fluoroscopy. The goal is to eliminate impingement of the base of the thumb metacarpal and the remaining trapezium upon motion. If the operation is performed under PSLA without sedation, active motion of the thumb and pinch can be tested on the table. If there is no bony contact or excessive joint translation demonstrated, no immobilization is needed and early active thumb mobilization exercises are encouraged. If residual joint instability is noted upon ballottement test under fluoroscopy, the thumb metacarpal should temporarily be pinned for 3 to 4 weeks to maintain the resection space. Alternatively, to decrease the amount of postoperative immobilization while still maintaining support to the thumb metacarpal, a mini- tightrope suture device (Arthrex, Naples, FL) can be placed between the thumb and the index metacarpal.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: SUTURE- BUTTON SUSPENSIONPLASTY

The mini- tightrope device consists of a braided suture that is looped between two steel buttons with one button affixed to the thumb metacarpal and the other to the index metacarpal. This creates a nonanatomic tether to the index metacarpal but assists in maintaining joint reduction and eliminates the need for Kirschner wire stabilization.

Following partial trapeziectomy, the surgeon makes a small incision 5 mm distal to the 1R portal along the base of the first metacarpal. Prior to implant fixation, some authors prefer to reduce the thumb metacarpal dorsoradial subluxation and adduction deformity, restore its normal height, and temporarily Kirschner wire it to the trapezium at 40 degrees of palmar abduction, 15 degrees of radial abduction, and sufficient pronation such that the thumb pulp faces the pulp of the middle finger. Subcutaneous dissection with a mosquito forceps on the volar- radial aspect of the thumb metacarpal base is performed. A fine periosteal elevator elevates the radial border of the thenar muscle and the periosteum from the bone so that the suture button does not irritate the periosteum and cause pain. Hardware prominence can be minimized by placing the suture button under part of the APL tendon, which also promotes pronation of the thumb. A Suture Passing Kirschner wire (Arthrex, Naples, FL) is then drilled from the thumb metacarpal base obliquely toward the proximal diaphysis of the index metacarpal. The pin is inserted 2 to 3 mm distal to the thumb metacarpal articular surface, at the volar- radial border of the thumb metacarpal base. The targeting point of the index metacarpal should be at the junction between the proximal third and the middle third of its length to minimize the complication of first web narrowing. The trajectory is confirmed under fluoroscopy. A second incision is made ulnar to the index metacarpal and retractors are placed to protect the superficial branch of the radial nerve. Subcutaneous dissection is necessary to protect cutaneous nerve branches. A fine periosteal elevator is used to elevate the interosseous muscle and periosteum from the bone. The Suture Passing Kirschner wire is driven bicortically through the index metacarpal. The looped tightrope suture is then passed through a wire loop at the trailing end of the Suture Passing Kirschner wire. The Suture Passing Kirschner wire is pulled through the exit wound to advance the tightrope suture through both Kirschner wire holes and snug the thumb suture button against the metacarpal base. The suture button is now seated on the volarradial cortex of the thumb metacarpal base and is covered by the thenar muscle. The Suture Passing Kirschner wire is removed, and the looped tightrope suture cut to free two strands of suture. A second suture button is placed over the tightrope sutures at the index metacarpal diaphysis, and the sutures are then tightened to seat the second button against the dorsoulnar cortex of the index metacarpal. A provisional knot is tied and held with a mosquito forceps. The pin across the thumb CMC joint is then removed. The alignment, mobility, and resistance to subsidence are then checked under fluoroscopy. Tensioning is adjusted accordingly, and the remaining knots are tied. The key is not to tie the sutures so tightly so that the thumb metacarpal impinges on the base of the index metacarpal. A ballottement test is performed under fluoroscopy to confirm proper

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopic Partial Trapeziectomy and Suture Button Suspensionplasty

Indications
- Painful, advanced OA thumb CMC joint despite conservative treatment
- Failed previous arthroscopic thumb CMC joint synovectomy and debridement

Preoperative Evaluation
- Radiographs demonstrating signiﬁcant joint subluxation, widening of the space between the bases of the ﬁrst and second metacarpal, a large medial osteophyte

Pearls
- Resect 4 mm of the distal trapezial articular surface, using the sheath of a 2.9 mm arthroscopic bur as a reference.
- Complete the resection to the desirable depth at one area ﬁrst, then excise the other areas, taking the initial depth of resection as a reference to ensure an even removal.
- Resect the medial osteophyte to help reduce the joint.
- For a CMC joint with signiﬁcant subluxation, the joint can be reduced and transﬁxed with a Kirschner wire and ﬁxed with a mini- tightrope.
- The incision over the base of the thumb and index metacarpal should be bluntly dissected to avoid injury to SBRN.
- After the button over the index metacarpal is secured, one provisional knot is tied and held with a mosquito forceps. The pin across the thumb CMC joint is then removed to allow assessment of alignment, mobility, and resistance to subsidence under ﬂuoroscopy. Tension is adjusted accordingly, and the remaining knots are tied.
```

A thenar portal can be used when resecting the dorsal side of the trapezium. A D2 portal can be used to resect the medial osteophyte more effectively. The button on the thumb metacarpal should be placed subperiosteally just underneath the APL tendon and 2 to 3 mm away from the joint margin. The guide pin of the mini- tightrope should aim toward the junction between the proximal and middle thirds of the index metacarpal and on the ulnar surface of the metacarpal to avoid impinging the extensor tendon. The knot should not be tied too tight to avoid impingement of the two metacarpals. The buttons should be placed in longitudinal orientation and well covered with thenar muscle and interossei muscle.

Inadequate resection of the medial osteophyte leads to suboptimal reduction. Inadequate resection of the distal trapezium leads to bone- on- bone contact and residual pain. Thumb metacarpal button impingement if placed too close to the articular surface, not covered by thenar muscle, or in a transverse orientation. Index metacarpal button impingement on the extensor tendons if placed too dorsally. Restricted ﬁrst web space and thumb movement if the index metacarpal but ton is placed too distally beyond midpoint of index metacarpal.

Short- arm thumb spica for 4 weeks with intermittent active thumb mobiliza tion out of splint positioning. The radial border of the thenar muscle fascia and the radial border of the interosseous fascia are closed to their origins, respectively, for coverage of the two suture buttons. The wounds are closed with fine nylon sutures. A short- arm thumb spica slab is applied for 5 to 7 days, then changed to a thumb spica splint for an additional 4 weeks. The patient is encouraged to do active rangeof- motion exercises without the splint several times per day (Figs. 17.106 to 17.110; eFig. 17.109; Video 17.46).

![[Fig.17.106.png]]
> **Fig. 17.106** X-ray showing a case of Eaton stage III painful OA of the left thumb CMC joint with a large medial osteophyte and joint subluxation.

![[Fig.17.107.png]]
> **Fig. 17.107** Arthroscopic distal partial trapeziectomy to remove the distal 3 to 4 mm thickness of the eburnated articular surface of the trapezium. The amount of resection can be verified with fluoroscopy.

![[Fig.17.108.png]]
