> **Fig. 17.108** The thumb metacarpal dorsoradial subluxation and adduction deformity was corrected and the metacarpal pinned to the trapezium at 40 degrees of palmar abduction, 15 degrees of radial abduction, and the thumb pulp facing the finger pulp of the middle finger. A guidewire is then drilled from the thumb metacarpal base obliquely to the proximal diaphysis of the second metacarpal. The exit point at the index metacarpal should aim at the junction between the proximal and middle thirds of its length to minimize the complication of first web narrowing.

![[Fig.17.109.png]]
> **Fig. 17.109** The suspension button is passed into the wound before tying.

![[Fig.17.110.png]]
> **Fig. 17.110** The alignment and the trapeziometacarpal distance of the thumb was well maintained 7 weeks after the surgery.

![[eFig.17.109.png]]
> **eFig. 17.109** X-ray showing the correct position of the suture buttons and the well-reduced CMC joint of the thumb both in alignment and trapeziometacarpal distance.

**Outcomes and complications.** Yao et al. reviewed the results of 16 suture- button suspensionplasty procedures in 14 patients with mean follow-up of 64 months. The cohort included 5 cases of arthroscopic hemitrapeziectomy and 11 full trapeziectomy, with average operative time of 97 minutes. The author permitted mobilization and rehabilitation as early as 10 days postoperatively. The mean improvement in quick- DASH score was 58.2. The average palmar and radial abduction range were 105% and 97% of the contralateral hand, respectively. Pinch and grip strength measured 107% and 102% of the nonsurgical side, respectively. The mean resultant trapezial space height was 71% of the preoperative trapezial height. There were 3 complications: 2 neurapraxia of the dorsal sensory radial nerve and 1 case of button impingement.

In managing Eaton stage IV disease, Cobb et al. have described a technique of arthroscopic resection arthroplasty in 35 patients with good pain relief and improvement in DASH score at 1 year of follow- up.In this technique, the standard 1R and 1U portals are made for thumb CMC joint arthroscopy. STT arthroscopy portals are made by placing the portals approximately 1 cm proximal to the standard 1R and 1U portals. These portals are ideally localized using fluoroscopy with 22- gauge needles. The operation involves resection of 2 to 3 mm of bone from the distal aspect of the trapezium and the proximal aspect of the thumb metacarpal at the thumb CMC joint, removal of 2 to 3 mm of bone from the distal scaphoid and from the proximal aspect of the trapezium and trapezoid at the STT joint, and thermal shrinkage to tighten the capsule and create articular denervation. Joint interposition with graft jacket (Wright Medical Technology, Inc., Memphis, TN) is optional.

### Small Joint Arthroscopy

For discussion on small joint arthroscopy, including additional figures, visit ExpertConsult.com.

#### Metacarpophalangeal Joint Arthroscopy

The progression and development of small and microarthroscopes have continued to push the development of small joint arthroscopy. Arthroscopy of the MCP joint, proximal interphalangeal (PIP) joint, and even the distal interphalangeal (DIP) joints has been described. These arthroscopic techniques include synovectomy, chondroplasty, capsulorrhaphy, joint resurfacing, and all arthroscopic- assisted fixation of articular fractures. Chen first described arthroscopy of the MCP joint, PIP joint, and DIP joint using Watanabe number 25 arthroscope in 1979. He established two portals on each side of the extensor tendon on each joint. Vaupel and Andrews first described using arthroscopic treatment in MCP joint on a professional golfer with a 1- year history of chronic synovitis within the thumb MCP joint. In this report, the golfer was able to return to his sport within a 6- month period. Ryu and Fagan presented a small series on treating Stener lesions involving the ulnar collateral ligament of the thumb MCP joint.They described arthroscopic reduction of the lesions in eight thumbs with a follow- up period of more than 3 years. Once reduction of the Stener lesion was performed, it was pinned to stabilize the joint to allow healing.

**Indications for MCP joint arthroscopy.** The indications for MCP joint arthroscopy were well described and summarized by Choi et al.

**Inflammatory arthritis.** Synovial biopsy and synovectomy can be performed without the need of an arthrotomy (eFig. 17.110). In severe cases, the arthroscopic views are frequently obscured by the dense synovial overgrowth inside the joint (Video 17.47). The detailed architecture of the joint may not be apparent at the beginning. It is mandatory to start shaving of synovium with small shaver even with limited vision in order to reveal more articular structure. Usually the view will be much improved after some debridement of synovium. The status of the articular cartilage should be documented on both sides of the joint. The morphology of the synovial overgrowth may also help differentiate the underlying pathology. Arthroscopy is particularly valuable in managing mono- or oligoarthritis, both for diagnostic and therapeutic effect.

![[eFig.17.110.png]]
> **eFig. 17.110** Arthroscopic synovectomy for a patient with psoriatic arthropathy of multiple finger joints on the left hand. The surgery achieved early pain relief and resolution of swelling 3 months postoperation with excellent cosmetic and functional outcome.

**Acute collateral ligaments injury/gamekeeper’s thumb.** The use of MCP joint arthroscopy for the treatment of gamekeeper’s thumb was first described by Ryu and Fagan. A Stener lesion can be diagnosed, confirmed, and reduced, and the ulnar collateral ligament can be repositioned back to its original position. The thumb is then immobilized with a cast for 4 weeks (eFig. 17.111).

![[eFig.17.111.png]]
> **eFig. 17.111** Arthroscopic reduction of Stener lesion in gamekeeper’s thumb.

**Reduction of metacarpal head fracture.** MCP joint arthroscopy can be used for direct visualization of an articular fracture of the metacarpal head to achieve anatomic reduction of this difficult intraarticular fracture (eFig. 17.112). The fracture can be fixed in a retrograde manner with absorbable pins (eFig. 17.113).

![[eFig.17.112.png]]
> **eFig. 17.112** X-ray showing an articular shear fracture of the fourth metacarpal head with displacement. The reduction and healing were good after arthroscopic-assisted fixation with absorbable pins.

![[eFig.17.113.png]]
> **eFig. 17.113** MCP joint arthroscopy can be used for direct visualization of an articular fracture of the metacarpal head to achieve anatomic reduction. The fracture can be fixed in a retrograde manner with absorbable pins under local anesthesia.

**Complex MCP joint dislocation.** MCP joint arthroscopy helps in reduction of complex MCP joint dislocation. Interposed tissue such as entrapped sesamoid bone can be reduced without opening the joint.

**Posttraumatic MCP joint pain of the thumb due to fibrosis and volar plate adhesion.** Vaupel and Andrews reported a case of MCP joint arthroscopy used to successfully treat a patient with chronic painful synovitis and burring of small chondral defect. Arthroscopy helps to identify the dense synovitis, fibrosis, and osteochondral lesion that are common pathologies encountered in posttraumatic MCP joint pain and stiffness. Another important cause is volar plate adhesion leading to incongruent and painful flexion. The joint space was narrowed on introduction of arthroscope. Synovectomy alone partially opened up the joint. It was fully released only after the volar plate adhesion was broken down by insertion of a freer dissector under direct vision. The volar recess is thus reopened. Normal congruent flexion can be restored after surgery.

**MCP joint chronic volar plate instability.** Thumb MCP joint chronic volar plate instability occurs after a hyperextension injury to the thumb or may develop in the absence of injury. It also occurs in patients with generalized ligamentous laxity, collagen vascular disease, paralytic disorders, or thumb basal joint arthritis. The patient usually presents with pain and reduction of pinch and grip power. Pain is induced by passive hyperextension of the thumb MCP joint. There is increased dorsal- volar translation but not radioulnar instability upon ballottement of joint associated with pain. Affected patients will exhibit a characteristic “abduction- collapse sign” (Video 17.48). The patient is requested to maximally abduct the thumb and index finger and resist the pushing of the maximally abducted thumb and index finger of the opposite hand. Pain will be induced at the affected thumb MCP joint and the thumb will typically give way and collapse.

At arthroscopy under traction, the volar plate can be seen often together with part of the sesamoid bone. Synovectomy is performed with 2.0 mm shaver and mini- RF probe until the volar plate is fully exposed. Outflow of fluid is maintained via an 18- gauge needle inserted into the MCP joint through the extensor tendon dorsocentrally. Thermal shrinkage of the volar plate is then performed using the RF probe. To reduce the complication of thermal damage to the FPL tendon, the A1 pulley is released on the volar side through small open incision, and the FPL tendon is retracted and protected using a plastic suction tube away from the volar plate before thermal shrinkage is begun. Regarding traction, instead of using a typical finger trap, the thumb can be suspended at the traction tower using a thick plastic suction tube placed across the condyle of the proximal phalanx of the thumb. In this way, the interphalangeal joint of the thumb can be free to move and lessen the intimate relationship of the FPL tendon and the volar plate (eFig. 17.114). RF energy is applied to the volar plate on a point- to- point basis systematically from radial to ulnar side, with each application lasting for 2 to 3 seconds. Traction is reduced to minimal during the shrinkage process. Postoperatively the thumb is immobilized with cast for 4 weeks, followed by further nocturnal brace immobilization for 2 more months.

![[eFig.17.114.png]]
> **eFig. 17.114** Measures to reduce the complication of thermal damage to the flexor pollicis longus (FPL) tendon. A, A1 pulley is released on the volar side through small open incision, and the FPL tendon is retracted and protected using a plastic suction tube away from the volar plate before thermal shrinkage commences. B, Instead of using a typical finger trap, the thumb is suspended by the traction tower using a thick plastic suction tube placed around the condyles of the proximal phalanx of the thumb. In this way, the interphalangeal joint of the thumb can be free to move and lessen the intimate relationship of the FPL tendon and the volar plate.

Wong and Ho reported the outcome on 8 patients with chronic thumb MCP joint volar plate instability treated with this technique.The mean follow- up period was 41.4 months (range, 2 of 134). One case had recurrence of instability requiring open volar plate capsulodesis. All other cases had their thumb hyperextensibility resolved and maintained throughout the entire follow-up period (Video 17.49).

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: MCP JOINT ARTHROSCOPY

The procedure can be performed under portal- site local anesthesia using 1.9 mm arthroscope. Other instruments include a 2.0 mm shaver, a mini- RF probe, and mini- arthroscopic instruments. The patient is placed in a supine position with the shoulder abducted and the arm supported on an arm- board. No tourniquet is required. A finger trap is applied to the affected finger (eFig. 17.115). Traction is applied at 5 to 10 lbs of tension with a traction tower. A two- portal technique is used, a dorsoradial portal and a dorsoulnar portal, each on either side of the central extensor tendon (eFig. 17.116). Once on traction, the vacuum sign becomes apparent and the joint space can easily be determined by thumb palpation. The intended port sites are marked with a marking pen. Local anesthesia with 1 to 1.5 mL of 2% lidocaine mixed with 1:200,000 adrenaline is injected to the portal sites with a 25- gauge needle from the skin down to the joint capsule. Articular injection is optional and the needle can be used to confirm the joint space. Two millimeters of normal saline is injected via either port site for distention of the joint space. A longitudinal or oblique 3 mm incision along the skin crease is made with a No. 15T blade. The subcutaneous tissue is dissected, and the extensor expansion is perforated by blunt dissection with a hemostat. The joint space is confirmed once a gush of saline fluid is expressed. Next, a cannula with blunt- end trocar is introduced into the left- sided portal (i.e., dorsoulnar for a left hand, dorsoradial for a right hand). Gentle force should be used to avoid gouging of the articular cartilage of the metacarpal head, particularly in a tight joint. The trocar is then removed. Continuous saline infusion using 3 L normal saline bag under gravity is connected to the cannula. Saline solution is allowed to fill up the joint and the cannula before the arthroscope is inserted to avoid trapping air bubbles inside the joint. The right- sided portal (i.e., dorsoradial for a left hand, dorsoulnar for a right hand) is used for instrumentation. The two portals are interchangeable. It is recommended to use the finger pivoting technique with the index or middle fingertip to fine control the lens motion as the joint is relatively small and shallow. It is particularly important in dealing with dorsal-sided pathology such as chondral lesion or synovitis to avoid the frequent slippage of the arthroscope out of the joint.

![[eFig.17.115.png]]
> **eFig. 17.115** The setting for arthroscopic surgery of the thumb MCP joint.

![[eFig.17.116.png]]
> **eFig. 17.116** The two-portal technique for MCP joint arthroscope: a dorsoradial portal and a dorsoulnar portal, each on either side of the central extensor tendon.

The MCP joint is a relatively simple joint to examine arthroscopically as there is only one joint space (Video 17.50 ). With the 30- degree arthroscope inside the joint, the dome- shaped cartilaginous surface of the metacarpal head should be apparent on the floor while part of the concave articular surface of the base of proximal phalanx can be seen in the upper part of the view. The volar plate can be seen between the two articular surfaces. However, the volar recess cannot be visualized normally due to its anterior and proximal location relative to the head. One can use a probe to palpate the recess. By rotating the lens in 360- degree manner, a much wider area of both articular surfaces can be reached. The dorsal part of the metacarpal head and the accompanying dorsal synovial recess and capsular reflection can be seen with the lens facing downward. This is an important part of the joint to be assessed as many painful chondral lesions tend to locate in this area, together with the associated synovitis. The synovitis needs to be debrided with a small shaver of 2 mm in order to reveal the true extent of any chondral lesion. Alternatively, the RF apparatus with a miniprobe is equally effective. However, the use at the dorsal side of the joint should be done with extreme caution due to the vicinity to the extensor mechanism. Over the volar plate area, the flexor tendons are at risk of thermal damage and the use of an RF probe should be performed with caution. Toward the radial side, the arthroscope can follow the articular surface of the metacarpal head to sweep into the radial gutter to reach the radial synovial recess where the radial collateral ligament originates. The ligament can be assessed for any substance tear, avulsion, and laxity, and any loose body can be removed. It may be difficult to see the ulnar gutter and ulnar synovial recess from the radial side, and the arthroscope needs to be inserted from the dorsoulnar portal in order to have a more thorough assessment.

After the procedure, the skin wounds are closed with a Steri-Strip and bulky dressing is applied for 2 days and then changed to a light dressing. The postoperative mobilization varies for different patients based on the underlying pathology. Patients with acute collateral ligament tears require immobilization after the operation; otherwise, patients are encouraged to start mobilization exercises permitted by their pain.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopy of the Metacarpophalangeal Joint

Indications
- Inﬂammatory arthritis, osteoarthritis, and posttraumatic arthritis of MCP joint
- Posttraumatic thumb MCP joint pain of uncertain etiology
- Articular fractures
- Volar plate instability
- Gamekeeper’s thumb

Preoperative Evaluation
- X- ray, MRI, and/or CT scan

Pearls
- Arthroscopy is very useful in managing chronic thumb MCP joint pain with out radiologic anomaly. Often chondral lesions, traumatic synovitis, and ligament lesions can be found and treated.
- MCP joint arthroscopy can be performed comfortably under PSLA without tourniquet.
- A 1.9 mm arthroscope and 2.0 mm shaver are recommended as standard equipment.
- Examine both radial and ulnar gutters of collateral ligaments to check for loose bodies.

Technical Points
- For thumb MCP joint arthroscopy, while on traction, supinate the forearm and bring the dorsum of thumb MCP joint to face the surgeon.
- Normally, the sesamoid bones of the thumb cannot be seen arthroscopi cally. Seeing the sesamoid bones may indicate chronic volar plate instabil ity.
- Dorsal aspect of the thumb metacarpal head needs to be assessed by rotat ing the lens to 7 o’clock and 4 o’clock positions, as often chondral lesion and synovitis can be detected for chronic thumb pain cases.
- When performing thermal shrinkage of the volar plate, the FPL tendon needs to be protected and retracted by looping a plastic suction tubing around through a small incision at A1 pulley level.
- When using the RF probe for synovectomy or other thermal ablation, insert an 18- gauge needle through the extensor tendon directly into the joint for outﬂow and bubble release.

Pitfalls
- Excessive thermal energy application to the volar plate may cause ﬂexor tendon damage or delayed rupture.
- Scraping and injury of the articular cartilage
- Finger traps may lacerate the fragile skin in rheumatoid arthritis patients.

Postoperative Care
- Free ﬁnger mobilization for simple synovectomy
- Thumb spica cast for 4 weeks and then splinting for 2 weeks after thermal shrinkage of the volar plate
```

#### Proximal Interphalangeal Joint Arthroscopy

Thomsen et al. described their experience with PIP joint arthroscopy. This paper focused on the anatomic findings and anatomy of eight cadaver PIP joints followed by two clinical examples. They noted the technique was demanding and, at the time, limited by instrumentation. Sekiya et al. reported their results in PIP joint arthroscopy using a 1.5 mm arthroscope for 21 rheumatoid patients. They modified Chen’s approach by making the portal more lateral just on the lateral aspect of the lateral bands, instead of going through the interval between the central slip and the lateral band (eFig. 17.117). Through the arthroscope, joint irrigation and synovectomy could be performed. It was noted that the technique was promising following synovectomy, with early clinical improvement in range of motion and pain control.

![[eFig.17.117.png]]
> **eFig. 17.117** Portals for PIP joint arthroscopy: Entry site of the dorsoradial portal is just radial to the extensor tendon, 5 mm proximal to PIP joint line. The dorsoulnar portal is located ulnar to the extensor tendon, 5 mm proximal to the PIP joint line.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: PIP JOINT ARTHROSCOPY

Similar to the CMC joint and MCP joint arthroscopy, PIP joint arthroscopy can be done as an outpatient procedure under local anesthesia. The patient is in supine position. No tourniquet is needed. Procedure can be done under portal site local anesthesia with injection of 1 mL of 2% lidocaine with 1:200,000 adrenaline at each portal site. Alternatively, the procedure can be done under digital nerve block with or without digital tourniquet. The hand should be placed on the operating table horizontally in a relaxed position with the interphalangeal joints slightly flexed and supported by a towel (eFig. 17.118). No traction is needed. The surgeon should sit at the shoulder side of the patient with the monitor placed at the end of the operating table. Portals used include the dorsoradial and the dorsoulnar portals. Entry site of the dorsoradial portal is radial to the extensor tendon, 5 mm proximal to PIP joint line. Similarly, the dorsoulnar portal is located ulnar to the extensor tendon, 5 mm proximal to the PIP joint line. A small transverse or oblique incision along skin crease is made, followed by blunt dissection with a hemostat through the subcutaneous tissues to raise the extensor tendon from the periosteum. The joint capsule is entered by blunt technique with a hemostat, followed by a blunt- end trocar- cannula (eFig. 17.119). A 1.9 mm arthroscope with 30- degree angle is used. The arthroscope is inserted on the left-sided portal (i.e., dorsal- ulnar portal for a left hand, dorsal- radial portal for a right hand) and the instrumental portal is on the right- sided portal (i.e., dorsal- radial portal for a left hand, dorsal- ulnar portal for a right hand). The arthroscopic portal and instrumental portal are interchangeable for complete visualization of the joint (Video 17.51). The arthroscope should be aimed at a proximal to distal direction into the dorsal recess of the joint. Insertion should be gentle to avoid any damage to the articular cartilage of the joint. Extreme care needs to be adopted when working around the central slip of the extensor tendon. Continuous saline infusion with a 3- L saline bag is connected to the cannula of the arthroscope. Outflow can be established with an 18- gauge needle inserted through the central slip into the joint (eFig. 17.120). It is essential when using RF apparatus to avoid thermal injury. Common procedures performed included synovectomy, removal of intraarticular loose bodies, and excision of unstable cartilage flaps or osteophytes (eFig. 17.121; Video 17.52). Accessibility of the joint from the arthroscopic approach is limited to the dorsal, radial, and ulnar recess (Video 17.53). The volar recess and the volar half of the joint cannot be reached arthroscopically. Nevertheless, since synovitis is mostly located in the dorsal aspect of the joint and around the collateral ligaments, arthroscopic synovectomy is still feasible in the majority of the cases associated with inflammatory arthritis.

![[eFig.17.118.png]]
> **eFig. 17.118** Settings for PIP joint arthroscopy. The patient’s hand should be placed on the operating table horizontally in a relaxed position with the interphalangeal joints slightly flexed and supported by a piece of towel. No traction is needed. The surgeon should sit at the shoulder side of the patient with the monitor placed at the end of the operating table.

![[eFig.17.119.png]]
> **eFig. 17.119** Surgical steps in creating portals for PIP joint arthroscopy: A small transverse or oblique incision along skin crease is made, followed by blunt dissection with a hemostat at the subcutaneous plane to raise the extensor tendon from the periosteum. The joint capsule is entered by blunt technique with a hemostat, followed by a blunted-end trocar cannula. A 1.9 mm arthroscope with 30-degree angle is used.

![[eFig.17.120.png]]
> **eFig. 17.120** Outflow can be established with an 18-gauge needle inserted through the central slip into the joint. It is essential when using RF apparatus to avoid thermal injury.

![[eFig.17.121.png]]
> **eFig. 17.121** Loose bodies can be removed from inside the PIP joint using a minigrasper under direct arthroscopic vision.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Arthroscopy of the Proximal Interphalangeal Joint

Indications
- Inﬂammatory arthritis and osteoarthritis PIP joint

Preoperative Evaluation
- Radiographic and clinical assessment

Pearls
- Traction is not necessary as only dorsal aspect of the joint can be accessed.
- Avoid use of RF probe on dorsal side to prevent injury to extensor tendon.

Technical Points
- Inserting the arthroscope more proximally away from PIP joint skin crease can create bigger joint cavity to manipulate.
- Use 2 mm shaver to remove dorsal osteophyte and synovitis.
- Important to examine both gutters of collateral ligaments.
- Passive full extension of PIP joint enables better exposure of base of middle phalanx and to expand the joint space.
- When using RF probe for synovectomy, insert an 18- gauge needle through the extensor tendon directly into the joint for saline outﬂow and bubble release.

Pitfalls
- Excessive thermal energy causes cartilage and extensor tendon injury.

Postoperative Care
- Check extensor function after surgery.
```

## FUTURE POTENTIAL

Forty years ago, people argued whether meniscal surgery of the knee could be done arthroscopically. Today the term “open meniscetomy” will probably only appear in the dictionary. A minimally invasive arthroscopic procedure is also the ideal and preferred mode of treatment if the result can be proven to be equal or superior to an open technique. With just over three decades of development and standardization, wrist arthroscopy has a tremendous potential for future development. The literature over the past 25 years demonstrates that attempts have been made to perform nearly all conventional open surgical procedures of the wrist arthroscopically. While diagnostic arthroscopy had been firmly established, most of the innovative therapeutic trials, consisting of few clinical subjects, lacked controlled randomized prospective study design and were devoid of longterm evaluation. Probably only the test of time and carefully planned clinical study can clear the skepticism and consolidate the therapeutic role of arthroscopy in various wrist disorders. With the development of PSLA technique to allow wrist arthroscopy to be performed under local anesthetic setting, the risks associated with arthroscopy have diminished and the acceptance of the surgery both by the patient and the surgeon has increased. One can foresee a much wider clinical application in the future, as well as the possibility of wrist arthroscopy as an office diagnostic procedure. Future development will probably be limited only by the imagination and skill of the surgeons. Nevertheless, more controlled randomized prospective studies need to be conducted to establish the true value of each arthroscopic treatment modality in the hand and wrist.

For Case Studies, Videos, and more, please visit eBooks for Practicing Clinicians atExpertConsult.com.
